A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\left\{0\right\})
, and the trace first-moment bounds

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\Tr(\xi)\,m(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)<\infty,\qquad\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\Tr(\xi)\,\frac{\langle y,\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\rangle}{\|\xi\|^{2}}\leq C_{\mu}\Tr(y),

hold for some
C_{\mu}<\infty
and all
y\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
, then for
every fixed
t\geq 0
,

X_{t}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s.

ii)

Let
(X^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}

be the sequence of finite-rank operator-valued affine processes in
Proposition
2.9
. Then
the sequence
(X^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
X
on
D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})

equipped with the Skorohod topology, i.e. it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[f(X^{d})\right]\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[f(X)\right],\quad\text{as
}d\to\infty,

for all bounded continuous
f\colon D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})\to\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
2.10
. Then for every
initial datum
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
and every fixed
t>0
, the limiting affine
process satisfies

X_{t}\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. If moreover
x\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
, then

X_{t}\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. for every fixed
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

Fix
t>0
and choose
\delta\in(0,t]
. By
Lemma
4.14
,

\sup_{d}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}[\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}]<\infty
. The embedding

\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is continuous and compact; hence

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
is lower semicontinuous as an extended function on

\mathcal{H}
. By Theorem
2.10
(ii) and
Lemma
4.17
, the continuous-mapping theorem gives

X_{t}^{d}\Rightarrow X_{t}
for every deterministic
t\geq 0
. Portmanteau’s theorem yields

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}[\|X_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}]\leq\liminf_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}[\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}]<\infty.

Thus
X_{t}\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. If
x\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
, the same
estimate is available with
\delta=0
, because

\sup_{d}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}x\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\|x\|_{\mathcal{V}}
, giving the assertion at
t=0
as
well.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

The affine processes
(X,(\mathbb{P}_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}})
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
from
Theorem
2.10
are
irregular
, in the sense that the derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi(t,u)\lvert_{t=0}
, cf.
[
25
, Definition 2.2]
, is not continuous at

u=0
, due to the unboundedness of
L
on
\mathcal{H}
. For general regularity
and path properties of affine processes on (finite-dimensional) state spaces,
including the role of such regularity assumptions, we refer to
[
26
]
. The coercivity assumption is the
ingredient that allows the Lyapunov estimates underlying the

\mathcal{V}
-smoothing of Proposition
2.11
above. If in part (a) of

2.2 iv)
the condition
B\in\mathcal{L}(H)
holds, then the
admissible parameter set is also admissible in the regular sense of
[
22
, Definition 2.3]

and existence would follow from
[
22
]
, but Assumption
Assumption

A

would not be satisfied in general.

3.
Finite-Rank Approximations of Heat-Modulated Affine SV
Models

In this section, we examine the joint stochastic covariance model

(f_{t},X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, where
(f_{t})_{t\geq 0}
represents the
mild solution to the HJMM equation (
2.1
) modulated by a stochastic
volatility process
(\sigma_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfying

X_{t}=\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{*}
in the
H
-valued formulation. The covariance process

(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is an irregular affine process on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, associated with the
admissible parameter set
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
, whose existence is guaranteed by
Theorem
2.10
. The main result of this section is that the
joint model
(f_{t},X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
admits an affine transform formula once the

H
-valued stochastic convolution is well defined (the accompanying
Feynman–Kac martingale identity for the affine covariance process is provided
by Proposition
3.4
), see
Theorem
3.5
below, and that the associated affine
transform is stable under finite-rank approximations of the covariance process
via a spectral Galerkin approximation of the generalized mild Riccati equations
(see Theorem
3.10
below). We conclude this section
with an example of heat-modulated affine stochastic volatility models, as well
as an application of the theory to the pricing of options written on

power flow forwards
.

Throughout this section it is useful to keep in mind that the
results below are organized as three independent layers, each of which adds an assumption to the previous one. Layer (i) is the
driftless
H
-valued
affine transform
of Theorem
3.5
: it gives the joint
Fourier–Laplace transform of
(f_{t},X_{t})
at imaginary
u_{1}\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H
, under
Assumption
Assumption

B

(stochastic integrability of the
square-root volatility); the accompanying limiting Feynman–Kac martingale
identity for the affine covariance process is
not
an extra hypothesis but
is established in Proposition
3.4
. Layer (ii)
is the
HJM no-arbitrage drift correction
for the geometric
specification: this is not part of the driftless transform theorem and has to
be imposed separately when forward prices are required to be martingales (see
the discussion before Theorem
3.5
). Layer (iii) is the

analytic-continuation/Carr–Madan pricing layer
used in
Proposition
3.15
: this is invoked through the
complex-transform result of
[
44
, Theorem 2.3]
and is
subject to its own admissibility hypotheses. The reader interested only in
finite-rank approximation rates can stop at layer (i) plus
Theorem
3.10
; the reader interested in
geometric-payoff option pricing needs layers (ii) and (iii) in addition.

3.1.
The Heat-Modulated Affine Stochastic Covariance
Model

As before, we consider the Hilbert space triple

V\hookrightarrow H\hookrightarrow V^{*}
compact, with
V
the state space of regular
forward curves. This triple of forward curve spaces induces, on the
symmetric covariance subspace
\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, the triple of
Hilbert–Schmidt operators

\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H})^{*}
. Throughout
this section we work consistently with
\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}
on the covariance side; the
symbol
\mathcal{V}
alone retains its meaning from Section
2.2

(full intersection
\mathcal{L}_{2}(V^{*},H)\cap\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)
, no symmetry built in).
Let
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
be an admissible parameter set as in
Definition
2.2
satisfying
Assumption
Assumption

C

, and denote by

(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the associated irregular affine process on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
given by
Theorem
2.10
. Moreover, denote the filtered
probability space of
X
by

(\Omega^{(1)},\mathcal{F}^{(1)},(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(1)})_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P}^{(1)})

and fix an initial value
\mathbb{P}^{(1)}(X_{0}=x)=1
for
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. We let

(\Omega^{(2)},\mathcal{F}^{(2)},(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(2)})_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P}^{(2)})

be another filtered probability space satisfying the usual conditions and that
carries a cylindrical Brownian motion
W\colon[0,\infty)\times H\rightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{(2)},\mathcal{F}^{(2)},\mathbb{P}^{(2)})
, in the following denoted by
(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. We assume that

\mathbb{P}^{(2)}(f_{0}=F(0,\cdot))=1
for some initial forward curve

F(0,\cdot)\in H
and define the following joint filtered probability space:

\displaystyle(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})\coloneqq(\Omega^{(1)}\times\Omega^{(2)},(\mathcal{F}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(2)}),(\mathcal{F}^{(1)}_{t}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(2)})_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P}^{(1)}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{(2)}).

We denote the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}
by
\mathbb{E}
and from now on
consider
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and the cylindrical Brownian motion
(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
to be defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
.

Throughout this subsection we work with the driftless specification

g_{t}\equiv 0
in (
2.1
). The covariance process remains the affine
process from Theorem
2.10
; the additional assumption
below only concerns the well-posedness of the
H
-valued stochastic
convolution.

For the
H
-valued model we fix, once and for all, a strongly
continuous semigroup
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
H
with generator
\mathcal{A}
. On the
infinite-horizon space this is the usual Musiela shift on the maturity
component. On the finite-horizon space

H=H_{0}\oplus\mathbb{R}
,
H_{0}=L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\max},w(x)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)
, used below, we take the
level-preserving stopped shift

S(t)(u,r)=(u_{t},r),\qquad u_{t}(x)=u(x+t)\mathbf{1}_{\{x+t<\Theta_{\max}\}},\qquad(u,r)\in H_{0}\oplus\mathbb{R}.

Equivalently, the full curve value is shifted on the available maturity window
and set equal to the level component once the shifted maturity leaves the
window. In financial terms, a forward maturity that drifts past the
horizon
\Theta_{\max}
is set equal to the finite-horizon level component
r
;
on the finite interval this level is the chosen scalar anchor of the
decomposition, not a long-end limit. This convention is well defined on

L^{2}
-equivalence classes and avoids using the endpoint value

u(\Theta_{\max})
, which is not defined in the pivot space. Its Hilbert-space adjoint semigroup is denoted by
(S^{*}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
Both
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(S^{*}(t))_{t\geq 0}
are strongly continuous
contractions on
H=L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\max},w(x)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R}
(the multiplier

\mathbf{1}_{\{x+t<\Theta_{\max}\}}
is bounded by
1
in absolute value, so

\|S(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(H)}\leq 1
and
\|S^{*}(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(H)}\leq 1
by duality). On the
regular space
V=V_{0,\beta}\oplus\mathbb{R}
the adjoint semigroup additionally
preserves the boundary condition
u(0)=0
on the shape component (the
truncated shift extends the source curve by the level value, which does not
change the value at
0
of the shifted curve), and satisfies the growth bound

\|S^{*}(s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(V)}\leq M_{1}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\omega s}
used in
Theorem
3.10
(ii); both properties are established in
Lemma
3.1
below, in fact with
M_{1}=1
and
\omega=0
.

Lemma 3.1

(Regularity of the adjoint stopped shift on the regular space)
.

Assume
0<\Theta_{\max}<\infty
,
H=H_{0}\oplus\mathbb{R}
with

H_{0}=L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\max},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)
,
V=V_{0,\beta}\oplus\mathbb{R}
and let the

H
-adjoint of the level-preserving stopped shift
S(t)(u,r)=(u_{t},r)
,

u_{t}(x)=u(x+t)\mathbf{1}_{\{x+t<\Theta_{\max}\}}
be given by

S^{*}(t)(v,\rho)=\bigl(\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}\,v(\cdot-t)\,\mathbf{1}_{\{\,\cdot\,>t\}}\,,\ \rho\,\bigr),\qquad(v,\rho)\in H_{0}\oplus\mathbb{R}.

Then
(S^{*}(t))_{t\geq 0}
leaves
V
invariant, i.e.
S^{*}(t)(V)\subseteq V
for all
t\geq 0
, and is a contraction on
V
:

\|S^{*}(t)(v,\rho)\|_{V}^{2}\leq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}\|v\|_{V_{0,\beta}}^{2}+|\rho|^{2}\leq\|(v,\rho)\|_{V}^{2},\qquad t\geq 0.

In particular the growth bound
\|S^{*}(s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(V)}\leq M_{1}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\omega s}
used in
Theorem
3.10
(ii) holds with
M_{1}=1
and
\omega=0
,
and
(S^{*}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a strongly continuous contraction semigroup on
V
.

Proof.

For
u,v\in H_{0}
the substitution
y=x+t
gives

\langle S(t)u,v\rangle_{H_{0}}=\int_{0}^{\Theta_{\max}}u(x+t)\mathbf{1}_{\{x+t<\Theta_{\max}\}}\,v(x)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x=\int_{t}^{\Theta_{\max}}u(y)\,v(y-t)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta(y-t)}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y,

so
(S^{*}(t)v)(y)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}v(y-t)\mathbf{1}_{\{y>t\}}
; the level component is
unchanged because
S(t)
acts as the identity on
\mathbb{R}
. Let
v\in V_{0,\beta}

and
w=S^{*}(t)v
. Then
w\equiv 0
on
(0,t)
, while on
(t,\Theta_{\max})
one has

w=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}v(\cdot-t)\in H^{1}
; at the front
y=t
the right limit is

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}v(0)=0
by the anchoring
v(0)=0
, so
w
is continuous across
t

and therefore
w\in H^{1}(0,\Theta_{\max},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)
with
w(0)=0
, i.e.
w\in V_{0,\beta}
. This is precisely where the short-end condition built into

V_{0,\beta}
is used: it makes the zero-extension of the shifted curve across
the front an
H^{1}
function. Differentiating,

w^{\prime}(y)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}v^{\prime}(y-t)\mathbf{1}_{\{y>t\}}
a.e., and with
z=y-t
,

\int_{0}^{\Theta_{\max}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta y}|w^{\prime}(y)|^{2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-2\beta t}\int_{t}^{\Theta_{\max}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta y}|v^{\prime}(y-t)|^{2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}\int_{0}^{\Theta_{\max}-t}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta z}|v^{\prime}(z)|^{2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z.

The identical computation with
v^{\prime}
replaced by
v
gives

\int\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta y}|w|^{2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}\int_{0}^{\Theta_{\max}-t}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta z}|v|^{2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z
.
Hence, for either the weighted
H^{1}
norm or its seminorm on
V_{0,\beta}
,

\|w\|_{V_{0,\beta}}^{2}\leq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}\|v\|_{V_{0,\beta}}^{2}
, so the contraction
is independent of the choice of equivalent norm. Adding the isometric level
component yields the displayed bound. Strong continuity on
V
follows from
strong continuity of translation on
H^{1}(0,\Theta_{\max},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)
and

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\beta t}\to 1
, together with the uniform bound
\|S^{*}(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(V)}\leq 1

and density of smooth functions vanishing near the endpoints.
∎

3.1.1.
Joint Affine Transform for the Driftless Model

We define a new set of generalized mild Riccati equations associated to the joint
process
(f_{t},X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. For this, define
\hat{\mathcal{R}}\colon\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}\to\mathcal{H}
as

(3.1)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}(h,u)

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{*}(u)-\tfrac{1}{2}h\otimes h-\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\big(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\xi,u\rangle}-1+\langle\chi(\xi),u\rangle\big)\frac{\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)}{\|\xi\|^{2}},

where for two elements
x
and
y
in
H
we define the operator

x\otimes y\in\mathcal{L}(H)
by
x\otimes y(h)=\langle x,h\rangle_{H}y
for every

h\in H
and write
x^{\otimes 2}\coloneqq x\otimes x
.
For complex arguments
v=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}h\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H
with
h\in H
we extend the
notation by complex bilinearity and identify the resulting object with the
real symmetric Hilbert–Schmidt operator

v^{\otimes 2}\coloneqq(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}h)\otimes(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}h)\equiv-h^{\otimes 2}\in\mathcal{H},

i.e. a real element of
\mathcal{H}
with the opposite sign of
h^{\otimes 2}
. Under
this convention
-\tfrac{1}{2}v^{\otimes 2}=\tfrac{1}{2}h^{\otimes 2}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
,
which is the sign needed for the forcing term
-\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{1}

in (
3.1
) to be order-preserving on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
when

\psi_{1}\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H
.

Let
T>0
,
u_{1}\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H
and
u_{2}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. We define the new system of

generalized joint mild Riccati equations
as follows:

(3.2a)

\displaystyle\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Phi(t,u)

\displaystyle=F(\psi_{2}(t,u)),

\displaystyle\quad\Phi(0,u)=0,

(3.2b)

\displaystyle\,\psi_{1}(t,u)

\displaystyle=u_{1}+\mathcal{A}^{*}\!\left(\int_{0}^{t}\psi_{1}(s,u)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right),

\displaystyle\quad\psi_{1}(0,u)=u_{1},

(3.2c)

\displaystyle\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi_{2}(t,u)

\displaystyle=L(\psi_{2}(t,u))+\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\psi_{1}(t,u),\psi_{2}(t,u)),

\displaystyle\quad\psi_{2}(0,u)=u_{2}.

The differential displays
(
3.2a
) and (
3.2c
) are formal:

L
is unbounded on
\mathcal{H}
, so the right-hand side
of (
3.2c
) is not defined for arbitrary

\psi_{2}(t,u)\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The actual solution concept is the

variation-of-constants
formulation

\psi_{1}(t,u)=S^{*}(t)u_{1},\qquad\psi_{2}(t,u)=\mathcal{T}(t)u_{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\psi_{1}(s,u),\psi_{2}(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

together with
\Phi(t,u)=\int_{0}^{t}F(\psi_{2}(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s
. We use the term
“mild solution” below to refer to a triple

(\Phi(\cdot,u),\psi_{1}(\cdot,u),\psi_{2}(\cdot,u))
that satisfies these
variation-of-constants identities; the differential displays are retained
only for notational convenience.

For every initial value
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
, set

\Psi(\cdot,u)\coloneqq(\psi_{1}(\cdot,u),\psi_{2}(\cdot,u)).

We say that
(\Phi(\cdot,u),\Psi(\cdot,u))
is a
mild solution
from

[0,T]
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}

to (
3.2a
)-(
3.2c
) if

\Phi(\cdot,u)\in C([0,T];\mathbb{R})
,
\psi_{1}(\cdot,u)\in C([0,T];\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H)
,

\psi_{2}(\cdot,u)\in C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{+})
and the map

(\Phi(\cdot,u),\Psi(\cdot,u))
satisfies the
variation-of-constants identities just displayed.
(
C^{1}
regularity for
\Phi
is recovered only once continuity of

s\mapsto F(\psi_{2}(s,u))
is established.)

Here
\mathcal{A}^{*}\colon\dom(\mathcal{A}^{*})\subseteq H\to H
denotes the

H
-adjoint of the generator
\mathcal{A}
of the
H
-level shift semigroup fixed
above; its Hilbert-space adjoint semigroup is
(S^{*}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
Before invoking the joint Riccati equations in the statement of
Theorem
3.5
below, we first establish their
well-posedness.

Lemma 3.2

(Well-posedness of the joint mild
Riccati equations)
.

Let
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
be an admissible parameter set in the
sense of Definition
2.2
and let
T>0
. For every
initial datum
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
there exists a unique
mild solution

\bigl(\Phi(\cdot,u),\psi_{1}(\cdot,u),\psi_{2}(\cdot,u)\bigr)\in C^{1}([0,T];\mathbb{R})\times C([0,T];\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H)\times C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{+})

of (
3.2a
)-(
3.2c
), which is
given by

(3.3)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(t,u)

\displaystyle=S^{*}(t)u_{1},

(3.4)

\displaystyle\psi_{2}(t,u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(t)u_{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\,\hat{\mathcal{R}}\bigl(\psi_{1}(s,u),\psi_{2}(s,u)\bigr)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\Phi(t,u)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}F\bigl(\psi_{2}(s,u)\bigr)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Proof.

Equation (
3.2b
) is the linear
integral form

\psi_{1}(t,u)=u_{1}+\mathcal{A}^{*}\!\bigl(\int_{0}^{t}\psi_{1}(s,u)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\bigr)
,
i.e. the mild form of
\partial_{t}\psi_{1}=\mathcal{A}^{*}\psi_{1}
with

\psi_{1}(0,u)=u_{1}
. By the Hille–Yosida theorem (see
e.g.
[
64
, Chapter 1]
),
\mathcal{A}^{*}
generates the strongly continuous
semigroup
(S^{*}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
H
, so

\psi_{1}(t,u)=S^{*}(t)u_{1}
is the unique continuous mild solution on

[0,T]
; the map
t\mapsto S^{*}(t)u_{1}
takes values in
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H
whenever

u_{1}\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H
because
S^{*}(t)
preserves the real subspace
H
.

Given
\psi_{1}(\cdot,u)\in C([0,T];\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H)
from the first step, define
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\psi_{1}}(t,u)\coloneqq\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\psi_{1}(t,u),u)=\hat{R}(u)-\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{1}(t,u)^{\otimes 2}
, where

\hat{R}
is defined in (
2.13
). By
[
22
, Remark 3.4]
the
map
u\mapsto\hat{R}(u)
is locally Lipschitz on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and

t\mapsto\psi_{1}(t,u)^{\otimes 2}
is continuous from
[0,T]
to
\mathcal{H}
;
consequently
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\psi_{1}}
is locally Lipschitz in
u
uniformly on

[0,T]
and continuous in
t
. Arguing exactly as in the proof
of Proposition
2.8

(quasi-monotonicity of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\psi_{1}}
with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is
inherited from
\hat{R}
because the additive forcing is independent of the

\psi_{2}
variable and therefore cancels in differences. Separately, for

v=\psi_{1}(t,u)\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H
, writing
v=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}h
with
h\in H
gives

v^{\otimes 2}=-h^{\otimes 2}
and hence

-\tfrac{1}{2}v^{\otimes 2}=\tfrac{1}{2}h^{\otimes 2}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, which is the
subtangential cone-invariance condition; see Lemma
4.4
), the
time-dependent version of the local existence theorem of
[
53
, Chapter
8, Theorem 2.1 & Remark 2.1]
yields a unique continuous mild solution

\psi_{2}(\cdot,u)\in C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{+})
satisfying
(
3.4
), which is globally defined on
[0,T]
by the same
moment bound used in the proof of
Proposition
2.8
.

With
\psi_{2}(\cdot,u)
in hand, the scalar component
\Phi(\cdot,u)
is
obtained by direct integration:

\Phi(t,u)=\int_{0}^{t}F(\psi_{2}(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Since
F
is continuous on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\psi_{2}(\cdot,u)
is continuous,
we have
\Phi(\cdot,u)\in C^{1}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
. Uniqueness of the triple
follows from the uniqueness established at each of the three steps.
∎

Assumption
\mathfrak{B}
.

The square-root volatility process
\sigma_{t}\coloneqq X_{t}^{1/2}
is progressively
measurable with respect to
\mathbb{F}
and satisfies the
H
-valued analogue of
Assumption
Assumption

A

, namely

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\|S(t-s)\sigma_{s}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,H)}^{2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right]<\infty,\qquad t\geq 0.

In this case we define the associated
H
-valued heat-modulated stochastic
covariance model by

(3.7)

\displaystyle f_{t}=S(t)f_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}S(t-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}W_{s},\qquad t\geq 0.

Remark 3.3

(Trace-class sufficient condition for Assumption
Assumption

B

)
.

If the shift semigroup
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
is bounded on
H
on compact time
intervals and

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[\Tr(X_{s})]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s<\infty,\qquad t\geq 0,

then Assumption
Assumption

B

holds for the square-root
volatility
\sigma_{s}=X_{s}^{1/2}
. Indeed,

\|S(t-s)X_{s}^{1/2}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,H)}^{2}\leq\|S(t-s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(H)}^{2}\,\Tr(X_{s}).

Thus the trace-class refinement of Theorem
2.10
, together
with the displayed integrated trace moment, gives a direct sufficient condition
for the
H
-valued stochastic convolution.

Proposition 3.4

(Affine Feynman–Kac martingale)
.

Let
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
be an admissible parameter set and let
X
be the
associated affine process on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
from
Theorem
2.10
; in particular assume
Assumption
Assumption

C

. For every
t>0
and every

u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
, with
(\Phi,\psi_{1},\psi_{2})
the mild
Riccati solution from Lemma
3.2
, the process

M_{s}=\exp\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s}\langle X_{r},\psi_{1}(t-r,u)^{\otimes 2}\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r\right)\exp\!\left(-\Phi(t-s,u)-\langle X_{s},\psi_{2}(t-s,u)\rangle\right),\qquad 0\leq s\leq t,

is a true martingale under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
for every
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

This is the infinite-dimensional Feynman–Kac martingale identity underlying the
affine transform formula. It is
proved below for every admissible parameter set satisfying
Assumption
Assumption

C

, and no trace-class
hypothesis is required for this. The proof is the joint analogue of
Proposition
4.16
: the identity is verified for the
finite-rank Galerkin processes, where the generator is bounded and the classical
affine Feynman–Kac calculation applies, and then passed to the limit. It is
given in Section
4.3
, in Lemma
4.23
and
the proof of Proposition
3.4
immediately thereafter. The
restriction
u_{1}\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H
is essential: it makes the Feynman–Kac potential

\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\cdot,\psi_{1}(t-r,u)^{\otimes 2}\rangle
non-positive on the cone,
which is what gives the uniform bound used to pass from a local to a true
martingale; for real
u_{1}
the sign is lost and the unconditional statement is
not claimed.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the affine process associated with
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
as in Theorem
2.10
; in particular
assume Assumption
Assumption

C

. Assume also
Assumption
Assumption

B

, and let

(f_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the
driftless

H
-valued stochastic covariance
model (
3.7
) (i.e. no HJM drift correction is included; the
forward curve evolves purely under the shift semigroup and the stochastic
convolution of
\sigma_{s}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}W_{s}
). Furthermore, let

u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}

and let
(\Phi(\cdot,u),(\psi_{1}(\cdot,u),\psi_{2}(\cdot,u)))
be the
unique mild solution from Lemma
3.2
of the
generalized joint mild Riccati equations
(
3.2a
)–(
3.2c
). Then, for all

t\geq 0
, the following holds:

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\langle f_{t},u_{1}\rangle_{H}-\langle X_{t},u_{2}\rangle}\right]=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\Phi(t,u)+\langle f_{0},\psi_{1}(t,u)\rangle_{H}-\langle x,\psi_{2}(t,u)\rangle}.

Remark 3.6

(Absence of leverage)
.

The covariance process
X
and the cylindrical noise
W
are constructed on the
two independent factors
\Omega^{(1)}
and
\Omega^{(2)}
of the product
space; this independence is used in the conditional-Gaussian step
(
4.34
) of the proof. Consequently the model has

no leverage
: the forward curve and its instantaneous covariance are
uncorrelated. This is in contrast to the leverage extension of the
Hilbert-space Barndorff–Nielsen–Shephard model in
[
17
]
. Introducing a
genuine price–volatility correlation (e.g. a common driving noise as
in
[
17
,
23
]
) would break the conditional Gaussianity and require a
different argument; we do not pursue it here. For the same reason we describe
the model as capturing maturity-specific risk rather than as reproducing
leverage-driven volatility clustering.

Remark 3.7
.

Theorem
3.5
is formulated at the natural

H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
level under the explicit stochastic-integrability condition
from Assumption
Assumption

B

. If, in addition,
f_{0}\in V

and
X_{t}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{V}
for all
t\geq 0
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. (see
Remark
3.14
(iv) for a sufficient condition), then
the same model also satisfies Assumption
Assumption

A

on

V\times\mathcal{V}
.

Example 3.8

(The heat-modulated BNS stochastic volatility model)
.

As an example of a heat-modulated affine stochastic volatility model, we introduce
the
heat–modulated Barndorff–Nielsen and Shephard (BNS) stochastic
volatility model
. This is a heat-modulated version of the Hilbert
space-valued BNS model presented in
[
14
,
17
]
. In the heat-modulated BNS stochastic volatility model we assume that the forward
curve process
(f_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is given by the driftless
H
-valued
specification (
3.7
) on the state space

H=L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\mathrm{max}},\mathrm{e}^{\beta x}\mathrm{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R},

for some
\Theta_{\mathrm{max}}>0
and
\beta>0
.
Here we deliberately specialize the general forward-curve setting from
Section
2.1.2
to the stronger weighted
L^{2}
pivot space

L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\mathrm{max}},\mathrm{e}^{\beta x}\mathrm{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R}
.
This choice is tailored to the diagonal BNS benchmark and the pricing
functional used below; the general existence theory above remains formulated on
the original pair

V=H_{\beta}(0,\Theta_{\max})\hookrightarrow L^{2}((0,\Theta_{\max}),\mathrm{e}^{\gamma x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R}\hookrightarrow V^{*}

with
0<\gamma<\beta
(i.e. the pivot space here is the weighted
L^{2}
space,
not the Filipovic-type Sobolev space
H_{\gamma}
).
The dynamics are modulated by an
instantaneous volatility process
(\sigma_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, defined as the operator
square root of an Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
,
satisfying

(3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X_{t}=L_{\Delta}(X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}L_{t},\\
X_{0}=x\in\mathcal{H}^{+},\end{cases}

where
(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a
\mathcal{H}^{+}
-valued Lévy subordinator and

L_{\Delta}
denotes the Lyapunov operator associated with the Laplacian, cf.
Example
2.5
.
We denote by
b_{\mathrm{phys}}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
the physical drift of the
subordinator and by
m
its Lévy measure on
\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}
. Since the
affine admissibility convention in Definition
2.2

uses the compensated truncation
\chi(\xi)=\xi\mathbf{1}_{\{\|\xi\|\leq 1\}}
, the
canonical affine drift parameter is

b_{\mathrm{adm}}\coloneqq b_{\mathrm{phys}}+I_{m},\qquad I_{m}\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\chi(\xi)\,m(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

Thus the Laplace exponent of
L
can be written equivalently as

\displaystyle\varphi_{L}(u)

\displaystyle=\langle b_{\mathrm{phys}},u\rangle+\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\big(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\langle\xi,u\rangle}\big)\,m(\mathrm{d}\xi)

(3.10)

\displaystyle=\langle b_{\mathrm{adm}},u\rangle-\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\big(\mathrm{e}^{-\langle\xi,u\rangle}-1+\langle\chi(\xi),u\rangle\big)\,m(\mathrm{d}\xi),\qquad u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}.

The first line is the physical subordinator exponent; the second is the
canonical affine coefficient
F
associated with the admissible drift

b_{\mathrm{adm}}
, see
[
29
]
.
The semigroup
(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
generated by
L_{\Delta}
satisfies

\mathcal{T}(t)u=T(t)uT^{*}(t),

where
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}=(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{tB})_{t\geq 0}
is the full semigroup on
H
generated by
B=\Delta\oplus 0
(cf. Example
2.5
), and we note that
\mathcal{T}(t)
maps
\mathcal{H}^{+}
into itself for all
t\geq 0
.
Moreover, the process
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a stochastically continuous affine Markov
process with values in
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, associated with the admissible parameter set

(b_{\mathrm{adm}},L_{\Delta},m,0)
. This is the parameter set used by the
affine theory; the physical drift
b_{\mathrm{phys}}
is recovered from

b_{\mathrm{adm}}-I_{m}
. This follows from the explicit Ornstein–Uhlenbeck
subordinator construction, equivalently from
Theorem
2.10
specialized to
\Gamma=0
and
\mu=0
when
Assumption
Assumption

C

is satisfied; it is
not a consequence of the joint transform formula, which is applied only after
the covariance process has been constructed. In this OU-subordinator
case the Feynman–Kac martingale identity of Proposition
3.4

can also be seen directly from the explicit mild representation

X_{t}=\mathcal{T}(t)x+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}L_{s}
and the independent-increment structure
of
L
.
In particular, the characteristic function of the forward curve process

(f_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\langle f_{t},u_{1}\rangle_{H}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\langle f_{0},S^{*}(t)u_{1}\rangle_{H}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{L}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s}\mathcal{T}(s-\tau)(S^{*}(\tau)u_{1})^{\otimes 2}\mathrm{d}\tau\right)\mathrm{d}s\right)

(3.11)

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big\langle x,\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-\tau)(S^{*}(\tau)u_{1})^{\otimes 2}\mathrm{d}\tau\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right),

where the arguments of
\varphi_{L}
belong to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Remark 3.9
.

Regarding Assumption
Assumption

A

for the heat-modulated BNS model:
since Theorem
3.5
is stated at the
H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}

level, the forward curve process
(f_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is modeled in
H
rather than

V
. The relevant condition is therefore Assumption
Assumption

B

,
and the general trace-class sufficient condition is recorded in
Remark
3.3
. In the present BNS specialization it
amounts to

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\int_{0}^{t}\|S(t-s)X_{s}^{1/2}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,H)}^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right]<\infty.

A sufficient trace-class criterion is
X_{s}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
for almost every
s
and

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[\Tr(X_{s})]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s<\infty,

because then

\|S(t-s)X_{s}^{1/2}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,H)}^{2}\leq\|S(t-s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(H)}^{2}\,\Tr(X_{s}).

Under the strengthened trace-class hypotheses of
Theorem
2.10
, together with the corresponding first-moment
integrability of
\Tr(X_{s})
, the characteristic-function
formula (
3.11
) is therefore rigorous without any
additional square-root regularity hypothesis.

The formula (
3.11
) follows from
Theorem
3.5
by specializing to
\mu=0
(no
state-dependent jumps),
\Gamma=0
,
u_{2}=0
(evaluating the
X
-component
marginal out). A small notational point: Theorem
3.5

and Lemma
3.2
treat
u_{1}\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H
, so that

\psi_{2}(t,u_{1})=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)(S^{*}(s)u_{1})^{\otimes 2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s
with a
negative sign. The display in (
3.11
) uses instead a

real
argument
u_{1}\in H
paired against the explicit imaginary unit on
the left-hand side, i.e. it is the application of the lemma at the imaginary
argument
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}u_{1}
. Using the sign identity
(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}h)^{\otimes 2}=-h^{\otimes 2}

from the convention introduced above, the corresponding loadings simplify to

\psi_{1}(t,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}u_{1})=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}S^{*}(t)u_{1}
and

\psi_{2}(t,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}u_{1})=+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)(S^{*}(s)u_{1})^{\otimes 2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

(
positive
sign for
u_{1}\in H
), which is exactly the expression
appearing in (
3.11
). Note that for the arguments
of
\varphi_{L}
to lie in
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, one needs

\psi_{2}(t,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}u_{1})\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, which follows from positivity of

(S^{*}(s)u_{1})^{\otimes 2}
and the positivity-preserving property of

(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
established in Lemma
4.1
.

The following theorem is our main result on the approximation of affine function-valued stochastic
covariance models by affine finite-rank models. Moreover, it provides an explicit convergence rate,
which depends on the speed of convergence of the Galerkin projection.

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
(f,X)
denote the stochastic covariance model from
Theorem
3.5
with
(f_{0},X_{0})=(f_{0},x)\in H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
. For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
let
X^{d}
denote
the affine process on
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}
as in
Proposition
2.9
and define
(f^{d},X^{d})
as the
stochastic covariance model obtained from (
3.7
) by replacing

X
with the finite-rank instantaneous covariance process
X^{d}
. Then the
following holds:

i)

For every
f_{0}\in H
,

x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
the stochastic covariance model
(f^{d},X^{d})
with

(f^{d}_{0},X_{0}^{d})=(f_{0},\mathbf{P}_{d}(x))
is well-defined and for every
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
t\geq 0
the
process satisfies

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\langle f^{d}_{t},u_{1}\rangle_{H}-\langle X^{d}_{t},u_{2}\rangle}\right]=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\Phi_{d}(t,u)+\langle f_{0},\psi_{1}(t,u)\rangle_{H}-\langle\mathbf{P}_{d}(x),\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\rangle},

for
(\Phi_{d}(\cdot,u),(\psi_{1}(\cdot,u),\psi_{2,d}(\cdot,u)))
the
unique Galerkin joint Riccati solution with

\psi_{2,d}(0,u)=\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{2}
,
\psi_{1}(t,u)=S^{*}(t)u_{1}
, and
with
F
and
L(\cdot)+\hat{\mathcal{R}}
in
(
3.2a
)–(
3.2c
) replaced by

F\circ\mathbf{P}_{d}
and

\mathbf{P}_{d}\circ L\circ\mathbf{P}_{d}+\mathbf{P}_{d}\circ\hat{\mathcal{R}}\circ\mathbf{P}_{d}
,
respectively. Since
X_{t}^{d}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
, the exponent only depends on the
projected covariance loading:

\langle X_{t}^{d},u_{2}\rangle=\langle X_{t}^{d},\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{2}\rangle
.

ii)

When specialized to the canonical
finite-horizon forward-curve setting of Example
2.5
,
namely

\Theta_{\max}<\infty,\qquad V=V_{0,\beta}(0,\Theta_{\max})\oplus\mathbb{R},\qquad H=L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\max},\mathrm{e}^{\beta x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R},

and
B=\Delta\oplus 0
with the boundary conditions stated there. Moreover, suppose the limiting

H
-valued stochastic covariance
model
(f,X)
from Theorem
3.5
is well defined
under Assumptions
Assumption

C

and

Assumption

B

. Fix
T>0
and assume moreover that

S^{*}(s)V\subseteq V,\qquad\|S^{*}(s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(V)}\leq M_{1}\operatorname{e}^{\omega s},\qquad 0\leq s\leq T,

and that rank-one tensors satisfy

\|h^{\otimes 2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq C_{\otimes}\|h\|_{V}^{2}
for
h\in V
.
If, moreover, the vector measure
\mu
takes values in
\mathcal{V}
and has
finite
\mathcal{V}
-total variation

|\mu|_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})<\infty
, and

\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H})\subseteq\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}
, then for every

\tilde{u}\in V
such that
\|\tilde{u}\|_{V}\leq 1
there exists a constant

\tilde{C}_{T}
such that for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(3.13)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}\langle f_{t},\tilde{u}\rangle_{H}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}\langle f^{d}_{t},\tilde{u}\rangle_{H}}\right]\right|\leq\tilde{C}_{T}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}(1+\|x\|).

Remark 3.11

(Explicit convergence rate)
.

This remark is a direct corollary of the specialized item (ii) of
Theorem
3.10
: it makes the projection-error rate

\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}
explicit in the canonical setting of
Example
2.5
, and is therefore not a separate
theorem but the substitution of the Sturm–Liouville eigenvalue asymptotics
into the rate already established in item (ii).
Concretely, in the heat-modulated setting with covariance generator

B=\Delta\oplus 0
on
V=V_{0,\beta}\oplus\mathbb{R}
, where
\Delta
is the
weighted Sturm–Liouville operator of Example
2.5
, the
projection error bound in (
3.13
)
becomes explicit. Let

\lambda_{d+1}^{\mathrm{tens}}\coloneqq\inf\{\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j}:\max(i,j)>d\}

be the first omitted Lyapunov eigenvalue in the tensor basis

\{\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\}_{i\leq j}
. The standard estimate

\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\lambda_{d+1}^{\mathrm{tens}}}}

follows from the variational characterisation of eigenvalues and the Hilbert-scale
norm induced by the Lyapunov generator. In the present finite-horizon Laplacian
setting the positive shape eigenvalues satisfy

\eta_{n}\sim(n\pi/\Theta_{\max})^{2}
by
Example
2.5
. With the one-based convention

e_{1}=(0,1)
and
e_{n+1}=(q_{n},0)
, the first omitted Lyapunov eigenvalue

\lambda_{d+1}^{\mathrm{tens}}
is the first omitted combined spatial
eigenvalue
\lambda_{d+1}
(equivalently the first omitted shape eigenvalue
under the chosen rank convention). Substituting into (
3.13
)
gives the explicit rate

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathbb{E}\big[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}\langle f_{t},\tilde{u}\rangle_{H}}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}\langle f_{t}^{d},\tilde{u}\rangle_{H}}\big]\right|\leq\frac{\tilde{C}_{T}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{d+1}^{\mathrm{tens}}}}(1+\|x\|),

which is the theoretical underpinning of the
1/\sqrt{\lambda_{d+1}^{\mathrm{tens}}}

reference lines used in Figure
2
.

Remark 3.12

(The structural hypotheses hold in the BNS specialization)
.

The four structural hypotheses of Theorem
3.10
(ii)
are met in the diagonal heat-modulated BNS setting used in the numerics, so the
rate (
3.13
) genuinely applies there.
Indeed: (i)
S^{*}(s)V\subseteq V
with the growth bound
\|S^{*}(s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(V)}\leq M_{1}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\omega s}
holds by Lemma
3.1
, in fact with

M_{1}=1
and
\omega=0
;
(ii) the rank-one bound

\|h^{\otimes 2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq C_{\otimes}\|h\|_{V}^{2}
follows from the continuous
embedding
V\hookrightarrow H
and the standard Hilbert–Schmidt identity

\|h^{\otimes 2}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)}=\|h\|_{H}\|h\|_{V}
; (iii)
\mu=0
gives

|\mu|_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})=0<\infty
; and (iv)
\Gamma=0
gives

\Gamma^{*}(\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H})=\{0\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}
trivially.

Remark 3.13

(Assumption
Assumption

C

in the diagonal BNS benchmark)
.

The diagonal heat-modulated BNS parameters used in the numerical section also
satisfy Assumption
Assumption

C

and the

H
-valued stochastic-integrability condition of
Assumption
Assumption

B

. Indeed, in that benchmark the physical
subordinator drift is
b_{\mathrm{phys}}=0
, while
\Gamma=0
and
\mu=0
.
Under the fixed truncation convention, the admissibility drift is

b_{\mathrm{adm}}=I_{m}
. Since the reported jump amplitude satisfies

\|d\|_{\mathcal{H}}=0.5(\sum_{k\geq 1}k^{-4})^{1/2}<1
, we have
\chi(d)=d
and hence

b_{\mathrm{adm}}=\beta_{L}d
. Therefore

b_{\mathrm{adm}}-\int\chi(\xi)m(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)=b_{\mathrm{phys}}=0\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, so the
drift admissibility condition is satisfied. The estimate below gives

d\in\mathcal{V}
, hence
b_{\mathrm{adm}}\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}
and the drift-regularity part
of Assumption
Assumption

C

holds; the state-dependent
jump conditions are trivial because
\mu=0
.
The compound-Poisson subordinator has finite intensity
\beta_{L}
and a single
diagonal jump amplitude
d=(d_{k})_{k\geq 1}
with
d_{k}=0.5/k^{2}
. Since the
finite-horizon Laplacian eigenvalues satisfy
\lambda_{k}\simeq k^{2}
,

\|d\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{k\geq 1}(1+\lambda_{k})d_{k}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{k\geq 1}(1+k^{2})k^{-4}<\infty.

Thus
\int\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}m(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)=\beta_{L}\|d\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}<\infty
. Moreover,

\Tr(d)=\sum_{k}d_{k}<\infty
and the initial covariance used in the figures has

x_{0,k}=0.5d_{k}
, hence
\Tr(x_{0})<\infty
. The explicit OU-subordinator
representation gives

\mathbb{E}\left[\Tr(X_{t})\right]\leq\Tr(x_{0})+\beta_{L}t\,\Tr(d),\qquad t\geq 0,

because the Lyapunov semigroup is trace-dissipative on positive operators.
Since the stopped-shift semigroup
S
is a contraction on
H
, this implies

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\|S(T-s)X_{s}^{1/2}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,H)}^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right]\leq\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\Tr(X_{s})\right]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s<\infty,

which is Assumption
Assumption

B

.

Remark 3.14
.

We make the following remarks on approximating the forward curve
process
(f_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by finite-dimensional models, and the modeling in
V
versus
H
:

i)

Note that since the embedding

H_{\beta}\subset\!\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\gamma x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R}
is compact if
0<\gamma<\beta
, we can approximate the forward curve dynamics

(f_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by a finite-dimensional process
(U_{d}(f_{t}))_{t\geq 0}

in the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\gamma x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R}
,
where
(U_{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
is defined as the finite-rank operator

\displaystyle U_{d}v=\sum_{k=1}^{d}s_{k}\langle v,h_{k}^{(d)}\rangle_{V}e_{k}

for some
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
and

(h_{k}^{(d)})_{k\leq d}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\mathcal{A}^{*})
(see

[
62
]
). However, the resulting approximations
(f_{t}^{d})_{t\geq 0}
are generally
not

affine processes, since finite-dimensional projections of affine processes do not
preserve the affine property in general. As a consequence, although

(U_{d}(f_{t}))_{t\geq 0}
is finite-dimensional, it may fail to be tractable in the
sense usually associated with affine models.
More specifically, the principal source of tractability of affine dynamics, the reduction of Fourier-Laplace transforms and characteristic functions to
generalized Riccati equations, is typically lost after projection. In particular,
one cannot in general expect exponential–affine representations of quantities
of the form

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\bigl(\langle u,U_{d}f_{t}\rangle\bigr)\right],

nor closed or semi-closed pricing formulas obtained via Fourier methods, even if
such formulas are available for the infinite-dimensional model.
This loss of structure has several practical implications:
(a) pricing and hedging of European-style claims must typically rely on simulation
or numerical PDE/integro-PDE methods in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
;
(b) calibration becomes substantially more costly, since each evaluation of an
objective function requires repeated Monte Carlo simulations or numerical PDE
solves; and
(c) preserving the no-arbitrage HJM drift condition under the reduced dynamics
requires additional care, as the projected dynamics need not inherit the same
structural drift restrictions as the original model.
Instead, using
Theorem
3.10
, we propose approximating the
forward curve dynamics by finite-rank affine stochastic covariance models

(f^{d},X^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
, which, for each
d\in\mathbb{N}
, are
infinite-dimensional, yet affine and exhibit only finite-rank noise.

ii)

If there exists an orthonormal basis (ONB)

(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of eigenvectors for the drift operator

\mathcal{A}
in (
2.1
), we could also approximate the process

f^{d}
by
H_{d}
-valued processes
(\tilde{f}^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
by
following the same ideas as in this paper, namely approximating
the unbounded operator
\mathcal{A}
by
\mathcal{A}^{d}\coloneqq\mathsf{P}_{d}\mathcal{A}\mathsf{P}_{d}
and
X
, as presented here, by
X^{d}
. In this
case, an analog of Theorem
3.10
holds, with

(f^{d},X^{d})
replaced by
(\tilde{f}^{d},X^{d})
. However, in applications, we
typically have
\mathcal{A}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
, which does not
admit an ONB of eigenvectors in the space
H_{\beta}
. One could, motivated
by
[
21
]
, consider the HJM model with

\mathcal{A}_{1}\coloneqq\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\Delta
. This
operator possesses a sequence of eigenvectors that forms an ONB for spaces
like
L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\mathrm{max}},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\beta x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)
, allowing for
finite-dimensional and finite-rank approximations. However, such a model is not arbitrage-free
in general. We leave these considerations for future
work.

iii)

If one wants to model the forward curve dynamics in the regular
space
V
, then the volatility, not only the covariance, must satisfy the
regularity required by Assumption
Assumption

A

. In the
square-root specification this means imposing

X_{t}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)
, and, if one wants the full covariance-regularity
convention used for
\mathcal{V}
,
X_{t}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{V}
. This is an additional
assumption. The fixed-time regularity
X_{t}\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
from
Theorem
2.10
does
not
imply

X_{t}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{V}
.

The distinction is visible already in the diagonal Hilbert-scale
case. Suppose that
H
has an orthonormal basis
(e_{n})
and that

\|h\|_{V}^{2}\simeq\sum_{n}\rho_{n}^{2}|\langle h,e_{n}\rangle_{H}|^{2},\qquad\rho_{n}\to\infty.

If
X_{t}e_{n}=\hat{x}_{n}(t)e_{n}
with
\hat{x}_{n}(t)\geq 0
, then

X_{t}\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)
requires

\sum_{n}\hat{x}_{n}(t)^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}<\infty,

whereas
X_{t}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)
requires the strictly stronger condition

\sum_{n}\hat{x}_{n}(t)\rho_{n}^{2}<\infty.

Thus trace-class covariance regularity and
\mathcal{V}
-regularity of
X_{t}
do not
by themselves control the square-root volatility in the regular curve space.

iv)

A clean sufficient condition for using the square-root
specification in the regular space
V
is to impose the square-root
condition directly:

X_{t}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\!\left[\int_{0}^{T}\|S(T-s)X_{s}^{1/2}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)}^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right]<\infty

for every
T>0
. In the diagonal Hilbert-scale regime above, a sufficient
pointwise condition is

\sum_{n}\hat{x}_{n}(t)\rho_{n}^{2}<\infty
. This condition may follow from a
stronger model specification forcing
X_{t}
to take values in

\mathcal{L}_{2}(V^{*},V)
, but it is not a consequence of the abstract admissible
parameter set. Alternatively, one may formulate the forward equation in the
pivot space
H
and use Assumption
Assumption

B

; this is the
route taken in Theorem
3.5
.

3.2.
Example: Robustness of Pricing Flow Forwards in Energy Markets

We study Fourier prices for exponential functionals of the forward
curve, motivated by European Call options written on
geometric power flow
forwards
in electricity and gas markets, cf.
[
11
]
.
The stochastic covariance model used here is the driftless

H
-valued affine benchmark from Theorem
3.5
. Thus the
formulas below are mathematically prices of exponential curve functionals under
that benchmark. If they are interpreted as no-arbitrage geometric forward-option
prices, this interpretation requires the additional geometric HJM pricing layer
and complex-transform assumptions stated in Proposition
3.15
;
it is not an automatic consequence of the driftless transform theorem alone.
Fix
t<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\Theta_{\max}
. We model the log-forward curve in Musiela
parametrisation as an
H
-valued process
(f_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, where

V=V_{0,\beta}(0,\Theta_{\max})\oplus\mathbb{R},\qquad H=L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\max},e^{\beta x}\,dx)\oplus\mathbb{R}.

This is the same finite-horizon weighted pair used in
Theorem
3.10
(ii) and in the numerical
implementation. On a bounded interval the exponential weight is equivalent to
the unweighted
L^{2}
norm, and the compactness needed for the Galerkin
approximation follows from the Sobolev embedding discussed in
Section
2.1.1
.
The instantaneous forward price is, formally, given by

F(t,T)=\exp(f_{t}(T-t))
. Point evaluation
f\mapsto f(T-t)
is well-defined
on the regular space
V=H_{\beta}
(where it is a bounded linear functional)
but
not
on the larger pivot space
H=L^{2}
. We therefore use this
pointwise expression only as motivation for the averaged functional below;
all subsequent statements are formulated in terms of
\ell_{t,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}
,
which has a bounded
H
-Riesz representer and is therefore well-defined on the

H
-valued forward curve
f_{t}
.
We define the
geometric flow forward
over
[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]
by

(3.14)

F^{\mathrm{geo}}(t;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\exp\Big(\frac{1}{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}}\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\log F(t,T)\,dT\Big)=\exp\big(\ell_{t,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}(f_{t})\big),

where the averaging functional
\ell_{t,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}:V\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(3.15)

\ell_{t,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}(f):=\frac{1}{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}}\int_{\tau_{1}-t}^{\tau_{2}-t}f(z)\,dz,\qquad f\in V.

Note that
\ell_{t,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}
is a bounded linear
functional on
V
, hence
\ell_{t,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}\in V^{*}
.
Moreover, on the finite-horizon pivot space

H=L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\max},e^{\beta x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R}
, the same averaging operation
admits an explicit Riesz representer in
H
: writing a curve as
(u,r)
with
actual value
u(x)+r
and setting
a_{\tau}\coloneqq\tau_{1}-t
,

b_{\tau}\coloneqq\tau_{2}-t
, the linear
functional

(u,r)\mapsto\frac{1}{b_{\tau}-a_{\tau}}\int_{a_{\tau}}^{b_{\tau}}\bigl(u(z)+r\bigr)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z

is represented in the standard product inner product as

\ell_{H}=\Bigl(\frac{e^{-\beta z}}{b_{\tau}-a_{\tau}}\mathbf{1}_{[a_{\tau},b_{\tau}]}(z),\;1\Bigr)\in H,

so that the averaged functional is
\langle f,\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}
for
f\in H
. Throughout the
proposition below we use this
H
-representer whenever the transform
machinery is applied to the
H
-valued process
f_{t}
; the notation

\ell\in V^{*}
is reserved for the regular-curve interpretation.

A European Call payoff with strike
K>0
and exercise time
t<\tau_{1}
written
on this exponential functional is

\Pi_{t}:=\big(F^{\mathrm{geo}}(t;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})-K\big)^{+}.

Proposition 3.15

(Robustness bound for forward-flow option prices)
.

Let
(f,X)
be the driftless affine stochastic covariance model on

L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\max},\mathrm{e}^{\beta x}\mathrm{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R}
and
let
(f^{d},X^{d})
be its finite-rank approximation as in
Theorem
3.10
, under
Assumptions
Assumption

C

and

Assumption

B

. Fix

t<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\Theta_{\max}
and
K>0
, and set

\ell_{H}\in H
equal to the Riesz representer of the averaging
functional displayed above, and let
\ell
denote its restriction to
V
.
Define

\pi_{0}:=\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathrm{e}^{\langle f_{t},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}-K)^{+}\big],\qquad\pi_{0}^{d}:=\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathrm{e}^{\langle f_{t}^{d},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}-K)^{+}\big].

Fix
\nu>1
, set
s(\lambda):=\nu+\mathrm{i}\lambda
, and define

g(\lambda):=\frac{K^{-(\nu-1+\mathrm{i}\lambda)}}{(\nu+\mathrm{i}\lambda)(\nu-1+\mathrm{i}\lambda)},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

Assume moreover that the Carr–Madan-type pricing assumptions of

[
44
, Proposition 3.2 and Theorem 2.3]
apply to the
functional represented by
\ell_{H}
. Finally, assume that on the pricing strip
\operatorname{Re}(z)=\nu
there exist a
measurable function
M_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
and functions

r_{d}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
such that
|g|M_{t}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(\lambda)|r_{d}(\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\to 0
, and

\Big|\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{s(\lambda)\langle f_{t},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}]-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{s(\lambda)\langle f_{t}^{d},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}]\Big|\leq M_{t}(\lambda)\,\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}(1+\|X_{0}\|)+r_{d}(\lambda)

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then:

(i)

With the above
g
and
s
,

\pi_{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(\lambda)\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathrm{e}^{s(\lambda)\langle f_{t},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda,

and, by the assumed complex affine transform formula,

\pi_{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(\lambda)\,\exp\Big(-\tilde{\Phi}(t,s(\lambda)\ell_{H})+\langle f_{0},\tilde{\psi}_{1}(t,s(\lambda)\ell_{H})\rangle_{H}-\langle x,\tilde{\psi}_{2}(t,s(\lambda)\ell_{H})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda,

where
(\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{\psi}_{1},\tilde{\psi}_{2})
denotes the complex-valued mild solution
of the associated generalized Riccati equations.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C(t)>0
such that for every
d\in\mathbb{N}
,

|\pi_{0}-\pi_{0}^{d}|\leq C(t)\,\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}\,(1+\|X_{0}\|)+\varepsilon_{d},

where
\varepsilon_{d}\to 0
as
d\to\infty
.

Proof.

(i).

By
[
44
, Proposition 3.2]
applied with the functional
u_{\vartheta}

replaced by the averaging functional represented by
\ell_{H}
, the call price

\pi_{0}=\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{e}^{\langle f_{t},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}-K)^{+}]
admits the Carr-Madan-type representation

\pi_{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(\lambda)\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathrm{e}^{s(\lambda)\langle f_{t},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}\right]\mathrm{d}\lambda.

The expression for
\pi_{0}
in terms of

(\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{\psi}_{1},\tilde{\psi}_{2})
follows from the assumed complex
affine transform formula with
u_{1}=s(\lambda)\ell_{H}
.

(ii).

Use the representation from part (i). With
s(\lambda)=\nu+\mathrm{i}\lambda
,

\pi_{0}-\pi_{0}^{d}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(\lambda)\Big(\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{s(\lambda)\langle f_{t},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}]-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{s(\lambda)\langle f_{t}^{d},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}]\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda.

Hence,

|\pi_{0}-\pi_{0}^{d}|\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(\lambda)|\,\Big|\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{s(\lambda)\langle f_{t},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}]-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{s(\lambda)\langle f_{t}^{d},\ell_{H}\rangle_{H}}]\Big|\,\mathrm{d}\lambda.

Using the dominated stability estimate assumed in the proposition, we obtain

|\pi_{0}-\pi_{0}^{d}|\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(\lambda)|M_{t}(\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\Big)\,\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}\,(1+\|X_{0}\|)+\varepsilon_{d},

where

\varepsilon_{d}:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(\lambda)|r_{d}(\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\to 0.

This proves the claim with

C(t)=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(\lambda)|M_{t}(\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda
.
∎

The numerical illustrations below use
\Theta_{\max}=50
, reference rank
d_{\mathrm{ref}}=12
in the convergence
benchmark, jump amplitudes
d_{k}=0.5/k^{2}
, compound-Poisson jump
intensity
\beta_{L}=1.5
of the subordinator
L
(so that, in the eigenbasis,
L

has physical Laplace exponent

\varphi_{L}(u)=\langle b_{\mathrm{phys}},u\rangle+\beta_{L}(1-\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle d,u\rangle})
with
b_{\mathrm{phys}}=0
in the
experiments; the admissibility drift is

b_{\mathrm{adm}}=\beta_{L}d
under the fixed truncation convention), initial covariance eigenvalues

x_{0,k}=0.5\,d_{k}
, initial forward curve

f_{0}(\theta)=0.03+0.01(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\theta})/\theta
, and Fourier settings

n_{\mathrm{trun}}=40
and
\Delta_{\lambda}=0.05
. In all numerical
experiments, the physical Lévy drift is set to
b_{\mathrm{phys}}=0
(i.e. the uncompensated subordinator has no deterministic drift beyond its jumps; the
canonical admissibility drift is supplied by the compensation term, and the
Lyapunov operator itself is always present). For consistency with the
plotting code, the figure captions below use
\alpha
for the
short-end-anchored weight exponent of the pivot space

L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\max},\mathrm{e}^{\beta x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)
introduced in
Example
2.5
; the two symbols refer to the same
parameter, and
\alpha=\beta=0.1
throughout the reported figures. The choice
d_{\mathrm{ref}}=12
is
adequate for the reported parameter set as a finite-rank benchmark: the
discrete Galerkin eigenvalues satisfy

\lambda_{12}^{\mathrm{tens}}\approx 60.16
, so the upper-bound rate

1/\sqrt{\lambda_{12}^{\mathrm{tens}}}\approx 0.13
is already small on the scale of the
displayed convergence plot. The last one or two rank errors should be read as
diagnostic finite-rank differences, since they are at or below the numerical
Fourier-truncation sensitivity reported below.
We stress the scope of these experiments: they confirm

qualitative
convergence within the finite-rank hierarchy, and the fitted
log-log slope
\approx 2.83>1
shows that the theoretical

1/\sqrt{\lambda_{d+1}^{\mathrm{tens}}}
bound is conservative here rather than
sharp. They are run in the BNS specialization (
\mu=0
,
\Gamma=0
); a numerical
study of the genuinely state-dependent case (
\mu\neq 0
), for which the affine
transform is now rigorous through Proposition
3.4
, is left for
future work.

We illustrate Proposition
3.15
numerically using the
diagonal heat-modulated BNS specialization introduced above.
Figure
2
shows the rank-
d
prices
\pi_{0}^{d}
converging to
the rank-
12

finite-rank benchmark

\pi_{0}^{\mathrm{ref}}
(we emphasize

\pi_{0}
is not available
analytically; the figure does not claim to reproduce
\pi_{0}
, only that the
rank-
d
prices converge
within
the finite-rank hierarchy. The absolute
error
|\pi_{0}-\pi_{0}^{d}|
is bounded by

|\pi_{0}-\pi_{0}^{12}|+|\pi_{0}^{12}-\pi_{0}^{d}|
; the first term is theoretically
controlled under Proposition
3.15
but is not observed
numerically because
\pi_{0}
is unavailable.
The second term is the finite-rank benchmark error displayed in the figure.)
Panel (b) compares the absolute error against the upper-bound rate

\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}\lesssim 1/\sqrt{\lambda_{d+1}^{\mathrm{tens}}}

from part (ii), using the discrete Galerkin eigenvalues

\lambda_{d+1}^{\mathrm{tens}}
and a
proportionality constant calibrated from the computed
errors; the log-log regression slope of error against rate is reported in the
panel title (a slope of
1.00
would indicate the error tracks the bound
exactly; a slope greater than
1.00
indicates faster-than-bound convergence,

Panel (c) displays the convergence constant

|\pi_{0}^{d}-\pi_{0}^{\mathrm{ref}}|\cdot\sqrt{\lambda_{d+1}^{\mathrm{tens}}}
. In the present
benchmark this quantity decreases with
d
, which is consistent with the fitted
slope
2.83>1
in panel (b) and indicates that the theoretical upper bound is
conservative rather than sharp here. The rank-
12
benchmark value is

\pi_{0}^{\mathrm{ref}}=0.05993553
, and the fitted proportionality constant in

C=5.85\times 10^{-4}
.
A quadrature and Fourier sensitivity check shows that varying the
Gauss-Legendre node count in
\{16,32,64\}
changes the benchmark price by less
than
10^{-10}
, while varying the Fourier half-bandwidth in
\{20,40,60\}

changes it by about
4.32\times 10^{-4}
between
20
and
40
and by about

2.75\times 10^{-6}
between
40
and
60
. We therefore use

n_{\mathrm{trun}}=40
throughout the reported figures.

lines across five sample sizes

, confirming

1/\sqrt{N}
Monte Carlo scaling for each tenor individually.

Figure 1.
Illustration of the heat regularization mechanism in the
rank-
12

Panel (a):
discrete Galerkin eigenvalues
\omega_{k}
of the
operator
-B=\Delta\oplus 0
in the weighted Laguerre basis (orange),
together with the Weyl asymptotic
(k\pi/\Theta_{\max})^{2}

(steelblue, dashed). The two sequences agree for low
k
where the
Laguerre basis resolves the oscillatory branch, and diverge at high

k
, reflecting the well-known fact that a finite Laguerre basis
overestimates high-frequency Sturm–Liouville eigenvalues; this
over-estimate is a variational
upper bound
on the true
eigenvalues and is the source of the conservative finite-rank rate
in Figure
2
.

Panel (b):
mean instantaneous covariance kernel diagonal

\mathbb{E}[k_{t}(x,x)]
as a function of time-to-maturity
x
, at five
snapshot times
t\in\{0,0.5,1,2,5\}
. The initial profile is sharply
peaked at the short end; under the Lyapunov decay each mode
k
is
damped at rate
2\omega_{k}
, with the higher modes (small spatial
scales) damped fastest, so the kernel relaxes towards the smoother
stationary profile dictated by the Lévy subordinator. The
finite-horizon level mode
\omega_{1}=0
is the only undamped mode and produces
the linear growth of the integrated variance.

4.
Proofs

In this section, we provide the proofs of our main results. The section is
structured as follows: In Section
4.1
, we present the proof of
Proposition
2.8
, which establishes the existence
and convergence of the spectral Galerkin approximation. In
Section
4.2
, we then prove
Proposition
2.9
, concerning the existence of affine
finite-rank operator-valued processes, and Theorem
2.10
,
which demonstrates the convergence of the finite-rank affine process to the
class of irregular affine processes, with values in the cone of positive
self-adjoint Hilbert–Schmidt operators and, under stronger hypotheses, in the
trace class. In Section
4.3
, we
provide the proofs related to the joint model. Specifically, we prove
Theorem
3.5
, which establishes the affine transform
formula for the
H
-valued heat-modulated stochastic covariance model under the
explicit stochastic-integrability assumption from
Assumption
Assumption

B

, and
Theorem
3.10
, which concerns the finite-rank
approximation of the corresponding affine transforms.

Throughout the proof section we use the regular-case arguments from

[
50
]
as a template whenever the dynamics are
finite-dimensional or governed by bounded projected operators. The genuinely
new ingredients in the present irregular setting are the construction of the
mild Riccati solution for the unbounded Lyapunov operator
L
, the smoothing
estimate that yields compact containment through the space
\mathcal{V}
, and the
trace-class regularization induced by the Lyapunov semigroup associated with
the Laplacian-type generator.

4.1.
Galerkin approximation of the generalized mild Riccati
equations

Let
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
be an admissible parameter set as in
Definition
2.2
. Recall the operator
L
from

Eq.

2.10
and the function
\hat{R}\colon\mathcal{H}^{+}\to\mathcal{H}

from (
2.13
). We denote the right-hand side function of the mild
generalized Riccati equations (
2.14b
) by
R
, i.e. we define
it as

\displaystyle R(u)\coloneqq L(u)+\hat{R}(u),\quad u\in\dom(L).

In the spirit of
[
53
]
, and going back to the classical study of
Riccati equations associated with unbounded operators in
[
63
]
, we
interpret (
2.14b
) as semi-linear differential equation on

\mathcal{H}^{+}
, with unbounded linear operator
L
and non-linear continuous
perturbation
\hat{R}
. Note that
\hat{R}
is even locally Lipschitz continuous,
see
[
22
, Remark 3.4]
. In the following lemma, we show that the
semigroup
(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, generated by
(L,\dom(L))
, is a
semigroup of
positive operators
on
\mathcal{H}
with respect to the cone

\mathcal{H}^{+}
:

Lemma 4.1
.

The operator-semigroup
(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is self-adjoint and
strongly continuous. Moreover,

\mathcal{T}(t)(\mathcal{H}^{+})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{+}
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

By Definition
2.2
(iv)(a),
B
is
self-adjoint and non-positive on
H
with spectral domain
\dom(B)
, and

T(t)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{tB}
is the associated self-adjoint
C_{0}
-contraction semigroup;
in particular
T^{*}(t)=T(t)
for every
t\geq 0
. For
t\geq 0
the
operator
\mathcal{T}(t):\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
satisfies

(4.1)

\mathcal{T}(t)x=T(t)xT^{*}(t),\qquad x\in\mathcal{H},

Since
T(t)\in\mathcal{L}(H)
and
x\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(H)
, we have
\mathcal{T}(t)x\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(H)
and

\|\mathcal{T}(t)x\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\|T(t)xT^{*}(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(H)}^{2}\,\|x\|,

so each
\mathcal{T}(t)
is bounded on
\mathcal{H}
. Moreover, (
4.1
)
immediately yields the semigroup property
\mathcal{T}(t+s)=\mathcal{T}(t)\mathcal{T}(s)
and
\mathcal{T}(0)=I
.
Strong continuity follows from the strong continuity of
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
: for any
x\in\mathcal{H}
and
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}(t)x-x\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\|T(t)xT^{*}(t)-x\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

Choose
\varepsilon>0
. Since finite-rank operators are dense in
\mathcal{H}
, there
exists a finite-rank operator
x^{(N)}\in\mathcal{H}
such that

\|x-x^{(N)}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\varepsilon
. Using that
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a
contraction semigroup,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}(t)x-x\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{T}(t)(x-x^{(N)})\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|\mathcal{T}(t)x^{(N)}-x^{(N)}\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|x^{(N)}-x\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon+\|\mathcal{T}(t)x^{(N)}-x^{(N)}\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

Write the finite-rank operator as

x^{(N)}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}u_{k}\otimes v_{k}
, where

(u\otimes v)h=\langle h,v\rangle_{H}u
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(t)x^{(N)}-x^{(N)}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bigl(T(t)u_{k}\otimes T(t)v_{k}-u_{k}\otimes v_{k}\bigr),

and each summand converges to zero in
\mathcal{H}
because

\displaystyle\|T(t)u_{k}\otimes T(t)v_{k}-u_{k}\otimes v_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|(T(t)u_{k}-u_{k})\otimes T(t)v_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|u_{k}\otimes(T(t)v_{k}-v_{k})\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|T(t)u_{k}-u_{k}\|_{H}\,\|v_{k}\|_{H}+\|u_{k}\|_{H}\,\|T(t)v_{k}-v_{k}\|_{H},

which tends to zero as
t\to 0^{+}
by strong continuity of
T(t)
on

H
. Since the sum is finite,
\|\mathcal{T}(t)x^{(N)}-x^{(N)}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\to 0
as

t\to 0^{+}
. Letting first
t\to 0^{+}
and then
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
proves
strong continuity of
(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathcal{H}
.
Next,
\mathcal{T}(t)
is self-adjoint on
\mathcal{H}
: for
x,y\in\mathcal{H}
, using the
trace cyclicity
\mathrm{Tr}(ABC)=\mathrm{Tr}(BCA)
together with
T^{*}(t)=T(t)

from the first paragraph of this proof,

\langle\mathcal{T}(t)x,y\rangle=\mathrm{Tr}\big((T(t)xT^{*}(t))\,y\big)=\mathrm{Tr}\big(x\,T^{*}(t)\,y\,T(t)\big)=\mathrm{Tr}\big(x\,(T(t)yT^{*}(t))\big)=\langle x,\mathcal{T}(t)y\rangle.

Finally,
\mathcal{T}(t)
is positive with respect to the cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}
: if

x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, then for all
h\in H
, using
T^{*}(t)=T(t)
,

\langle\mathcal{T}(t)x\,h,h\rangle_{H}=\langle T(t)\,x\,T^{*}(t)h,\,h\rangle_{H}=\langle x\,T^{*}(t)h,\,T^{*}(t)h\rangle_{H}=\langle x\,T(t)h,T(t)h\rangle_{H}\geq 0,

since
x
is positive self-adjoint. Hence
\mathcal{T}(t)x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
and therefore

\mathcal{T}(t)(\mathcal{H}^{+})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{+}
for all
t\geq 0
.
It remains to identify the generator. This follows spectrally, avoiding any
formal differentiation of an unbounded product. If

x=\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i,j}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}
, then

\mathcal{T}(t)x=\sum_{i\leq j}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\lambda_{i,j}t}x_{i,j}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}.

Hence the generator of
(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the spectral operator with domain

\left\{x\in\mathcal{H}:\sum_{i\leq j}\lambda_{i,j}^{2}|x_{i,j}|^{2}<\infty\right\},

acting by

x\mapsto-\sum_{i\leq j}\lambda_{i,j}x_{i,j}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}
, which is precisely

(L,\dom(L))
from (
2.10
).
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

The Lyapunov semigroup
(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
regularizes
\mathcal{H}
into

\mathcal{V}
. More precisely, equipping

\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{L}_{2}(V^{*},H)\cap\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)

with the natural Hilbert norm

\|x\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\coloneqq\|x\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)}^{2}+\|x\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(V^{*},H)}^{2},

there exists a constant
C_{\mathcal{V}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}\leq C_{\mathcal{V}}(1+t^{-1/2}),\qquad t>0.

In particular, for every
0<\delta\leq T<\infty
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[\delta,T]}\|\mathcal{T}(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}<\infty,

and the map
t\mapsto\mathcal{T}(t)x
is continuous from
(0,\infty)
into

\mathcal{V}
for every
x\in\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathbf{e}_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
be the eigenbasis from
(
2.11
). By the spectral Hilbert-scale compatibility
in Definition
2.2
(iv)(c), every eigenvector
e_{i}

of
B
belongs to
V
, and the norm equivalence gives

\displaystyle\|e_{i}\|_{V}^{2}\leq C(1+\lambda_{i}).

For

i<j
, the symmetrized rank-one operator

\mathbf{e}_{i,j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}+e_{j}\otimes e_{i})
therefore
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\|e_{i}\|_{V}^{2}+\|e_{j}\|_{V}^{2}\bigr)\leq C_{1}(1+\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j}),

The same spectral weight controls the
V^{*}\to H
realization. Indeed, for

\eta\in V^{*}
and the rank-one convention

(u\otimes v)\eta=\langle u,\eta\rangle_{V,V^{*}}v
,

\|e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(V^{*},H)}\leq\|e_{i}\|_{V}\|e_{j}\|_{H}=\|e_{i}\|_{V}.

Equivalently, using the Hilbert adjoint characterization from
Remark
2.6
, the Hilbert–Schmidt norm of

e_{i}\otimes e_{j}:V^{*}\to H
agrees with the

\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)
norm of its adjoint
e_{j}\otimes e_{i}:H\to V
. Hence

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(V^{*},H)}^{2}\leq C_{2}\bigl(1+\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j}\bigr).

For the diagonal case
i=j
, the operator
\mathbf{e}_{i,i}=e_{i}\otimes e_{i}

is the unsymmetrised rank-one tensor and satisfies the same bounds:

\|\mathbf{e}_{i,i}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)}^{2}=\|e_{i}\|_{V}^{2}\leq C(1+\lambda_{i})\leq C(1+\lambda_{i,i})
(using
\lambda_{i,i}=2\lambda_{i}
),
and, by the same adjoint argument,

\|\mathbf{e}_{i,i}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(V^{*},H)}^{2}\leq\|e_{i}\|_{V}^{2}\leq C(1+\lambda_{i,i})
.
Thus, with
\lambda_{i,j}=\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j}
, there exists a
constant
C_{3}>0
such that

(4.2)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\leq C_{3}(1+\lambda_{i,j}),\qquad i\leq j,

covering both the off-diagonal (
i<j
) and diagonal (
i=j
) cases.

Here, and in the spectral estimates of
Lemma
4.14
, we use the equivalent Hilbert-scale
norm on
\mathcal{V}
for which the eigenbasis
\{\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\}_{i\leq j}
is orthogonal.
On the spectral core set

\|x\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{i\leq j}(1+\lambda_{i,j})|\langle x,\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle|^{2}.

Equivalently, this is the intersection norm built from the coercive
form
[u,v]_{B}\coloneqq\langle-Bu,v\rangle_{V^{*},V}+\lambda\langle u,v\rangle_{H}
of
Definition
2.2
(iv)(c). Coercivity yields constants

0<c\leq C
such that

c\|x\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\|x\|_{\mathcal{V},B}\leq C\|x\|_{\mathcal{V}},\qquad x\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}.

Moreover,
\mathbf{P}_{d}
is contractive in
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
and

\mathcal{T}_{d}(t)\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}\mathcal{T}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}
is a contraction semigroup in this norm. The
estimates below are taken in
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
and transferred back to the
original
\mathcal{V}
norm by this equivalence; constants are absorbed into

C_{\mathcal{V}}
and
C_{3}
.

Now write
x=\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i,j}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\in\mathcal{H}
. Since

\mathcal{T}(t)\mathbf{e}_{i,j}=e^{-\lambda_{i,j}t}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}(t)x\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\leq C_{3}\sum_{i\leq j}(1+\lambda_{i,j})e^{-2\lambda_{i,j}t}|x_{i,j}|^{2}\leq C_{3}\sup_{r\geq 0}(1+r)e^{-2rt}\,\|x\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

Since
\sup_{r\geq 0}(1+r)e^{-2rt}\leq C(1+t^{-1})
, this yields

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}(t)x\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq C_{\mathcal{V}}(1+t^{-1/2})\|x\|_{\mathcal{H}},\qquad t>0.

The continuity of
t\mapsto\mathcal{T}(t)x
as a
\mathcal{V}
-valued map on

(0,\infty)
follows from the dominated convergence theorem applied to the
coefficient expansion above.
∎

As before, let
(e_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be the orthonormal basis of
H
consisting of
the eigenvectors of
B
and for every
d\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
H_{d}
the

d
-dimensional subspace of
H
spanned by the first
d
basis vectors,
i.e.
H_{d}\coloneqq\lin\left\{e_{i}\colon i=1,\ldots,d\right\}
and let

\mathcal{H}_{d}\coloneqq\lin\left\{\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\colon\,1\leq i\leq j\leq d\,\right\}
. To prove the
existence and convergence of the
d^{\mathrm{th}}
-Galerkin approximations

\phi_{d}(\cdot,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))
and
\psi_{d}(\cdot,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))
, we collect some
important properties of the operators
L
and
B
, and the function
\hat{R}
in
the following lemma:

Lemma 4.3
.

For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
the following holds:

i)

\mathcal{H}_{d}\subseteq\dom(L)
for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

ii)

B\rvert_{H_{d}}
is a bounded operator.

Proof.

The family
(\mathbf{e}_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
is the complete orthonormal basis of

\mathcal{H}
consisting of eigenvectors of
L
, with
L\mathbf{e}_{i,j}=-\lambda_{i,j}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}

(see (
2.11
)). Since

\mathcal{H}_{d}=\lin\left\{\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\colon 1\leq i\leq j\leq d\right\}
is a finite linear
combination of these eigenvectors,
\mathcal{H}_{d}\subseteq\dom(L)
for every

d\in\mathbb{N}
. (We work with the spectral operator
L
rather than with an
unbounded Hilbert-space realization of the form-level map
\mathbf{B}
; only

\mathcal{H}_{d}\subseteq\dom(L)
and the boundedness of the finite-rank restriction are
used below.)

The restriction of operator
B
on the finite-dimensional subspace
H_{d}
,
i.e.
B\rvert_{H_{d}}
, is bounded
as
H_{d}=\lin\left\{e_{i}\colon i=1,\ldots,d\right\}
for

\left\{e_{i}\colon i=1,\ldots,d\right\}
the set of eigenvectors of
B
and hence

\mathrm{ran}(B\rvert_{H_{d}})\subseteq H_{d}
.
∎

To ensure that the solution
\psi(t,u)
remains within the cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}
for
all
t\geq 0
and
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, we introduce the concept of

quasi-monotonicity
:

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\mu^{(k)}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{\{\|\xi\|>1/k\}}\,\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)
and, for each
u\in\dom(L)
, we define the map

\displaystyle\hat{R}^{(k)}(u)\coloneqq\Gamma^{*}(u)-\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\left(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\xi,u\rangle}-1+\langle\chi(\xi),u\rangle\right)\frac{\mu^{(k)}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)}{\|\xi\|^{2}}.

Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the map
\hat{R}^{(k)}
is Lipschitz
continuous and quasi-monotone with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Since

\hat{R}^{(k)}(0)=0
, its Lipschitz continuity also gives

\|\hat{R}^{(k)}(u)\|\leq K_{k}\|u\|
for some constant
K_{k}<\infty
and all

u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. That is, for all

v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
satisfying
v_{1}\leq_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}v_{2}
and
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}

such that
\langle v_{2}-v_{1},u\rangle=0
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\hat{R}^{(k)}(v_{2})-\hat{R}^{(k)}(v_{1}),u\rangle\geq 0.

Proof.

The Lipschitz continuity of
\hat{R}^{(k)}
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
follows from

[
22
, Lemma 3.3]
, and the quasi-monotonicity is established by

[
22
, Lemma 3.2]
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

We record here, for use in the proof below, that the cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is
regular
in
\mathcal{H}
: every sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{H}^{+}
that is monotone decreasing (
x_{n+1}\leq_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}x_{n}
) and bounded below by
0
converges strongly in
\mathcal{H}
. This follows from the general theory of ordered Hilbert spaces: the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is an order-compatible inner product on
\mathcal{H}
(i.e.
\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\geq 0
for
x,y\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
), and the cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is self-dual and generating. In this setting,
[
58
, Theorem 2.1]
(see also
[
65
, Chapter V, §7]
) implies that bounded monotone sequences converge strongly. In particular, any monotone decreasing sequence in
\mathcal{H}^{+}
that is bounded below by
0\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
must be norm-convergent in
\mathcal{H}
.

With these two lemmas in hand, we can now proceed to prove
Proposition
2.8
.

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
, and
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
We split the proof into a finite-dimensional and an
infinite-dimensional part. On each invariant subspace
\mathcal{H}_{d}
, the
restricted operator
L_{d}=\mathbf{P}_{d}L\mathbf{P}_{d}
is bounded, so the Galerkin system can
be treated by the same argument as in the regular case; compare

[
50
, Proposition 2.7]
. The genuinely new part in the
present paper is the construction of the mild solution to
(
2.14b
) for the unbounded Lyapunov operator
L
and the
derivation of a convergence estimate that is uniform in
d
.
The uniqueness of the
solutions
\phi(\cdot,u)
and
\psi(\cdot,u)
to equations
(
2.14a
) and (
2.14b
), as well as the
solutions
\phi_{d}(\cdot,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))
and

\psi_{d}(\cdot,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))
to equations
(
2.17a
) and (
2.17b
),
follows from the fact that the mappings
F
,
\hat{R}
,
F_{d}
, and

\hat{R}_{d}
are Lipschitz continuous on bounded sets, which follows from
[
50
, Lemma 4.1]
.

Additionally, note that due to the continuity of
F
and
F^{d}
, given
solutions
\psi(\cdot,u)
and
\psi_{d}(\cdot,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))
of
(
2.14b
) and (
2.17b
),
respectively, we can define:

\displaystyle\phi(t,u)=\int_{0}^{t}F(\psi(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\quad t\geq 0,

and

\displaystyle\phi_{d}(t,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))=\int_{0}^{t}F^{d}(\psi^{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u)))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\quad t\geq 0,

which are the unique continuously differentiable solutions to
(
2.14a
) and (
2.17a
),
respectively. Therefore, it remains to show the existence of a solution to
equations (
2.14b
) and
(
2.17b
).

By Lemma
4.3
, the operator
B\rvert_{H_{d}}
is
bounded, hence the Galerkin approximation of the generalized Riccati equations
along the eigenvectors are continuous in the sense that the linear part
B\rvert_{H_{d}}
is bounded. Consequently, the existence of a solution to the Galerkin
approximation follows directly from the corresponding result in the regular
affine case, established in
[
50
, Proposition
2.7]
.

It remains to prove the existence of a mild solution
\psi(\cdot,u)
to the
semi-linear equation (
2.14b
) on
[0,T]
with the initial
condition
\psi(0,u)=u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, satisfying the variation-of-constants
formula (
2.19
).

First, by Lemma
4.1
, the operator semigroup

(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is strongly continuous with generator
(L,\dom(L))
,
and satisfies
\mathcal{T}(t)(\mathcal{H}^{+})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{+}
for all
t\geq 0
. Moreover, by Lemma
4.4
, the function

\hat{R}^{(k)}
is locally Lipschitz continuous on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
quasi-monotone with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The quasi-monotonicity of

\hat{R}^{(k)}
is equivalent to the condition

\lim_{h\to 0+}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{H}^{+}}\frac{\|x+h\hat{R}^{(k)}(x)-y\|}{h}=0
, as shown in
[
28
, Lemma 4.1 & Example 4.1]
. Therefore, we can
apply the results of
[
53
, Chapter 8, Theorem 2.1 & Remark 2.1]
,
which ensures the existence of a
T_{1}>0
and a mapping

\psi^{(k)}(\cdot,u)\colon[0,T_{1})\to\mathcal{H}^{+}
, which is the unique local
solution to equation (
2.14b
) when replacing
\hat{R}
with
\hat{R}^{(k)}
, satisfying

(4.3)

\displaystyle\psi^{(k)}(t,u)=\mathcal{T}(t)u+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\quad t\in[0,T_{1}).
