Next, we show that this unique local solution
\psi^{(k)}(\cdot,u)
can be
extended to
[0,T]
for any
T>T_{1}
. To do this, note that the map

u\mapsto\hat{R}^{(k)}(u)
maps bounded sets of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
to bounded sets
of
\mathcal{H}
. By
[
53
, Chapter 8, Theorem 2.1]
, it suffices to show that

\|\psi^{(k)}(t,u)\|<\infty
for every
t\in[0,T_{1})
. This implies
that the solution
\psi^{(k)}(\cdot,u)
on
[0,T_{1})
can be extended to
[0,T]
,
thus proving that
\psi^{(k)}(\cdot,u)
exists on the entire interval

[0,T]
, as desired.

We now check that
\|\psi^{(k)}(t,u)\|<\infty
for every
t\in[0,T_{1})

holds. Note first that by part i) of Lemma
4.3
, it
holds
\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\in\dom(L)
for every
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
. The coordinate
functional
t\mapsto\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle
is differentiable
on
[0,T_{1})
: differentiating the variation-of-constants
formula (
4.3
) against
\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\in\dom(L)

yields

\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\lambda_{i,j}t}\langle u,\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\lambda_{i,j}(t-s)}\langle\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(s,u)),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

so the coordinate is absolutely continuous in
t
(the integrand

s\mapsto\langle\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(s,u)),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle
is bounded
by
\|\hat{R}^{(k)}\circ\psi^{(k)}\|_{C([0,T_{1});\mathcal{H})}<\infty
on
[0,T_{1})
).
Differentiating this identity directly gives the displayed ODE.
Moreover, on
[0,T_{1})
we have

\displaystyle\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),L(\mathbf{e}_{i,j})\rangle+\langle\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(t,u)),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{i,j}\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle+\langle\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(t,u)),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=2\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{i,j}\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle\langle\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(t,u)),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle,

and hence by the variation-of-constant formula we see that

\displaystyle\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-2\lambda_{i,j}t}\langle u,\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2\int_{0}^{t}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-2\lambda_{i,j}(t-s)}\langle\psi^{(k)}(s,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle\langle\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(s,u)),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Given that

\displaystyle\|\psi^{(k)}(t,u)\|^{2}=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N},i\leq j}\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle^{2}

and

\displaystyle\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(t,u))\rangle=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N},i\leq j}\langle\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle\langle\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(t,u)),\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle,

it follows from the estimate
\|\hat{R}^{(k)}(u)\|\leq K_{k}\|u\|
from
Lemma
4.4
that:

\displaystyle\|\psi^{(k)}(t,u)\|^{2}\leq\|u\|^{2}+M_{k}\int_{0}^{t}\|\psi^{(k)}(s,u)\|^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

for some constant
M_{k}>0
(depending on the fixed truncation level
k
). Thus,
by applying Gronwall’s inequality, we conclude that for all
t\in[0,T_{1})
:

\displaystyle\|\psi^{(k)}(t,u)\|^{2}\leq\|u\|^{2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{M_{k}t}<\infty.

Therefore,
\|\psi^{(k)}(t,u)\|<\infty
for every
t\in[0,T_{1})
. Hence,
following
[
53
, Chapter 8, Proposition 4.1]
, we conclude that there
exists a unique solution
\psi^{(k)}(\cdot,u)
to
equation (
2.14b
) on the entire interval
[0,T]
given
by (
4.3
).

It follows by the same reasoning as in
[
22
, Proposition 3.7]
that

\psi^{(k+1)}(t,u)\;\leq_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\;\psi^{(k)}(t,u),\qquad t\in[0,T],\ u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}.

Hence, for fixed
t\in[0,T]
and
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, the sequence

\bigl(\psi^{(k)}(t,u)\bigr)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is monotone decreasing with respect
to the partial order induced by the cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and bounded from below by
0
.
Since the cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is regular, i.e. every monotone and order-bounded
sequence in
\mathcal{H}^{+}
converges strongly in
\mathcal{H}
, there exists for every

t\in[0,T]
and
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
a limit

\psi(t,u)\coloneqq\lim_{k\to\infty}\psi^{(k)}(t,u)\quad\text{in }\mathcal{H}.

By the same reasoning as in
[
22
, Lemma 3.6]
, the truncated mappings

\hat{R}^{(k)}
converge to
\hat{R}
uniformly on bounded subsets of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Thus by the monotonicity of the sequence
\psi^{(k)}(t,u)
in

k\in\mathbb{N}
and by inserting formula (
4.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\psi(t,u)

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\psi^{(k)}(t,u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(t)u+\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(t)u+\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\bigl(\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(s,u))-\hat{R}(\psi^{(k)}(s,u))\bigr)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle+\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{R}(\psi^{(k)}(s,u))\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(t)u+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

To justify the interchange of the limit and the integral in the last equality, we argue as follows. By monotonicity in the cone,
0\leq_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\psi^{(k)}(s,u)\leq_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\psi^{(1)}(s,u)
for all
k
and
s\in[0,T]
; since the cone is self-dual, each order interval is norm bounded, and the Gronwall estimate for the fixed truncation
k=1
gives
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\|\psi^{(1)}(s,u)\|<\infty
. Hence there exists
\tilde{M}_{T}>0
such that
\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\|\psi^{(k)}(s,u)\|\leq\tilde{M}_{T}
. On the ball
\{v\in\mathcal{H}^{+}:\|v\|\leq\tilde{M}_{T}\}
, local Lipschitz continuity and
\hat{R}(0)=0
imply that
\hat{R}
is bounded, and the uniform convergence
\hat{R}^{(k)}\to\hat{R}
on bounded subsets of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(from
[
22
, Lemma 3.6]
) gives a uniform bound for
\hat{R}^{(k)}
on the same ball, after absorbing finitely many initial
k
. Thus
\|\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{R}^{(k)}(\psi^{(k)}(s,u))\|\leq C_{T}
uniformly in
k
and
s\in[0,t]
. The dominated convergence theorem therefore justifies passing the limit through the integral, using pointwise convergence
\psi^{(k)}(s,u)\to\psi(s,u)
for each
s
and the same uniform convergence of
\hat{R}^{(k)}
on bounded subsets.

It remains to record that the limiting path is continuous. Set

g(s)\coloneqq\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))
. The preceding uniform bound and the linear growth
of
\hat{R}
imply
\sup_{s\leq T}\|g(s)\|<\infty
. Let
t_{n}\to t
in

[0,T]
. Strong continuity of
(\mathcal{T}(r))_{r\geq 0}
gives

\mathcal{T}(t_{n})u\to\mathcal{T}(t)u
. For the integral term, assume first
t_{n}\geq t
; the
other case is identical. Then

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t_{n}}\mathcal{T}(t_{n}-s)g(s)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)g(s)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|(\mathcal{T}(t_{n}-s)-\mathcal{T}(t-s))g(s)\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+\int_{t}^{t_{n}}\|g(s)\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

The first term tends to zero by dominated convergence, using contraction of

\mathcal{T}
and pointwise strong continuity; the second tends to zero by boundedness
of
g
. Thus
t\mapsto\psi(t,u)
is continuous as an
\mathcal{H}
-valued map. Since

\mathcal{H}^{+}
is closed,
\psi(\cdot,u)\in C([0,T],\mathcal{H}^{+})
.
This proves the existence of a unique solution to
equation (
2.14b
) satisfying the variation-of-constants
formula (
2.19
).

This is the only place where the irregular setting genuinely differs
from the regular case: the bounded-generator ODE argument from

[
50
]
must here be replaced by a semilinear mild
equation argument for the unbounded Lyapunov operator
L
. Once the mild
solution is available, the convergence estimate follows by the same perturbation
strategy as in the regular case.

Lastly, we prove the uniform convergence stated
in (
2.21
).
First, note that the semigroup
(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
commutes with the projection

\mathbf{P}_{d}
for every
d\in\mathbb{N}
, since both operators are diagonal with
respect to the orthonormal eigenbasis
\{\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\}_{i\leq j}
of the generator

L
, and
\mathbf{P}_{d}
projects onto the invariant subspace

\mathcal{H}_{d}=\mathrm{span}\{\mathbf{e}_{i,j}:1\leq i\leq j\leq d\}
.

At this point one can follow the same perturbation argument as in the
proof of
[
50
, Proposition 2.7]
, with the bounded
semigroup
\mathrm{e}^{tB^{*}}
there replaced here by the Lyapunov semigroup

(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and using the commutation relation

\mathbf{P}_{d}\mathcal{T}(t)=\mathcal{T}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}
.
By setting
M_{T}\coloneqq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\mathcal{T}(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}
, we obtain for

t\in[0,T]
and
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
the estimate

\displaystyle\|\psi(t,u)-\psi_{d}(t,\mathbf{P}_{d}u)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{T}(t)\,u-\mathcal{T}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}u\|

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\Big\|\mathcal{T}(t-s)\Big(\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))-\hat{R}_{d}(\psi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}u))\Big)\Big\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\leq M_{T}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}u\|+M_{T}\int_{0}^{t}\Big\|\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))-\hat{R}_{d}(\psi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}u))\Big\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Next, we split the difference by adding and subtracting
\mathbf{P}_{d}\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))
:

\displaystyle\Big\|\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))-\hat{R}_{d}(\psi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}u))\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))-\mathbf{P}_{d}\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))\Big\|

\displaystyle\quad+\Big\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))-\hat{R}_{d}(\psi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}u))\Big\|.

Combining the two displays yields

\displaystyle\|\psi(t,u)-\psi_{d}(t,\mathbf{P}_{d}u)\|

\displaystyle\leq M_{T}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}u\|+M_{T}\int_{0}^{t}\Big\|\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))-\mathbf{P}_{d}\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))\Big\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+M_{T}\int_{0}^{t}\Big\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))-\hat{R}_{d}(\psi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}u))\Big\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

We claim that there exists
M>0
, independent of
d\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\|\psi(t,u)\|\leq M
and
\|\psi_{d}(t,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))\|\leq M
for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
. For
\psi(\cdot,u)
this follows from the Gronwall bound established above. For
\psi_{d}(\cdot,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))
, the same Gronwall argument applies uniformly in
d
: the local Lipschitz constant of
\hat{R}_{d}=\mathbf{P}_{d}\hat{R}\mathbf{P}_{d}
on bounded sets is bounded by the same constant as that of
\hat{R}
(since
\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\|\leq 1
), and the initial data
\|\mathbf{P}_{d}(u)\|\leq\|u\|
is uniformly bounded. Thus
M
can be chosen independently of
d
. Now, let
M>0
be as above, then by the local
Lipschitz property of
\hat{R}
, we find a constant
K_{M}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{d}(\hat{R}(\psi(s,u)))-\hat{R}_{d}(\psi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u)))\|\leq K_{M}\|\psi(s,u)-\psi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))\|,

and thus by setting

\displaystyle K^{d}_{t}=M_{T}\Big(\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}(u)\|+\int_{0}^{t}\|\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))-\mathbf{P}_{d}\hat{R}(\psi(s,u))\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\Big),

we obtain

\displaystyle\|\psi(t,u)-\psi_{d}(t,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))\|\leq K_{t}^{d}+M_{T}K_{M}\int_{0}^{t}\|\psi(s,u)-\psi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Again, by an application of Gronwall’s inequality, we find that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\|\psi(\cdot,u)-\psi_{d}(\cdot,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))\|\leq K_{t}^{d}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{M_{T}K_{M}t}.

Hence taking the supremum over all
t\in[0,T]
on both sides
of (
4.4
) yields

(4.5)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\psi(t,u)-\psi_{d}(t,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))\|\leq K_{T}^{d}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{M_{T}K_{M}T}.

Similarly, to the proof
[
50
, Proposition 3.1]
we can
then also bound the Galerkin approximation error of the first
equation (
2.14a
) as

(4.6)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\phi(t,u)-\phi_{d}(t,\mathbf{P}_{d}(u))|

\displaystyle\leq K_{2,M}K_{T}^{d}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{M_{T}K_{M}T},\quad t\in[0,T],u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}.

Combining (
4.5
) and (
4.6
), and
using that
\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}z\|\to 0
for every
z\in\mathcal{H}
, we conclude that the
right-hand sides tend to zero as
d\to\infty
. This proves
(
2.21
). Thus the approximation argument
has the same perturbative structure as in the regular case, but the
convergence is expressed here qualitatively at the level of the projected mild
Riccati solutions.
∎

Lemma 4.6

(Continuity of the mild Riccati flow in the initial datum)
.

Let
u_{n},u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
with
\|u_{n}-u\|\to 0
. Then for every

T<\infty
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(|\phi(t,u_{n})-\phi(t,u)|+\|\psi(t,u_{n})-\psi(t,u)\|\right)\to 0.

Proof.

Since
u_{n}\to u
, the set
\{u\}\cup\{u_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is bounded in
\mathcal{H}
.
The Gronwall estimate used in the proof of
Proposition
2.8
therefore gives a constant

M_{T}<\infty
such that

\sup_{n}\sup_{t\leq T}\|\psi(t,u_{n})\|+\sup_{t\leq T}\|\psi(t,u)\|\leq M_{T}.

Let
L_{T}
be a Lipschitz constant of
\hat{R}
on

\{z\in\mathcal{H}^{+}:\|z\|\leq M_{T}\}
. Subtracting the mild equations and using
contractivity of
\mathcal{T}
gives

\|\psi(t,u_{n})-\psi(t,u)\|\leq\|u_{n}-u\|+L_{T}\int_{0}^{t}\|\psi(s,u_{n})-\psi(s,u)\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Gronwall’s lemma yields

\sup_{t\leq T}\|\psi(t,u_{n})-\psi(t,u)\|\leq\|u_{n}-u\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{L_{T}T}\to 0.

The functions
F
are locally Lipschitz on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
by

[
22
, Remark 3.4]
; with
L_{T}^{F}
a Lipschitz constant on the same
bounded ball,

\sup_{t\leq T}|\phi(t,u_{n})-\phi(t,u)|\leq L_{T}^{F}\int_{0}^{T}\|\psi(s,u_{n})-\psi(s,u)\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\to 0.

This proves the claim.
∎

4.2.
Affine Finite-Rank Approximations and Their Weak Convergence

In this section, we provide the proofs for
Proposition
2.9
and
Theorem
2.10
. We build on the finite-rank approximation
framework for
regular affine processes
introduced in

[
50
]
, making the necessary adjustments to handle the

irregular case
.

4.2.1.
Finite-Rank Admissible Parameters

First, we introduce finite-rank approximations for the irregular admissible
parameter set
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
. Then, we demonstrate that a finite-rank
affine process can be associated with this approximate parameter set.

We use the following notation: for any two measurable spaces
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1})

and
(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2})
, and a measurable function
f:(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1})\to(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2})
, we denote the push-forward of a measure
\mu_{1}:\mathcal{E}_{1}\to[0,\infty]

with respect to
f
as
f_{*}\mu_{1}
. Specifically, we have
f_{*}\mu_{1}(A)=\mu_{1}(f^{-1}(A))
for any
A\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
. Note that
f_{*}\mu_{1}
is a proper
measure on
(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2})
. For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the Borel sets

E_{d}\coloneqq\{\xi\in\mathcal{H}^{+}\colon 0<\|\mathbf{P}_{d}(\xi)\|\leq 1,\|\xi\|>1\},

and
E_{d}^{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\{\xi\colon\mathbf{P}_{d}(\xi)\neq 0\}
. Note that

E_{d}\subseteq E_{d}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}
for every
d\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
(vector-valued) measure
\lambda
on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})
, the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}
, we denote the restriction of
\lambda
to the trace of the Borel

\sigma
-algebra generated by the open sets in
E_{d}^{0}
as

\lambda|_{E_{d}^{0}}
. We now introduce the following notion, which differs from the corresponding definition in
[
50
, Definition
5.1]
only in the part iv):

Definition 4.7
.

For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and admissible parameter set
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
we
define the parameters
(b_{d},\mathbf{B}_{d},m_{d},\mu_{d})
and
M_{d}
as follows:

i)

The measure
m_{d}\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}\setminus\left\{0\right\})\to[0,\infty]
is defined as the push-forward of
m|_{E_{d}^{0}}
with
respect to
\mathbf{P}_{d}
, i.e.

\displaystyle m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\coloneqq(\mathbf{P}_{d\,*}m|_{E_{d}^{0}})(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

ii)

The vector
b_{d}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}
is given by

(4.7)

\displaystyle b_{d}\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}(b)+\int_{\mathbf{P}_{d}(E_{d})}\xi\,m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

iii)

The
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}
-valued measure
\mu_{d}\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}\setminus\left\{0\right\})\to\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}
is defined as the

\mathbf{P}_{d}
-projection of the push-forward of
\mu|_{E_{d}^{0}}
with respect to

\mathbf{P}_{d}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mu_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d\,*}\mu|_{E_{d}^{0}})(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

Moreover, we define the
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}
-valued measure
M_{d}
on

\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
as follows: For every

A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}\setminus\left\{0\right\})
we set

(4.8)

\displaystyle M_{d}(A)\coloneqq\int_{E_{d}^{0}}\mathbf{1}_{A}(\mathbf{P}_{d}(\xi))\frac{1}{\|\xi\|^{2}}\mathbf{P}_{d}(\mu|_{E_{d}^{0}}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)).

iv)

The linear operator
\mathbf{B}_{d}\colon\mathcal{H}_{d}\to\mathcal{H}_{d}
is given by

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{B}_{d}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}(\mathbf{B}(y))+\int_{\mathbf{P}_{d}(E_{d})}\xi\,\langle y,M_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\rangle

(4.9)

\displaystyle=By+yB^{*}+\Gamma_{d}(y)+\int_{\mathbf{P}_{d}(E_{d})}\xi\,\langle y,M_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\rangle,\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d},

The displayed formula defines the linear extension on all of

\mathcal{H}_{d}
; the admissibility checks below are applied on the cone

\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
.
Here
\Gamma_{d}\colon\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}\to\mathcal{H}_{d}
is given by

(4.10)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{d}(y)\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}(\Gamma(y)).

The idea behind the approximate parameter set is that we can use the parameters

b_{d}
,
m_{d}
,
M_{d}
, and
\mathbf{B}_{d}
to express the functions
F_{d}
and

\hat{R}_{d}
as follows:

(4.11)

\displaystyle F_{d}(u)

\displaystyle=\langle b_{d},u\rangle-\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}\setminus\{0\}}\left(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\xi,u\rangle}-1+\langle\chi(\xi),u\rangle\right)m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi),

and by denoting
K(\xi,u)\coloneqq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\xi,u\rangle}-1+\langle\chi(\xi),u\rangle
and writing

\Gamma_{d}^{*}(u_{d})\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}(\Gamma^{*}(u_{d}))
for the adjoint action on

\mathcal{H}_{d}
, we can express
\hat{R}_{d}
for every
u_{d}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}
as

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{d}(u_{d})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}(\Gamma^{*}(u_{d}))-\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\left(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\xi,u_{d}\rangle}-1+\langle\chi(\xi),u_{d}\rangle\right)\mathbf{P}_{d}(M(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi))

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{d}^{*}(u_{d})+\int_{\mathbf{P}_{d}(E_{d})}\langle\xi,u_{d}\rangle\,M_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)-\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}\setminus\{0\}}K(\xi,u_{d})\,M_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

4.2.2.
Matrix-Valued Admissible Parameters

Next, we relate the finite-rank approximate admissible parameters
b_{d}
,
m_{d}
,

M_{d}
, and
\mathbf{B}_{d}
to their matrix-valued counterparts. This is necessary
in order to apply the existence results for matrix-valued affine processes
from
[
25
]
.

For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\}
the standard basis
of
\mathbb{R}_{d}
, and define the coordinate system
\Xi_{d}\colon\mathbb{R}_{d}\to H_{d}
associated with the basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{d}\}
of
H_{d}
by

(4.12)

\displaystyle\Xi_{d}(v_{i})=e_{i},\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,d.

(We use the symbol
\Xi_{d}
here rather than the more natural

\Phi_{d}
in order to avoid a clash with the joint Galerkin cumulant

\Phi_{d}(t,u)
from (
3.12
).)
The coordinate system
\Xi_{d}
identifies the
d
-dimensional subspace
H_{d}
with
\mathbb{R}_{d}
, allowing us to represent every linear operator
A\in\mathcal{L}(H_{d})
as a
d\times d
-matrix through the mapping
i_{d}\colon\mathcal{L}(H_{d})\to\mathbb{S}_{d}
, defined by

(4.13)

\displaystyle i_{d}(A)\coloneqq\Xi_{d}^{-1}\circ A\circ\Xi_{d},

where, under the usual matrix identification, we interpret
i_{d}(A)
as an element in
\mathbb{M}_{d}
. Note that when
A
is self-adjoint, its matrix representation
i_{d}(A)
is also self-adjoint. This can be verified by taking
x,y\in\mathbb{R}_{d}
and performing the following computation:

\displaystyle(i_{d}(A)x,y)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}=(A\circ\Xi_{d}(x),\Xi_{d}(y))_{H}=(\Xi_{d}(x),A^{*}\circ\Xi_{d}(y))_{H}=(x,i_{d}(A)y)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}.

Under the mapping
i_{d}
in (
4.13
), we identify

\mathcal{H}_{d}|_{H_{d}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(H_{d})
with
\mathbb{S}_{d}
and
note that
i_{d}
is an isometry between
\mathbb{S}_{d}
and

\mathcal{H}_{d}|_{H_{d}}
, meaning that it identifies the Frobenius norm with
the Hilbert-Schmidt norm. In the following, we may sometimes omit writing the
restriction
\rvert_{H_{d}}
when it is clear from the context. Moreover, we
denote by
\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
the convex cone of all symmetric positive
semi-definite
d\times d
-matrices, and observe that positivity is preserved
under
i_{d}
, i.e.
i_{d}(\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+})=\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
be an admissible parameter set as in
Definition
2.2
and for
d\in\mathbb{N}
let

(b_{d},\mathbf{B}_{d},m_{d},\mu_{d})
and
M_{d}
be as in
Definition
4.7
. For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
we define the parameters

(\tilde{b}_{d},\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{d},\tilde{m}_{d},\tilde{\mu}_{d})
and
\tilde{M}_{d}
as follows:

i)

The matrix
\tilde{b}_{d}\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
is
defined as
\tilde{b}_{d}\coloneqq i_{d}(b_{d})
.

ii)

Define the linear operator

\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{d}\colon\mathbb{S}_{d}\to\mathbb{S}_{d}
by

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{d}\coloneqq i_{d}\circ\mathbf{B}_{d}\circ i^{-1}_{d}.

iii)

The measure
\tilde{m}_{d}\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\left\{0\right\})\to[0,\infty]
is defined as the push-forward of
m_{d}
with respect to
i_{d}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\tilde{m}_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\coloneqq(i_{d\,*}m_{d})(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

iv)

The matrix-valued measure
\tilde{\mu}_{d}\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\left\{0\right\})\to\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
is defined as the
composition of
i_{d}
and the push-forward of
\mu_{d}
with respect to

i_{d}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)=i_{d}((i_{d\,*}\mu_{d})(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)).

Moreover, we define the
\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
-valued measure
\tilde{M}_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)

as follows: For every

A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\left\{0\right\})
we set

\displaystyle\tilde{M}_{d}(A)=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\mathbf{1}_{A}(i_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}(\xi)))\frac{1}{\|\xi\|^{2}}i_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}(\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi))),

and for every
x\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
we write
\tilde{M}_{d}(x,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\coloneqq\langle x,\tilde{M}_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\rangle
.

With the definition of the matrix-valued admissible parameters at hand we can
now prove Proposition
2.9
.

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

Throughout this proof we use the following notation, consistent with the
identification
i_{d}:\mathcal{L}(H_{d})\to\mathbb{S}_{d}
introduced in
(
4.13
). For matrices
x,u\in\mathbb{S}_{d}
, we write

x_{d}\coloneqq i_{d}^{-1}(x)\in\mathcal{H}_{d}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
and
u_{d}\coloneqq i_{d}^{-1}(u)\in\mathcal{H}_{d}\subseteq\mathcal{H}

for their operator-valued counterparts on
H_{d}
; the Frobenius pairing on
\mathbb{S}_{d}

is denoted

\langle x,u\rangle_{d}\coloneqq\mathrm{Tr}(xu)
. Since
i_{d}
is an isometry between
\mathbb{S}_{d}

(with Frobenius inner product) and
\mathcal{H}_{d}\rvert_{H_{d}}
(with Hilbert–Schmidt inner
product inherited from
\mathcal{H}
), we have
\langle x,u\rangle_{d}=\langle x_{d},u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
for all
x,u\in\mathbb{S}_{d}
. We also write

\chi_{d}(\zeta)\coloneqq\zeta\mathbf{1}_{\{\|\zeta\|_{d}\leq 1\}}
for the matrix truncation.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tilde{b}_{d}
,
\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{d}
,
\tilde{m}_{d}
,

\tilde{\mu}_{d}
, and
\tilde{M}_{d}
be as defined in parts (i) to (iv) of
Definition
4.8
. The positivity and
integrability conditions for
\tilde{b}_{d}
,
\tilde{m}_{d}
,
\tilde{\mu}_{d}
, and

\tilde{M}_{d}
are inherited from the projected parameters exactly as in

[
50
, Lemma 5.6 and Proposition 5.7]
. These checks
involve only positivity of the cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, the orthogonal projection

\mathbf{P}_{d}
, the pushforward of
m
and
\mu
under
i_{d}\circ\mathbf{P}_{d}
, and the
moment and integrability assumptions from
Definition
2.2
; the unboundedness of the full
Lyapunov operator
L
on
\mathcal{H}
plays no role here, since in the
finite-rank problem the linear drift enters only through the restriction

B\rvert_{H_{d}}
, which is bounded on

H_{d}=\operatorname{span}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
by
Lemma
4.3
. The remaining
point is the matrix admissibility condition for the linear drift. Namely, for
all
x,u\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
such that
\langle x,u\rangle_{d}=0
, we have to prove

(4.15)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{d}(x),u\rangle_{d}-\int_{\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\chi_{d}(\zeta),u\rangle_{d}\,\tilde{M}_{d}(x,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)\geq 0.

To prove this, let
x,u\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
such that

\langle x,u\rangle_{d}=0
. The orthogonality

\langle x,u\rangle_{d}=0
transfers to the operator level: since
i_{d}

identifies the Frobenius and Hilbert–Schmidt inner products,

\langle x_{d},u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle x,u\rangle_{d}=0
with

x_{d},u_{d}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Since
x_{d}
and
u_{d}
are positive
finite-rank operators and
\Tr(x_{d}u_{d})=0
, we have
x_{d}u_{d}=u_{d}x_{d}=0
. As

H_{d}
is invariant under
B
by construction, the Lyapunov part therefore
satisfies

\displaystyle\langle Lx_{d},u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\Tr(Bx_{d}u_{d})+\Tr(B^{*}u_{d}x_{d})=0.

Moreover, by the isometry property of
i_{d}
,

\langle\Gamma_{d}(x_{d}),u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle\mathbf{P}_{d}\Gamma(x_{d}),u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle\Gamma(x_{d}),u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
, because
u_{d}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}
and

\mathbf{P}_{d}
is self-adjoint. For the jump integral we use the
pushforward identity for
\tilde{M}_{d}
: for every bounded Borel

g\colon\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}g(\zeta)\,\tilde{M}_{d}(x,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}g(\zeta)\bigl\langle x,\tilde{M}_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)\bigr\rangle_{d}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}g(i_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}(\eta)))\,\frac{\bigl\langle x_{d},\mathbf{P}_{d}(\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta))\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}{\|\eta\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}g(i_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}(\eta)))\,\frac{\langle\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta),x_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}{\|\eta\|^{2}},

where the second equality is the definition of
\tilde{M}_{d}
in
Definition
4.8
(iv) and the third uses

\mathbf{P}_{d}x_{d}=x_{d}
together with self-adjointness of
\mathbf{P}_{d}
. Specialising
this identity to
g(\zeta)=\langle\chi_{d}(\zeta),u\rangle_{d}
and using the
standard radial truncation
\chi_{d}(\zeta)=\zeta\,\mathbf{1}_{\{\|\zeta\|_{d}\leq 1\}}

together with the isometry identity

\langle i_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta),u\rangle_{d}=\langle\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta,u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle\eta,u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(by
u_{d}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}
and self-adjointness of
\mathbf{P}_{d}
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\chi_{d}(\zeta),u\rangle_{d}\tilde{M}_{d}(x,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\eta,u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{0<\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta\|\leq 1\}}\,\frac{\langle\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta),x_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}{\|\eta\|^{2}}.

The compensation term built into
\mathbf{B}_{d}
gives, again by the
same pushforward calculation,

\displaystyle\left\langle\int_{\mathbf{P}_{d}(E_{d})}\zeta\,\langle x_{d},M_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)\rangle,u_{d}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{d}}\langle\eta,u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\frac{\langle\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta),x_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}{\|\eta\|^{2}}.

Combining the preceding identities with the definition of

E_{d}=\{\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{+}:0<\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta\|\leq 1,\ \|\eta\|>1\}
yields

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{d}(x),u\rangle_{d}-\int_{\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\chi_{d}(\zeta),u\rangle_{d}\,\tilde{M}_{d}(x,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)

\displaystyle\quad=\langle\Gamma(x_{d}),u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\eta,u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\bigl(\mathbf{1}_{E_{d}}(\eta)-\mathbf{1}_{\{0<\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta\|\leq 1\}}(\eta)\bigr)\frac{\langle\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta),x_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}{\|\eta\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\langle\Gamma(x_{d}),u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\chi(\eta),u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\frac{\langle\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta),x_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}{\|\eta\|^{2}}\geq 0.

In the last equality we used

\langle\eta,u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta,u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
, so the
integrand vanishes on
\{\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta=0\}
, and on

\{\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta\neq 0\}
the identity

\mathbf{1}_{E_{d}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{0<\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta\|\leq 1\}}=-\mathbf{1}_{\{0<\|\eta\|\leq 1\}}
holds. The final inequality is exactly
Definition
2.2
2.2 iv)
(b)
applied to
x_{d},u_{d}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
with

\langle x_{d},u_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0
. This proves
(
4.15
).

Together with the already verified finite-dimensional positivity and
integrability conditions, this shows that the corresponding matrix-valued
parameter tuple is admissible in the sense of
[
54
, Definition 3.1]
.
More explicitly, with

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{d}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{b}_{d}-\int_{\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{d}(\zeta)\,\tilde{m}_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta),

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{d}(v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{d}^{*}(v)-\int_{\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\chi_{d}(\zeta),v\rangle_{d}\,\tilde{M}_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta),\qquad v\in\mathbb{S}_{d},

the seven-parameter tuple

(0,\tilde{c}_{d},\tilde{D}_{d},0,0,\tilde{m}_{d},\tilde{M}_{d})
satisfies the
admissibility conditions of
[
54
, Definition 3.1]
; the inequality
(
4.15
) is precisely the quasi-monotonicity
condition for
\tilde{D}_{d}
.
Therefore, by
[
25
, Theorem
2.4]
and
[
54
, Theorem 3.2]
, there exists a unique affine
process
(\tilde{X}^{d})_{t\geq 0}
with values in
\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
.

Note further that the existence of a càdlàg version of

(\tilde{X}^{d})_{t\geq 0}
follows from
[
26
]
, and we will denote this
version again by
(\tilde{X}^{d})_{t\geq 0}
. Moreover, we denote the law of

\tilde{X}^{d}
, given that
\tilde{X}^{d}_{0}=x
, by
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{x}
. By

[
25
, Remark 2.5]
, combined with the second moment assumption in

4.8 iii)
and
4.8 iv)
, the

\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
-valued affine process
(\tilde{X}^{d})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{x}\left(\left\{\tilde{X}^{d}_{t}\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\colon t\geq 0\right\}\right)=1.

For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
, the law

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{x}
is thus defined on
\mathcal{B}(D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}))
. Furthermore, since the diffusion part is zero, it follows
from
[
54
]
that
(\tilde{X}^{d})_{t\geq 0}
is a finite-variation
process.

Additionally, from
[
25
, Theorem 2.6]
, we know that the process

\tilde{X}^{d}
is a semimartingale with characteristics given by:

\displaystyle\tilde{C}^{d}_{t}=0,\quad\tilde{\nu}^{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}t,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)=\big(\tilde{m}_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+\tilde{M}_{d}(\tilde{X}^{d}_{t},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\big)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}t,

and

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{d}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{b}_{d}+\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{d}(\tilde{X}^{d}_{s})\right)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Finally, since the process
(\tilde{X}^{d}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is of finite
variation, it is locally bounded and by the admissibility in the matrix sense,
we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{d}^{2}\,\tilde{\nu}^{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)<\infty,

for all
t\geq 0
. By
[
46
, Chapter II, Proposition 2.29(b)]
,
this finiteness of the predictable compensator of the square-jump measure
ensures that the compensated jump integral
\bar{J}^{d}
in
(
4.16
) is a purely discontinuous locally
square-integrable martingale on
\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
. Combined with the admissibility
moment bound in
4.8 iii)
, we obtain the second-moment estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{x}}\left[\|\tilde{X}^{d}_{t}\|_{d}^{2}\right]<\infty,

for all
t\geq 0
.

Now, for every
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let

(\tilde{X}^{d},(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}})
be the

\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
-valued affine process given as above. More precisely, let

\tilde{X}^{d}
be a version with paths in

\Omega=D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+})
, and denote by
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{x}
the
law of
\tilde{X}^{d}
, defined on
\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
, given that

\tilde{X}_{0}^{d}=x\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
. Moreover, let

(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{d}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denote the natural filtration of the process

\tilde{X}^{d}
.

By identifying the cones
\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
and
\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
under the mapping

i_{d}^{-1}
, we define the process
X^{d}=(X^{d}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
as

\displaystyle X^{d}_{t}\coloneqq i_{d}^{-1}(\tilde{X}^{d}_{t})=\Xi_{d}\circ\tilde{X}^{d}_{t}\circ\Xi_{d}^{-1},\quad t\geq 0.

Note that the process
(X^{d}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
has paths in

D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+})
, and the law of
X^{d}
is given by the push-forward
measure
(i_{d}^{-1})_{*}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{x}
for
x\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
. Here,

i_{d}^{-1}
acts pointwise on the functions in

D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+})
, such that

\displaystyle i_{d}^{-1}(D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}))=D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}).

Moreover, we observe that

D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+})\subseteq D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})
for all

d\in\mathbb{N}
, see
[
47
, Remark 4.5]
. For every
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, we
define the measure
\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x}
on
D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x}(A)=(i_{d}^{-1})_{*}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{i_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}(x))}(A\cap D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+})),\quad A\in\mathcal{B}(D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})).

Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x}\big(X^{d}_{0}=\mathbf{P}_{d}(x)\big)=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{i_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}(x))}\big(\tilde{X}^{d}_{0}=i_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}(x))\big)=1,

and the process
(X^{d},(\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}})
is a Markov process on the
ambient space
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with respect to its natural filtration

\mathbb{F}^{d}=(\mathcal{F}^{d}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. We set
\mathcal{F}^{d}=\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\infty}
. Moreover, for
every
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, denoting by
\mathcal{N}^{d}_{x}
the collection of all

\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x}
-null sets of
\mathcal{F}^{d}
, we define

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}^{d}_{t}\vee\mathcal{N}^{d}_{x}\quad\text{for every }t\geq 0,

and set
\bar{\mathbb{F}}^{d}\coloneqq(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. In other words,

\bar{\mathbb{F}}^{d}
is the usual augmented filtration of
X^{d}
, and
X^{d}
remains a
Markov process with respect to
\bar{\mathbb{F}}^{d}
.

It remains to verify that the Galerkin approximation

(X^{d},(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d})_{x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}})
is an affine process on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
satisfying the
affine transform formula (
2.23
) associated with the
finite-rank Riccati
equations (
2.17a
)-(
2.17b
),
and that the canonical process of
\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x}
on
D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+})
is a
semimartingale with respect to
(\Omega,\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{d},\bar{\mathbb{F}}^{d},\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x})
.

For the affine transform formula, we apply the matrix-valued affine
transform theorem
[
54
, Theorem 3.2]
to

(\tilde{X}^{d},(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{\tilde{x}})_{\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}})
: for every

\tilde{u}\in\mathbb{S}_{d}^{+}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\tilde{x}}^{d}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\tilde{X}_{t}^{d},\tilde{u}\rangle_{d}}\right]=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\tilde{\phi}_{d}(t,\tilde{u})-\langle\tilde{x},\tilde{\psi}_{d}(t,\tilde{u})\rangle_{d}},\qquad t\geq 0,

where
(\tilde{\phi}_{d},\tilde{\psi}_{d})
is the unique solution of the matrix-valued
Riccati system associated with

(\tilde{b}_{d},\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{d},\tilde{m}_{d},\tilde{\mu}_{d})
in the sense
of
[
25
]
. Pulling the exponent back under
i_{d}
and using the identities

\langle\tilde{X}_{t}^{d},\tilde{u}\rangle_{d}=\langle X_{t}^{d},u\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
and

\langle\tilde{x},\tilde{\psi}_{d}(t,\tilde{u})\rangle_{d}=\langle\mathbf{P}_{d}(x),i_{d}^{-1}\tilde{\psi}_{d}(t,i_{d}(u))\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
for

\tilde{u}=i_{d}(u)
, one obtains (
2.23
); the identification
of
i_{d}^{-1}\tilde{\psi}_{d}(t,i_{d}(\cdot))
with the mild solution

\psi^{(d)}(t,\cdot)
of the finite-rank operator Riccati
equation (
2.17b
) is uniqueness in the
ODE-system
[
25
, Theorem 2.4]
.

For the semimartingale and martingale claims, we reuse the computation
already done above: the characteristics of
X^{d}
under
\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}
are obtained
by pulling back those of
\tilde{X}^{d}
via
i_{d}^{-1}
, which preserves the
finite-variation and predictable-compensator structure. The compensated jump
integral in (
4.16
) is then a locally square-integrable
martingale: the predictable compensator of the square-jump measure is bounded
by
C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})
by
Lemma
4.11
; the local boundedness of

\tilde{X}^{d}
(from finite variation of
\tilde{X}^{d}
) then implies the
square-integrability of
\bar{J}^{d}
on compact intervals via

[
46
, Chapter II, Proposition 2.29(b)]
. Only the admissibility of the
Galerkin parameters and the second-moment bound from
4.8 iii)
are
used; both are preserved under the unbounded generator
B
considered
here. Hence

\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}(\{X_{t}^{d}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}\colon t\geq 0\})=1

and (
2.23
) holds. In particular, defining the compensated
jump process
(\bar{J}^{d}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by

(4.16)

\displaystyle\bar{J}^{d}_{t}\coloneqq X^{d}_{t}\!-\!\mathbf{P}_{d}(x)\!-\!\!\int_{0}^{t}\!\left(b_{d}+\mathbf{B}_{d}(X^{d}_{s})+\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}\cap\{\|\xi\|>1\}}\xi\,(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(X^{d}_{s},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi))\right)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

the process
\bar{J}^{d}
is a locally square-integrable martingale on

\mathcal{H}_{d}
by the argument above.
∎

4.2.3.
Weak Convergence of the Affine Finite-Rank Processes

Let
(b,B,m,\mu)
be an admissible parameter set, and for each

d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
X^{d}
denote the associated affine finite-rank
operator-valued process given by
Proposition
2.9
. In this section, we study the
tightness and weak convergence of the sequence
(X^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
in
the space
D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})
equipped with the Skorohod topology. More
precisely, for each
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, we consider the sequence

(\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
, which represents the laws of
X^{d}
given that

X^{d}_{0}=\mathbf{P}_{d}(x)
, defined on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{B}(D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+}))
, and examine its weak convergence as

d\to\infty
.

To make the weak-convergence argument rigorous, we now separate the
three ingredients that are needed in the irregular setting: a uniform
square-moment bound for the projected semimartingales, a positive-time
smoothing estimate in
\mathcal{V}
, and the identification of the weak limit via the
martingale problem. Once these ingredients are established, the proof follows
the same template as in
[
50
, Section 6]
.

Lemma 4.9

(Maximal inequality for jump stochastic convolutions)
.

Let
E
be a Hilbert space and let
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a contraction

C_{0}
-semigroup on
E
. Let
\mu
be an integer-valued random measure on

[a,b]\times E
with predictable compensator
\nu
, and assume

\mathbb{E}\int_{a}^{b}\int_{E}\|\zeta\|_{E}^{2}\,\nu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)<\infty.

Then the stochastic convolution

Z_{r}=\int_{a}^{r}\int_{E}S(r-s)\zeta\,(\mu-\nu)(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta),\qquad a\leq r\leq b,

satisfies

\mathbb{E}\sup_{a\leq r\leq b}\|Z_{r}\|_{E}^{2}\leq C\,\mathbb{E}\int_{a}^{b}\int_{E}\|\zeta\|_{E}^{2}\,\nu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta),

with a universal constant
C
independent of
E
,
S
, and the length of the
interval.

Proof.

This is the Kotelenez maximal inequality for Hilbert-space stochastic
convolutions with contraction semigroups; see, for instance,

[
56
, Theorem 9.15]
. Applied to the square-integrable purely
discontinuous martingale

\int_{a}^{\cdot}\int_{E}\zeta\,(\mu-\nu)(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)
, the predictable quadratic
variation is exactly

\int_{a}^{\cdot}\int_{E}\|\zeta\|_{E}^{2}\,\nu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)
, giving the stated
estimate.
∎

Corollary 4.10

(Endpoint estimate for stopped jump stochastic convolutions)
.

Let
E
be a Hilbert space and let
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a contraction

C_{0}
-semigroup on
E
. Let
\mu
be an integer-valued random measure on

[0,T+1]\times E
with predictable compensator
\nu
, let
\tau\leq T
be a
stopping time, and let
0\leq\theta\leq 1
be deterministic. Assume

\mathbb{E}\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\theta}\int_{E}\|\zeta\|_{E}^{2}\,\nu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)<\infty.

Then

N_{\tau,\theta}\coloneqq\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\theta}\int_{E}S(\tau+\theta-s)\zeta\,(\mu-\nu)(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta)

satisfies

\mathbb{E}\|N_{\tau,\theta}\|_{E}^{2}\leq C\,\mathbb{E}\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\theta}\int_{E}\|\zeta\|_{E}^{2}\,\nu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta),

with the same universal constant convention as in
Lemma
4.9
.

Proof.

The process

(\omega,s,\zeta)\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau(\omega)<s\leq\tau(\omega)+\theta\}}S(\tau(\omega)+\theta-s)\zeta

is predictable; this follows first for finitely valued stopping times and
then by the standard decreasing approximation of bounded stopping times. The
Hilbert-space isometry for compensated jump integrals gives

\mathbb{E}\|N_{\tau,\theta}\|_{E}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\theta}\int_{E}\|S(\tau+\theta-s)\zeta\|_{E}^{2}\,\nu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\zeta).

Since
S
is a contraction, the right-hand side is bounded by the displayed
expression.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mu^{X^{d}}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)
denote the jump
measure of
X^{d}
and define

\displaystyle\nu^{d}(\omega;\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\coloneqq\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(X_{s-}^{d}(\omega),\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Then
\nu^{d}
is the compensator of
\mu^{X^{d}}
, the compensated jump
martingale from (
4.16
) admits the representation

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{t}^{d}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\xi\,\bigl(\mu^{X^{d}}-\nu^{d}\bigr)(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi),\qquad t\geq 0,

and its predictable quadratic variation is given by

\displaystyle\langle\bar{J}^{d}\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(X_{s-}^{d},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of

d\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr)\leq C\bigl(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\bigr),\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}.

The state-dependent part satisfies the sharper homogeneous estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq C\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}},\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}.

Proof.

The form of the compensator follows directly from the semimartingale
characteristics in Proposition
2.9
. The
representation of
\bar{J}^{d}
is therefore the standard compensated jump
integral representation of the purely discontinuous martingale part in
(
4.16
). The formula for the predictable quadratic
variation is the corresponding Hilbert-space bracket formula for square-
integrable purely discontinuous martingales.

It remains to prove the uniform bound. Since
\mathbf{P}_{d}
is an
orthogonal projection on
\mathcal{H}
, it is contractive, and the projected kernels
inherit the second-moment bounds from Definition
2.2
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,m(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\coloneqq C_{0},

while the state-dependent part is first order in
y
. Indeed, using the
definition of the projected kernel and contractivity of
\mathbf{P}_{d}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{E_{d}^{0}}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\frac{\langle y,\mathbf{P}_{d}\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta)\rangle}{\|\eta\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{E_{d}^{0}}\langle y,\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta)\rangle\leq\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\|\mu(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mu}\bigl(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\bigr),\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+},

for a constant
C_{\mu}
independent of
d
. The penultimate bound is the
sharper homogeneous estimate for the state-dependent part, and combining it
with the
m_{d}
estimate proves the displayed total bound.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
T>0
. Then there exists a constant

C_{T}(x)>0
, independent of
d\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\bar{J}_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C_{T}(x).

Proof.

We keep the decomposition strategy of

[
50
, Lemma 6.1]
, but spell out the estimates in the
present irregular setting. Define

\displaystyle\beta_{d}(y)\coloneqq b_{d}+\Gamma_{d}(y)+\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\cap\{\|\xi\|>1\}}\xi\,\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr),\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}.

By contractivity of
\mathbf{P}_{d}
, boundedness of
\Gamma
, the second-moment bound
for
m_{d}
, and the preceding state-dependent estimate, the large-jump drift

a_{d}(y)\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\cap\{\|\xi\|>1\}}\xi\,\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr)

satisfies

\|a_{d}(y)\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}),\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}.

Indeed, the
m_{d}
-part is bounded by

\int_{\{\|\xi\|>1\}}\|\xi\|\,m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq\int\|\xi\|^{2}\,m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq C
. For the state-dependent part,

\|\xi\|>1
implies
\|\xi\|\leq\|\xi\|^{2}
, and
Lemma
4.11
gives the sharper homogeneous bound

\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq\int_{E_{d}^{0}}\langle y,\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta)\rangle\leq\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\|\mu(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})\|_{\mathcal{H}},

so this part is bounded by
C\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Hence there exists a constant
C_{\beta}>0
, independent of
d
, such that

(4.17)

\displaystyle\|\beta_{d}(y)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq C_{\beta}(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}),\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}.

Step 1: One-time second moments.

Let
h(y)\coloneqq\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
. Dynkin’s formula gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]=\|\mathbf{P}_{d}x\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}I_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right],

where

\displaystyle I_{d}(y)

\displaystyle=2\langle y,b_{d}+L_{d}y+\Gamma_{d}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\quad+2\Big\langle y,\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\cap\{\|\xi\|>1\}}\xi\,\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr)\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr).

The individual terms are estimated as follows. First,

\displaystyle 2\langle y,b_{d}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|b_{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}),

and the Lyapunov part is dissipative:

\displaystyle\langle y,L_{d}y\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle y,Ly\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq 0.

Next,

\displaystyle 2\langle y,\Gamma_{d}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq 2\|\Gamma\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

For the large-jump drift term, Young’s inequality and the large-jump drift
bound above yield

\displaystyle 2\langle y,a_{d}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\|a_{d}(y)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}).

Together with Lemma
4.11
, this gives

\displaystyle I_{d}(y)\leq C_{0}+C_{1}\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2},\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+},

with constants independent of
d
. Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq\|x\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+C_{0}t+C_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{s}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

and Gronwall’s lemma yields

(4.18)

\displaystyle\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]<\infty.

Step 2: Maximal estimate.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{d}(t)\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}\mathcal{T}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}
. Since
(\mathcal{T}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a
contraction semigroup on
\mathcal{H}
, the same holds for
(\mathcal{T}_{d}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
Writing the mild form of (
4.16
), we obtain

\displaystyle X_{t}^{d}=\mathcal{T}_{d}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}x+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}_{d}(t-s)\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+M_{t}^{d,\mathcal{T}},

where

\displaystyle M_{t}^{d,\mathcal{T}}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{T}_{d}(t-s)\xi\,\bigl(\mu^{X^{d}}-\nu^{d}\bigr)(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

Hence, for every
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|X_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq 3\|x\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+3t\int_{0}^{t}\|\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+3\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|M_{r}^{d,\mathcal{T}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

Taking expectations and using (
4.17
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|X_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C(1+\|x\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})+C\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{s}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+3\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|M_{r}^{d,\mathcal{T}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right].

By Lemma
4.9
and the
contractivity of
\mathcal{T}_{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|M_{r}^{d,\mathcal{T}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C_{\mathrm{BDG}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\mathcal{T}_{d}(t-s)\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\nu^{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{BDG}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\nu^{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\right].

Using Lemma
4.11
and
(
4.18
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|M_{r}^{d,\mathcal{T}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C_{t}(x),

with a constant independent of
d
. Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|X_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C_{t}(x),

uniformly in
d
. Finally, applying the same BDG estimate directly to

\bar{J}^{d}
and using Lemma
4.11
once more gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|\bar{J}_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C_{t}(x),

uniformly in
d
. This proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Assume that
x\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
, that
m(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)
and

\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)
are supported on
\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)\setminus\{0\}
, that

\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{1}(H))\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
, that

b,\mu(A)\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
for all
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})
, and that the
trace first-moment bounds

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\Tr(\xi)\,m(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)<\infty,\qquad\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\Tr(\xi)\,\frac{\langle y,\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\rangle}{\|\xi\|^{2}}\leq C_{\mu}\Tr(y),\quad y\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+},

hold for some
C_{\mu}<\infty
. Then for every
T>0
there exists a constant

C_{T}^{\Tr}(x)>0
, independent of
d\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\Tr(X_{t}^{d})\right]\leq C_{T}^{\Tr}(x).

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\displaystyle h_{n}(y)\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle ye_{k},e_{k}\rangle_{H},\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}^{+},

where
(e_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is the eigenbasis of
B
. Fix
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
choose
n\geq d
. Since
X_{t}^{d}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
, we have

h_{n}(X_{t}^{d})=\Tr(X_{t}^{d})
for all
t\geq 0
. Applying Dynkin’s formula to the
linear functional
h_{n}
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\Tr(X_{t}^{d})\right]=\Tr(\mathbf{P}_{d}x)+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}K_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right],

where

\displaystyle K_{d}(y)

\displaystyle=\Tr(b_{d})+h_{n}(L_{d}y)+h_{n}(\Gamma_{d}(y))

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\Tr(\xi)\,\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr).

We estimate the individual terms. Since
T(t)e_{k}=e^{-\lambda_{k}t}e_{k}
,
one has

\displaystyle h_{n}(\mathcal{T}_{d}(t)y)=\sum_{k=1}^{d}e^{-2\lambda_{k}t}\langle ye_{k},e_{k}\rangle_{H}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}\langle ye_{k},e_{k}\rangle_{H}=h_{n}(y),

for every
y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
. Differentiating at
t=0
therefore gives

\displaystyle h_{n}(L_{d}y)\leq 0,\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}.

Next, the restriction of
\Gamma
to
\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
is bounded. To see
this, recall that
(\mathcal{L}_{1}(H),\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)})
is a Banach space and
that the embedding
\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is continuous, with

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)}
. By hypothesis,

\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{1}(H))\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
and
\Gamma\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
is bounded.
Suppose
y_{n}\to y
in
\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
and
\Gamma(y_{n})\to z
in
\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
. Then

y_{n}\to y
and
\Gamma(y_{n})\to z
also in
\mathcal{H}
by continuity of the
embedding, and the boundedness of
\Gamma
on
\mathcal{H}
gives

\Gamma(y_{n})\to\Gamma(y)
in
\mathcal{H}
. Hence
z=\Gamma(y)
, so the graph of

\Gamma\rvert_{\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)}
is closed in
\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)\times\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
. The closed
graph theorem now yields
C_{\Gamma,1}>0
such that

\displaystyle h_{n}(\Gamma_{d}(y))\leq\Tr(\Gamma_{d}(y))\leq C_{\Gamma,1}\Tr(y)=C_{\Gamma,1}h_{n}(y),

for all
y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
. Furthermore, the definition of
b_{d}
includes the
projected compensation drift, and the trace first-moment assumption controls
it uniformly. The positivity of
b
used below follows from admissibility:
for
v\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
the integrand
\langle\chi(\xi),v\rangle
is non-negative,
so the drift condition in Definition
2.2
(ii)
implies
\langle b,v\rangle\geq 0
for all
v\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
; by self-duality of

\mathcal{H}^{+}
,
b\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Hence

\displaystyle\Tr(b_{d})

\displaystyle=\Tr(\mathbf{P}_{d}b)+\int_{\mathbf{P}_{d}(E_{d})}\Tr(\xi)\,m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)

\displaystyle=\Tr(\mathbf{P}_{d}b)+\int_{E_{d}}\Tr(\mathbf{P}_{d}\eta)\,m(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta)

\displaystyle\leq\Tr(b)+\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\Tr(\eta)\,m(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\eta)\coloneqq C_{b}^{\Tr}<\infty.

Here we used positivity of
b
and
\eta
, the compression inequality

\Tr(\mathbf{P}_{d}z)\leq\Tr(z)
for positive trace-class
z
, and the push-forward
definition of
m_{d}
. The trace first-moment assumptions in the lemma imply

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\Tr(\xi)\,m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq C_{m},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\Tr(\xi)\,M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq C_{\mu}\,\Tr(y)=C_{\mu}\,h_{n}(y),

with constants independent of
d
. Altogether,

\displaystyle K_{d}(y)\leq C_{0}+C_{1}\Tr(y),\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+},

for constants
C_{0},C_{1}
independent of
d
. Since

\Tr(\mathbf{P}_{d}x)\leq\Tr(x)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\Tr(X_{t}^{d})\right]\leq\Tr(x)+C_{0}t+C_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\Tr(X_{s}^{d})\right]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Gronwall’s lemma now yields

\displaystyle\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\Tr(X_{t}^{d})\right]\leq C_{T}^{\Tr}(x),

which proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

Assume Assumption
Assumption

C

. Let

x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
,
T>0
and
\delta\in(0,T]
. Then there exists a constant

C_{T,\delta}(x)>0
, independent of
d\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{\delta\leq t\leq T}\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]\leq C_{T,\delta}(x).

Proof.

We first prove the estimate for initial data already in

\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Work in the equivalent adapted norm

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
introduced in
Lemma
4.2
. By norm equivalence,
contractivity of
\mathbf{P}_{d}
in
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
, and
Assumption
Assumption

C

, the projected kernels satisfy

\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\,m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq C

and

\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\,M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}),\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}.

Since the left-hand side in the second display is homogeneous and linear in

y
, applying the estimate to
y/\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
when
y\neq 0
gives the
sharper homogeneous bound

(4.19)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\,M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\leq C\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B},\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+},

with the convention that the left-hand side is zero at
y=0
.
Consequently, the large-jump drift

a_{d}^{\mathcal{V}}(y)\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\cap\{\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}>1\}}\xi\,\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr)

has linear growth in the adapted norm:

(4.20)

\displaystyle\|a_{d}^{\mathcal{V}}(y)\|_{\mathcal{V},B}\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}),\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}.

Indeed, on
\{\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}>1\}
the continuous embedding

\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
implies

\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{V},B}\leq C\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}
after changing constants; the

m_{d}
-part is therefore bounded by the
\mathcal{V}
second moment, and the

M_{d}
-part by (
4.19
). For

q_{d}(y)\coloneqq\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}
,
y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
, the finite-dimensional generator
satisfies the uniform growth estimate

(4.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{d}q_{d}(y)+\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr)\leq C\bigl(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\bigr),

with a constant independent of
d
. To see this, expand

y=\sum y_{i,j}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}
on
\mathcal{H}_{d}
. The Lyapunov part is dissipative:

2\langle L_{d}y,y\rangle_{\mathcal{V},B}=-2\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq d}\lambda_{i,j}(1+\lambda_{i,j})|y_{i,j}|^{2}\leq 0.

The projection
\mathbf{P}_{d}
is contractive in
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
, so

\sup_{d}\|b_{d}\|_{\mathcal{V},B}<\infty
. Boundedness of

\Gamma:\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}
gives

\|\Gamma_{d}(y)\|_{\mathcal{V},B}\leq C\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
. Hence Cauchy–Schwarz yields

2\langle y,b_{d}+\Gamma_{d}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{V},B}\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}).

For the large-jump drift term, Young’s inequality and
(
4.20
) give

2\langle y,a_{d}^{\mathcal{V}}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{V},B}\leq\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}+\|a_{d}^{\mathcal{V}}(y)\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}).

The quadratic jump term is controlled by Assumption

Assumption

C

, transferred to

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
by norm equivalence, together with the weaker form of
(
4.19
). Thus

\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi))\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}),

which proves (
4.21
). Dynkin’s formula and
Gronwall’s lemma give the corresponding pointwise estimate

\sup_{d}\sup_{t\leq T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{y}^{d}}\left[\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\right]\leq C_{T}(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}).

We now prove the maximal estimate. Write the finite-variation drift as

\beta_{d}(z)\coloneqq b_{d}+\Gamma_{d}(z)+\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\cap\{\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}>1\}}\xi\,\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(z,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr).

The large-jump drift estimate (
4.20
), together with
boundedness of
b_{d}
and
\Gamma_{d}
in the adapted norm, gives

\|\beta_{d}(z)\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\leq C\bigl(1+\|z\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\bigr),\qquad z\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+},

with
C
independent of
d
. For
y\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
define

F_{d}(t)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{y}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq r\leq t}\|X_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\right],\qquad 0\leq t\leq T.

The mild representation in the finite-dimensional Hilbert space

(\mathcal{H}_{d},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V},B})
is

X_{r}^{d}=\mathcal{T}_{d}(r)y+\int_{0}^{r}\mathcal{T}_{d}(r-s)\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+M_{r}^{d,\mathcal{T}},

where

M_{r}^{d,\mathcal{T}}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{T}_{d}(r-s)\xi\,(\mu^{X^{d}}-\nu^{d})(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

Since
\mathcal{T}_{d}
is contractive in
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
,

\sup_{r\leq t}\|\mathcal{T}_{d}(r)y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\leq\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}.

For the finite-variation term, Jensen’s inequality and the drift-growth bound
yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{y}^{d}}\left[\sup_{r\leq t}\left\|\int_{0}^{r}\mathcal{T}_{d}(r-s)\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq t\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{y}^{d}}\left[\|\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\right]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(1+F_{d}(s)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

For the martingale convolution, Lemma
4.9

gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{y}^{d}}\left[\sup_{r\leq t}\|M_{r}^{d,\mathcal{T}}\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{y}^{d}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\bigl(m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)+M_{d}(X_{s}^{d},\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(1+F_{d}(s)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

by (
4.21
). Combining the three estimates gives

F_{d}(t)\leq C\bigl(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\bigr)+C\int_{0}^{t}F_{d}(s)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\qquad 0\leq t\leq T,

with constants independent of
d
. Gronwall’s lemma yields, whenever the
initial datum is in
\mathcal{V}
,

(4.22)

\displaystyle\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{y}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\right]\leq C_{T}\bigl(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V},B}^{2}\bigr),\qquad y\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}.

By equivalence of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V},B}
, the same
estimate holds with
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
, after changing
C_{T}
.

For a general
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
we do not bound

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{\delta}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]
pointwise directly; we first
establish a time-averaged
\mathcal{V}
estimate and then restart the process at a
positive time. We claim that for every
\delta\in(0,T]
,

(4.23)

\displaystyle\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{s}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\leq C_{\delta}(x)<\infty.

The mechanism is the elementary spectral identity: for every Lyapunov mode

\lambda_{i,j}\geq 0
and every
\tau\in[0,\delta]
,

(4.24)

\displaystyle(1+\lambda_{i,j})\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-2\lambda_{i,j}\sigma}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\sigma=(1+\lambda_{i,j})\,\frac{1-\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-2\lambda_{i,j}\tau}}{2\lambda_{i,j}}\leq\tau+\tfrac{1}{2}\leq\delta+\tfrac{1}{2},

where the left-hand side is read as
\tau
when
\lambda_{i,j}=0
. The factor

1+\lambda_{i,j}
is exactly the
\mathcal{V}
-weight of (
4.2
),
and (
4.24
) shows that integrating in time trades it
for a bounded constant; this is the precise sense in which the heat smoothing
absorbs the time singularity.

We use the finite-rank mild representation, with
\mathcal{T}_{d}(t)\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}\mathcal{T}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}
,

X_{t}^{d}=\mathcal{T}_{d}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}x+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}_{d}(t-s)\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+M_{t}^{d,\mathcal{T}},\qquad M_{t}^{d,\mathcal{T}}=\int_{0}^{t}\!\!\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{T}_{d}(t-s)\xi\,(\mu^{X^{d}}-\nu^{d})(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

Deterministic term.
By (
4.2
) and
(
4.24
),

\int_{0}^{\delta}\|\mathcal{T}_{d}(s)\mathbf{P}_{d}x\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\leq C_{3}\sum_{i\leq j}(1+\lambda_{i,j})|\langle\mathbf{P}_{d}x,\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\rangle|^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-2\lambda_{i,j}s}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\leq C_{3}\bigl(\delta+\tfrac{1}{2}\bigr)\|x\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

Martingale term.
By the Itô isometry for the compensated jump
integral, Tonelli’s theorem, (
4.2
) and
(
4.24
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|M_{s}^{d,\mathcal{T}}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\int_{0}^{\delta}\!\!\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\Bigl(\int_{r}^{\delta}\|\mathcal{T}_{d}(s-r)\xi\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\Bigr)\nu^{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\bigl(\delta+\tfrac{1}{2}\bigr)\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\int_{0}^{\delta}\!\!\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\nu^{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\bigl(\delta+\tfrac{1}{2}\bigr)\int_{0}^{\delta}\bigl(C_{0}+C_{1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\bigr)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r,

which is finite uniformly in
d
by
Lemma
4.11
and
Lemma
4.12
. This step uses only the

\mathcal{H}
jump second moment, hence is valid in the state-dependent case

\mu\neq 0
as well.

Drift term.
Set
A_{\delta}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\delta}C(1+\sigma^{-1/2})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\sigma=C(\delta+2\sqrt{\delta})
. Using
\|\mathcal{T}(r)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}\leq C(1+r^{-1/2})
,
the growth bound (
4.17
), Cauchy–Schwarz in
r
, and the

\mathcal{H}
-moment bound,

\int_{0}^{\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\Bigl\|\int_{0}^{s}\mathcal{T}_{d}(s-r)\beta_{d}(X_{r}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r\Bigr\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\leq A_{\delta}^{2}\,\delta\,\sup_{r\leq\delta}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|\beta_{d}(X_{r}^{d})\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]<\infty

uniformly in
d
. Summing the three contributions
proves (
4.23
).

It follows, applying (
4.23
) on
[\delta/2,\delta]
, that for
every
d
there is a deterministic time
s_{d}\in[\delta/2,\delta]
with

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{s_{d}}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2}{\delta}\,C_{\delta/2}(x)\coloneqq K_{\delta}(x),

uniformly in
d
; in particular
X_{s_{d}}^{d}\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}

\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}
-almost surely. Restarting the finite-rank Markov process at the
deterministic time
s_{d}\leq\delta
and using the
\mathcal{V}
-initial-datum maximal
estimate (
4.22
) together with the Markov property,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{\delta\leq t\leq T}\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{s_{d}\leq t\leq T}\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\Psi_{d}(X_{s_{d}}^{d})\right]\leq C_{T}\bigl(1+K_{\delta}(x)\bigr)\coloneqq C_{T,\delta}(x),

where
\Psi_{d}(y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{y}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]\leq C_{T}(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2})
by (
4.22
). This is the
assertion of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Assume Assumption
Assumption

C

. For every

x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
the sequence
(\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
of laws of

(X^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
is a tight sequence of measures on

\mathcal{B}(D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+}))
.

Proof.

We use Aldous’ criterion on
D([0,T],\mathcal{H}^{+})
for each fixed
T>0
. The state
space is the closed cone of the separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. We write

\mathcal{T}_{d}(t)=\mathbf{P}_{d}\mathcal{T}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}
and use the mild form

X_{t}^{d}=\mathcal{T}_{d}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}x+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}_{d}(t-s)\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+M_{t}^{d,\mathcal{T}},

where
\beta_{d}
is defined in (
4.17
) and

M_{t}^{d,\mathcal{T}}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{T}_{d}(t-s)\xi\,(\mu^{X^{d}}-\nu^{d})(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

Step 1: Compact containment.

We use the standard compactness criterion in Hilbert spaces: a family is
relatively compact if it is bounded and its tails with respect to the finite-rank
orthogonal projections
\mathbf{P}_{N}
vanish uniformly. The maximal

\mathcal{H}
-moment estimate in Lemma
4.12

provides the boundedness. It remains to control the spectral tails.

Fix
\alpha>0
. On every interval
[\delta,T]
with
\delta>0
,
Lemma
4.14
and compactness of

\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
imply

(4.25)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\!\left(\sup_{\delta\leq s\leq T}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}X_{s}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}>\alpha\right)=0.

Indeed

\|\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}z\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}\|z\|_{\mathcal{V}}
and

\|\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}\to 0
. This compactness follows from the
spectral Hilbert-scale compatibility in
Definition
2.2
(iv)(c), since

\lambda_{i}\to\infty
makes omitted spectral tails vanish uniformly on bounded
subsets of
\mathcal{V}
.

On the short interval
[0,\delta]
we work in
\mathcal{H}
and keep the Lyapunov part
inside the semigroup. The deterministic term satisfies

\sup_{0\leq s\leq\delta}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}_{d}(s)\mathbf{P}_{d}x\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}x\|_{\mathcal{H}},

because the spectral projections commute with
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}
is contractive.
For the drift convolution, contraction, Jensen’s inequality,
(
4.17
), and
Lemma
4.12
give

\sup_{d}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq s\leq\delta}\left\|\int_{0}^{s}\mathcal{T}_{d}(s-r)\beta_{d}(X_{r}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C\delta^{2}.

For the martingale convolution, Lemma
4.9
,
Lemma
4.11
, and the same moment estimate give

\sup_{d}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq s\leq\delta}\|M_{s}^{d,\mathcal{T}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C\delta.

Consequently,

(4.26)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\!\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq\delta}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}X_{s}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}>\alpha\right)=0.

We now make the diagonal compact set explicit. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. By
Lemma
4.12
, choose
R<\infty
such that

\sup_{d}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\!\left(\sup_{s\leq T}\|X_{s}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}>R\right)\leq\varepsilon/2.

Let
\alpha_{k}=2^{-k}
,
k\geq 1
. For each
k
, first choose

\delta_{k}\in(0,T]
small enough and then choose
N_{k}
recursively increasing so
that (
4.26
) and
(
4.25
) give

\sup_{d}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\!\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{k}}^{\perp}X_{s}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}>\alpha_{k}\right)\leq\varepsilon\,2^{-k-1}.

Indeed, the probability on
[0,T]
is bounded by the sum of the probabilities
on
[0,\delta_{k}]
and
[\delta_{k},T]
, and the two preceding tail estimates make
these terms arbitrarily small by first choosing
\delta_{k}
and then
N_{k}
.
Define

K\coloneqq\left\{z\in\mathcal{H}^{+}:\|z\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq R,\ \|\mathbf{P}_{N_{k}}^{\perp}z\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\alpha_{k}\text{ for all }k\geq 1\right\}.

The set
K
is closed. It is compact in
\mathcal{H}
by the Hilbert compactness
criterion: it is bounded, and for every
\eta>0
one can choose
k
with

\alpha_{k}<\eta
, after which all elements of
K
have spectral tail beyond the
finite-dimensional space
\mathcal{H}_{N_{k}}
bounded by
\eta
. Consequently,

\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\bigl(X_{s}^{d}\notin K\text{ for some }s\in[0,T]\bigr)\leq\varepsilon/2+\sum_{k\geq 1}\varepsilon\,2^{-k-1}\leq\varepsilon.

This proves compact containment.

Step 2: Aldous’ increment condition.

Let
(\tau_{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
be stopping times bounded by
T
and let

\theta_{d}\downarrow 0
. The mild representation gives

\displaystyle X_{\tau_{d}+\theta_{d}}^{d}-X_{\tau_{d}}^{d}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{T}_{d}(\theta_{d})-I)X_{\tau_{d}}^{d}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\tau_{d}}^{\tau_{d}+\theta_{d}}\mathcal{T}_{d}(\tau_{d}+\theta_{d}-s)\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+N_{d}(\tau_{d},\theta_{d}),

where

N_{d}(\tau,\theta)\coloneqq\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\theta}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{T}_{d}(\tau+\theta-s)\xi\,(\mu^{X^{d}}-\nu^{d})(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

For the semigroup term, compact containment from Step 1 and uniform strong
continuity of
\mathcal{T}(h)
on compact subsets of
\mathcal{H}
imply

\lim_{\theta\downarrow 0}\sup_{d}\sup_{\tau_{d}\leq T}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\!\left(\|(\mathcal{T}_{d}(\theta)-I)X_{\tau_{d}}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}>\alpha\right)=0.

For the drift term, using contraction and the supremum moment estimate,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\left\|\int_{\tau_{d}}^{\tau_{d}+\theta_{d}}\mathcal{T}_{d}(\tau_{d}+\theta_{d}-s)\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C\theta_{d}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[1+\sup_{r\leq T+1}\|X_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C\theta_{d}^{2}.

For the compensated jump convolution,
Corollary
4.10
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|N_{d}(\tau_{d},\theta_{d})\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\int_{\tau_{d}}^{\tau_{d}+\theta_{d}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\nu^{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\theta_{d}\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[1+\sup_{r\leq T+1}\|X_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C\theta_{d}.

Chebyshev’s inequality yields Aldous’ increment condition. Since
T>0
was
arbitrary, the sequence is tight on
D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})
.
∎

Proposition 4.16
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0}\coloneqq\lin\{f_{u}\colon f_{u}(y)\coloneqq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle},\ u\in\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}\}.

Let
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
and let
\mathbb{P}
be a weak limit point of the sequence

(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then
\mathbb{P}
solves the martingale problem for

(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{0})
on
D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})
, where on the exponential generators

f_{u}(y)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle}
with
u\in\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
the
operator
\mathcal{G}
is given pointwise by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\cdot,u\rangle}(y)\coloneqq\bigl(-F(u)-\langle y,R(u)\rangle\bigr)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle},\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}^{+},\ u\in\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}.

The restriction
u\in\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
is essential:
R(u)=L(u)+\hat{R}(u)

involves the unbounded Lyapunov operator
L
and is therefore not defined for
arbitrary
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The class
\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}^{+}
:
for every
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, the spectral truncation
\mathbf{P}_{d}(u)
lies in

\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\subseteq\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
and converges to
u
in
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

This is the irregular analogue of

[
50
, Proposition 6.3]
, restricted to test loadings
on which the limiting generator
\mathcal{G}
is well defined. By linearity it
suffices to verify the martingale property on the generators

f_{u}(y)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle}
for fixed
u\in\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Set

u_{d}\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}(u)\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\subseteq\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
and

f_{u}^{d}(y)\coloneqq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u_{d}\rangle}
. By
Proposition
2.9
, the process

\displaystyle M_{t}^{d,u}\coloneqq f_{u}^{d}(X_{t}^{d})-f_{u}^{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}(x))-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}^{d}f_{u}^{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

is a martingale under
\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}
. We pass to the limit only on this
restricted test domain, where both
L(u)
and
L(u_{d})\to L(u)
in
\mathcal{H}

are defined (the latter by spectral truncation: if

u=\sum_{i\leq j}u_{i,j}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\in\dom(L)
, then

L(u_{d})=-\sum_{i\leq j\leq d}\lambda_{i,j}u_{i,j}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\to L(u)
in

\mathcal{H}
). Combined with the continuity of
F
and
\hat{R}
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}

(
[
22
, Remark 3.4]
), this gives

\displaystyle f_{u}^{d}\longrightarrow f_{u},\qquad\mathcal{G}^{d}f_{u}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}f_{u},

locally uniformly on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The generator terms have linear growth, so we
add the uniform-integrability step explicitly. Since

F_{d}(u_{d})=F(u_{d})\to F(u)
and

R_{d}(u_{d})=L(u_{d})+\mathbf{P}_{d}\hat{R}(u_{d})\to L(u)+\hat{R}(u)=R(u)
in
\mathcal{H}
, there is
a constant
C_{u}<\infty
such that, for all
d
