and all
y\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
,

|\mathcal{G}^{d}f_{u}^{d}(y)|+|\mathcal{G}f_{u}(y)|\leq C_{u}(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}).

Indeed,
0\leq f_{u}^{d},f_{u}\leq 1
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and the only unbounded term is the
linear pairing with
y
. Lemma
4.12

implies, in fact,

\sup_{d}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}(1+\|X_{s}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right]<\infty,\qquad T<\infty.

The same estimate is inherited by any weak limit by Fatou’s lemma, first for
bounded continuous truncations and then by monotone convergence.

Let
\rho_{R}\colon\mathcal{H}^{+}\to[0,1]
be continuous with
\rho_{R}(y)=1
for

\|y\|\leq R
and
\rho_{R}(y)=0
for
\|y\|\geq R+1
. The locally uniform
convergence above gives convergence of the bounded continuous functions

\rho_{R}\mathcal{G}^{d}f_{u}^{d}
to
\rho_{R}\mathcal{G}f_{u}
. The corresponding time-integral
functionals are continuous at paths except possibly at their jump times, which
have zero Lebesgue measure for every càdlàg path. Hence they pass to the
weak limit. The error on
\{\|y\|>R\}
is bounded uniformly by the displayed
linear-growth estimate and

(1+\|y\|)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|y\|>R\}}\leq R^{-1}(1+\|y\|^{2})
, and therefore tends
to zero as
R\to\infty
. Thus the hypotheses of

[
32
, Chapter 4, Lemma 5.1]
are satisfied, and we conclude that

\displaystyle f_{u}(X_{t})-f_{u}(x)-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}f_{u}(X_{s})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

is a martingale under
\mathbb{P}
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is the linear span of these
generators, the same holds for every test function in
\mathcal{D}_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 4.17

(No fixed-time jumps of weak limits)
.

Assume Assumption
Assumption

C

. Let
\mathbb{P}_{x}

be any weak limit point of
(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
on

D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})
. Then, for every deterministic
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}_{x}(\Delta X_{t}=0)=1,

where
\Delta X_{t}=X_{t}-X_{t-}
for
t>0
and
\Delta X_{0}=0
.
Consequently, the coordinate map
\omega\mapsto\omega(t)
is

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. continuous on
D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})
.

Proof.

The assertion is trivial at
t=0
by convention. Fix a deterministic

t>0
, choose
T>t+1
, and let
\eta>0
. It is enough to prove the
deterministic-time left and right oscillation estimates

(4.27)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\limsup_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\!\left(\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq h}\|X_{t+\theta}^{d}-X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}>\eta\right)=0,

and

(4.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\limsup_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\!\left(\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq h}\|X_{t}^{d}-X_{t-\theta}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}>\eta\right)=0.

We prove the right estimate; the left estimate is identical with

[t,t+h]
replaced by
[t-h,t]
. Let
h<t/2
. The mild representation gives,
for
0\leq\theta\leq h
,

X_{t+\theta}^{d}-X_{t}^{d}=(\mathcal{T}_{d}(\theta)-I)X_{t}^{d}+\int_{t}^{t+\theta}\mathcal{T}_{d}(t+\theta-s)\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+N_{\theta}^{d,h},

where

N_{\theta}^{d,h}\coloneqq\int_{t}^{t+\theta}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{T}_{d}(t+\theta-s)\xi\,(\mu^{X^{d}}-\nu^{d})(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi).

First consider the semigroup term. By Lemma
4.14
,

\sup_{d}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{t/2\leq r\leq T}\|X_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right]<\infty.

Hence, for every
\varepsilon>0
, we can choose
R
such that

\sup_{d}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\!\left(\sup_{t/2\leq r\leq T}\|X_{r}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{V}}>R\right)<\varepsilon.

The set
K_{R}=\{z\in\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}:\|z\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq R\}
is compact in

\mathcal{H}
, and strong continuity of
(\mathcal{T}(\theta))_{\theta\geq 0}
is uniform on
compact subsets. Since
\mathcal{T}_{d}(\theta)z=\mathcal{T}(\theta)z
for

z\in\mathcal{H}_{d}
, it follows that

\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\sup_{d}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\!\left(\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq h}\|(\mathcal{T}_{d}(\theta)-I)X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}>\eta\right)=0.

For the drift contribution, (
4.17
) and
Lemma
4.12
yield

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq h}\left\|\int_{t}^{t+\theta}\mathcal{T}_{d}(t+\theta-s)\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq h\int_{t}^{t+h}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|\beta_{d}(X_{s}^{d})\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s\leq Ch^{2},

uniformly in
d
. For the compensated jump convolution,
Lemma
4.9
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq h}\|N_{\theta}^{d,h}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\leq C\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\int_{t}^{t+h}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\nu^{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\right]\leq Ch,

where the last inequality follows from
Lemma
4.11
and
Lemma
4.12
. Chebyshev’s inequality gives
(
4.27
); the same argument on
[t-h,t]
gives
(
4.28
).

The deterministic-time oscillation criterion for weak convergence in the
Skorohod topology (Ethier–Kurtz
[
32
, Chapter 3, Section 7]
) now
implies that every weak limit has zero jump at the fixed time
t
. Since

t>0
was arbitrary, the assertion holds for all deterministic times. The last
statement is the usual characterization of continuity points of the coordinate
projection on Skorohod space.
∎

Lemma 4.18

(Riccati stability for moving Galerkin loadings)
.

Let
w_{d}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
and
w\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
with

\|w_{d}-w\|_{\mathcal{H}}\to 0
. Then, for every
T<\infty
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(|\phi_{d}(t,w_{d})-\phi(t,w)|+\|\psi_{d}(t,w_{d})-\psi(t,w)\|\right)\to 0.

Proof.

The proof is the same Gronwall argument as in
Proposition
2.8
, with the initial loadings
allowed to vary. Since
w_{d}\to w
, the family
\{w_{d}\}_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\cup\{w\}
is
bounded in
\mathcal{H}
. The local growth estimates for the mild Riccati equations
therefore give a constant
M_{T}
such that

\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\leq T}\|\psi_{d}(t,w_{d})\|+\sup_{t\leq T}\|\psi(t,w)\|\leq M_{T}.

Let
L_{T}
be a Lipschitz constant of
\hat{R}
on the ball

\{z\in\mathcal{H}^{+}:\|z\|\leq M_{T}\}
. Subtracting the mild equations and using

\mathbf{P}_{d}\mathcal{T}(t)=\mathcal{T}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}
gives

\displaystyle\|\psi_{d}(t,w_{d})-\psi(t,w)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{T}(t)(w_{d}-w)\|

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\mathcal{T}(t-s)[\hat{R}(\psi_{d}(s,w_{d}))-\hat{R}(\psi(s,w))]\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{R}(\psi(s,w))\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\|w_{d}-w\|+L_{T}\int_{0}^{t}\|\psi_{d}(s,w_{d})-\psi(s,w)\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+a_{d}(t),

where

a_{d}(t)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{R}(\psi(s,w))\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

The map
(t,s)\mapsto\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{R}(\psi(s,w))
has compact range in

\mathcal{H}
on the triangle
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
, and

\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\to 0
strongly; hence
\sup_{t\leq T}a_{d}(t)\to 0
. Gronwall’s lemma
yields
\sup_{t\leq T}\|\psi_{d}(t,w_{d})-\psi(t,w)\|\to 0
. Finally,

|\phi_{d}(t,w_{d})-\phi(t,w)|\leq\int_{0}^{t}|F(\psi_{d}(s,w_{d}))-F(\psi(s,w))|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

and the continuity of
F
on bounded subsets of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
gives the
claimed uniform convergence of
\phi_{d}
as well.
∎

Lemma 4.19

(Riccati semiflow)
.

For all
s,t\geq 0
and
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
,

\psi(t+s,u)=\psi(t,\psi(s,u)),\qquad\phi(t+s,u)=\phi(s,u)+\phi(t,\psi(s,u)).

Equivalently, since the equation is autonomous,

\psi(t+s,u)=\psi(s,\psi(t,u)),\qquad\phi(t+s,u)=\phi(t,u)+\phi(s,\psi(t,u)).

Proof.

Fix
s\geq 0
and set

\tilde{\psi}(t)\coloneqq\psi(t,\psi(s,u))
and

\tilde{\phi}(t)\coloneqq\phi(s,u)+\phi(t,\psi(s,u))
. By the
variation-of-constants formula and the semigroup property of
\mathcal{T}
,

(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\psi})
solves the same mild Riccati system on

[0,\infty)
as
(\phi(t+s,u),\psi(t+s,u))
, with the same initial value at

t=0
. Uniqueness in Proposition
2.8
gives the
identities.
∎

Lemma 4.20

(Laplace transforms determine laws on the cone)
.

Let
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
be Borel probability measures on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. If

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle}\,\nu_{1}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y)=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle}\,\nu_{2}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y),\qquad u\in\mathcal{H}^{+},

then
\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}
. The same conclusion holds on product cones

(\mathcal{H}^{+})^{n}
: the functions

(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\mapsto\exp\!\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle y_{k},u_{k}\rangle\right),\qquad u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{H}^{+},

are measure determining.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{H}
is a separable Hilbert space and
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is closed,

\mathcal{H}^{+}
is Polish. The cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is self-dual and generating in the
Hilbert space of self-adjoint Hilbert–Schmidt operators: if

z=z^{+}-z^{-}
is the spectral decomposition into positive and negative parts,
then
z^{\pm}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
z^{+}z^{-}=0
. Hence positive loadings separate
points of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
: if
\langle x-y,u\rangle=0
for all
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, then
testing with the positive and negative parts of
x-y
gives
x=y
.

Choose a countable dense set
(u_{m})_{m\geq 1}
in
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. By continuity of
the pairings, this countable family still separates points. Moreover, the
Borel
\sigma
-field of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is generated by the coordinates

y\mapsto\langle y,u_{m}\rangle
: rational linear combinations of the

u_{m}
generate a countable weakly separating family, and on a separable
Hilbert space the weak and norm Borel
\sigma
-fields coincide.

For any finite set
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}
and any

\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\geq 0
, equality of the cone Laplace transforms gives
equality of the finite-dimensional Laplace transforms

\int\exp\!\left(-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}\langle y,u_{m_{j}}\rangle\right)\nu_{1}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y)=\int\exp\!\left(-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}\langle y,u_{m_{j}}\rangle\right)\nu_{2}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y),

because
\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}u_{m_{j}}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Classical uniqueness of
Laplace transforms on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{k}
therefore identifies all finite-dimensional
distributions of the coordinate map

y\mapsto(\langle y,u_{m}\rangle)_{m\geq 1}
. Since these coordinates generate the
Borel
\sigma
-field,
\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}
. Applying the same argument to the product
cone
(\mathcal{H}^{+})^{n}
proves the final assertion.
∎

Lemma 4.21

(Finite-dimensional Laplace identification)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
2.10
. Let

0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n}
,
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. For
each
d\in\mathbb{N}
define recursively

v_{n}^{d}\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{n},\qquad a_{n}^{d}\coloneqq 0,

and, for
k=n-1,\ldots,1
,

v_{k}^{d}\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{k}+\psi_{d}(t_{k+1}-t_{k},v_{k+1}^{d}),\qquad a_{k}^{d}\coloneqq a_{k+1}^{d}+\phi_{d}(t_{k+1}-t_{k},v_{k+1}^{d}).

Define
(v_{k},a_{k})
by the same recursion with
\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{k}
,

\phi_{d}
, and
\psi_{d}
replaced by
u_{k}
,
\phi
, and
\psi
. Then

(4.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\exp\!\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle X_{t_{k}}^{d},\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{k}\rangle\right)\right]=\exp\!\left(-a_{1}^{d}-\phi_{d}(t_{1},v_{1}^{d})-\langle\mathbf{P}_{d}x,\psi_{d}(t_{1},v_{1}^{d})\rangle\right),

and the right-hand side converges to

(4.30)

\displaystyle\exp\!\left(-a_{1}-\phi(t_{1},v_{1})-\langle x,\psi(t_{1},v_{1})\rangle\right).

Proof.

The identity (
4.29
) follows by iterating
the finite-rank affine Markov property from
Proposition
2.9
(i), starting at time
t_{n}
and
moving backwards. Since
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is a cone and the Riccati flow preserves

\mathcal{H}^{+}
, all recursively defined loadings stay in
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The convergence

v_{k}^{d}\to v_{k}
and
a_{k}^{d}\to a_{k}
follows by backward induction. The claim is
immediate for
k=n
because
\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{n}\to u_{n}
. If

v_{k+1}^{d}\to v_{k+1}
and
a_{k+1}^{d}\to a_{k+1}
, then
Lemma
4.18
, applied at the time

t_{k+1}-t_{k}
, gives

\psi_{d}(t_{k+1}-t_{k},v_{k+1}^{d})\to\psi(t_{k+1}-t_{k},v_{k+1})
and

\phi_{d}(t_{k+1}-t_{k},v_{k+1}^{d})\to\phi(t_{k+1}-t_{k},v_{k+1})
. Since
\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{k}\to u_{k}
, the recursive
definitions imply
v_{k}^{d}\to v_{k}
and
a_{k}^{d}\to a_{k}
. Substituting these
convergences in (
4.29
) gives
(
4.30
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.10
.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. By Lemma
4.15
, the family

(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight on
D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})
. Let
\mathbb{P}
be any weak
limit point. We identify
\mathbb{P}
directly through the finite-rank affine
transform formula; this avoids applying the limiting unbounded generator

\mathcal{G}
to time-dependent test functions
g_{T,u}(t,\cdot)
whose loadings

\psi(T-t,u)
need not lie in
\dom(L)
.

Step 1: bounded convergence on the marginal transform.

Fix
t\geq 0
and
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Set
u_{d}\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{d}(u)\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
; then

u_{d}\to u
in
\mathcal{H}
as
d\to\infty
and

|\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t}^{d},u_{d}\rangle}|\leq 1
uniformly in
d
. By
Proposition
2.9
(i) the finite-rank affine
transform formula reads

(4.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t}^{d},u_{d}\rangle}\right]=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\phi_{d}(t,u_{d})-\langle\mathbf{P}_{d}(x),\psi_{d}(t,u_{d})\rangle}.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{x}
denote any weak limit point of
(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}

(along a subsequence). By Lemma
4.15
such a limit
exists; the goal of this step is to compute its marginal Laplace transform.
For
t=0
the assertion follows directly from
\mathbf{P}_{d}x\to x
, so assume

t>0
. By Lemma
4.17
, the coordinate map

\omega\mapsto\omega(t)
is
\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. continuous. Hence the
continuous-mapping theorem gives

X_{t}^{d}\Rightarrow X_{t}\quad\text{under }\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}\Rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{x}.

The remaining issue is that the loading also varies with
d
. Since

a,b\geq 0
imply
|\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-a}-\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-b}|\leq|a-b|
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t}^{d},u_{d}\rangle}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t},u\rangle}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\left|\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t}^{d},u_{d}\rangle}-\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t}^{d},u\rangle}\right|\right]+\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t}^{d},u\rangle}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t},u\rangle}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\|u_{d}-u\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\|X_{t}^{d}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right]+\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t}^{d},u\rangle}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t},u\rangle}\right]\right|.

The first term tends to zero by
u_{d}\to u
and
Lemma
4.12
; the second tends to zero by
the continuous-mapping theorem applied to the bounded continuous function

y\mapsto\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle}
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Thus

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t}^{d},u_{d}\rangle}\right]\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t},u\rangle}\right]
.

Step 2: Riccati limit on the restricted class.

For
u\in\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
, Proposition
2.8

gives

\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\Big(|\phi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}u)-\phi(s,u)|+\|\psi_{d}(s,\mathbf{P}_{d}u)-\psi(s,u)\|\Big)\to 0.

Hence the right-hand side of (
4.31
)
converges to
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\phi(t,u)-\langle x,\psi(t,u)\rangle}
. Combining
Steps 1 and 2,

(4.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t},u\rangle}\right]=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\phi(t,u)-\langle x,\psi(t,u)\rangle},\qquad u\in\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}.

Step 3: extension by density.

For any
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, the spectral truncations
u_{n}\coloneqq\mathbf{P}_{n}(u)
satisfy

u_{n}\in\dom(L)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
u_{n}\to u
in
\mathcal{H}
. By
Lemma
4.6
,
\phi(t,u_{n})\to\phi(t,u)
and

\psi(t,u_{n})\to\psi(t,u)
in
\mathcal{H}
; hence both sides
of (
4.32
) are continuous in
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
(the
left side by bounded convergence,
|\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t},u_{n}\rangle}|\leq 1

and
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u_{n}\rangle}\to\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle}
pointwise).
The identity (
4.32
) therefore extends to every

u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, proving the affine transform
formula (
2.25
).

Step 4: finite-dimensional distributions and uniqueness.

The one-time identity is not by itself enough to determine a path law. We
therefore use Lemma
4.21
. Let

0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n}
and
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Along any weakly convergent
subsequence, Lemma
4.17
makes the coordinate map

\omega\mapsto(\omega(t_{1}),\ldots,\omega(t_{n}))

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. continuous.
Together with the uniform second-moment bound and the replacement

\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{k}\to u_{k}
, the continuous-mapping theorem gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\exp\!\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle X_{t_{k}}^{d},\mathbf{P}_{d}u_{k}\rangle\right)\right]\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[\exp\!\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle X_{t_{k}},u_{k}\rangle\right)\right]

for arbitrary deterministic times
0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n}
. The right-hand
side is then identified by (
4.30
). The class

\left\{\exp\!\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle y_{k},u_{k}\rangle\right):u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}\right\}

is multiplicatively closed and separates points on

(\mathcal{H}^{+})^{n}
. By Lemma
4.20
, the
finite-dimensional distributions of every weak limit point are uniquely
determined.

It remains to make the Markov-affine structure explicit. For
t\geq 0
and

x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, let
p_{t}(x,\cdot)
denote the unique probability measure on

\mathcal{H}^{+}
determined by

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle}\,p_{t}(x,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\phi(t,u)-\langle x,\psi(t,u)\rangle},\qquad u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}.

Existence follows from the one-time weak limit above (with initial
state
x
), and uniqueness follows from
Lemma
4.20
. This family is a Borel
transition kernel. Indeed,
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is a closed subset of the separable
Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, hence is Polish. Choose a countable dense subset

D\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{+}
and set
e_{u}(y)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,u\rangle}
for

u\in D
. The map

\Theta:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}^{+})\to[0,1]^{D},\qquad\Theta(\nu)=\left(\int e_{u}(y)\,\nu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y)\right)_{u\in D},

is Borel and injective: density of
D
and continuity in
u
recover the
Laplace transform for all
u\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and
Lemma
4.20
then identifies the measure. By
the Lusin–Souslin theorem,
\Theta^{-1}
is Borel on
\Theta(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}^{+}))
. Since

x\mapsto\Theta(p_{t}(x,\cdot))=\left(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\phi(t,u)-\langle x,\psi(t,u)\rangle}\right)_{u\in D}

is Borel, the map
x\mapsto p_{t}(x,\cdot)
is Borel as a map into

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}^{+})
.
By
Lemma
4.19
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle z,u\rangle}\,p_{t}(y,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}z)\,p_{s}(x,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\phi(t,u)}\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle y,\psi(t,u)\rangle}\,p_{s}(x,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\phi(t,u)-\phi(s,\psi(t,u))-\langle x,\psi(s,\psi(t,u))\rangle}=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\phi(t+s,u)-\langle x,\psi(t+s,u)\rangle}.

The Laplace class therefore gives the Chapman–Kolmogorov identity

p_{t+s}(x,\cdot)=\int p_{t}(y,\cdot)\,p_{s}(x,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}y)
. Iterating this transition
kernel yields exactly the finite-dimensional Laplace transforms identified
above, so the canonical process under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
is Markov and has affine
transition transform
p_{t}
. Hence all weak limit points coincide, and tightness
implies that the whole sequence
(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d})
converges weakly to the single
Markov affine law
\mathbb{P}_{x}
on
D(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathcal{H}^{+})
. This proves the existence and
uniqueness of the affine process
(X,(\mathbb{P}_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}})
. The martingale
problem of Proposition
4.16
(on the restricted
class
\mathcal{D}_{0}
) is satisfied by every weak limit point; the law itself is
identified by the Markov affine finite-dimensional distributions just
constructed.

It remains to verify the trace-class assertion. Assume in addition
that
x\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
, that
m
and
\mu
are supported on

\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)\setminus\{0\}
, that
\Gamma(\mathcal{L}_{1}(H))\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
, and that

b,\mu(A)\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
for all
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})
, and that the
trace first-moment bounds stated in
Theorem
2.10
(a) hold. By
Lemma
4.17
, the coordinate map at each deterministic

t\geq 0
is
\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. continuous. Therefore the continuous-mapping theorem
gives
X_{t}^{d}\Rightarrow X_{t}
for every fixed
t\geq 0
. Moreover,
Lemma
4.13
gives

\displaystyle\sup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\Tr(X_{t}^{d})\right]<\infty,\qquad T>0.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\displaystyle h_{n}(y)\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle ye_{k},e_{k}\rangle_{H},\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}^{+}.

The map
h_{n}
is continuous and non-negative on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, so
Portmanteau’s theorem implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[h_{n}(X_{t})\right]\leq\liminf_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[h_{n}(X_{t}^{d})\right]\leq\liminf_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d}}\left[\Tr(X_{t}^{d})\right]<\infty,\qquad t\geq 0.

Letting
n\to\infty
and using monotone convergence yields

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[\Tr(X_{t})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\langle X_{t}e_{k},e_{k}\rangle_{H}\right]<\infty,\qquad t\geq 0.

Hence
X_{t}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(H)
almost surely for every
t\geq 0
, which proves the
claim.
∎

4.3.
Finite-Rank Approximation of Heat Modulated Affine Stochastic Covariance
Models

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
T>0
. Then

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big(|\Phi_{d}(t,u)-\Phi(t,u)|+\|\psi_{2,d}(t,u)-\psi_{2}(t,u)\|\Big)\longrightarrow 0,\qquad d\to\infty.

Proof.

Fix
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
T>0
. Since

\psi_{1}(\cdot,u)
solves the linear equation
(
3.2b
), it is continuous on
[0,T]
and therefore
bounded. The map
v\mapsto\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\psi_{1}(t,u),v)
is locally Lipschitz on

\mathcal{H}^{+}
, with Lipschitz constants independent of
t\in[0,T]
, because the
dependence on the first argument enters only through the explicit additive
term
-\frac{1}{2}\psi_{1}(t,u)^{\otimes 2}
. Arguing exactly as in the proof of
Proposition
2.8
, one therefore obtains a
constant
M_{T,u}>0
, independent of
d
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\bigl(\|\psi_{2}(t,u)\|\vee\|\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\|\bigr)\leq M_{T,u}.

Subtracting the mild equations for
\psi_{2}
and
\psi_{2,d}
, and
using that
\mathcal{T}(t)
commutes with
\mathbf{P}_{d}
, yields

\displaystyle\|\psi_{2}(t,u)-\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\|\leq a_{d}(t)+C_{T,u}\int_{0}^{t}\|\psi_{2}(s,u)-\psi_{2,d}(s,u)\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

where
C_{T,u}
is a Lipschitz constant on the ball of radius

M_{T,u}
and

\displaystyle a_{d}(t)\coloneqq\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t)u_{2}\|+\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\psi_{1}(s,u),\psi_{2}(s,u))\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Both
t\mapsto\mathcal{T}(t)u_{2}
and

(t,s)\mapsto\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\psi_{1}(s,u),\psi_{2}(s,u))
are continuous on the
compact sets
[0,T]
and
\Delta_{T}\coloneqq\{(t,s):0\leq s\leq t\leq T\}
respectively,
so their ranges are compact subsets
K_{1},K_{2}
of
\mathcal{H}
. Since

(\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp})_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
is a decreasing sequence of orthogonal projections
converging strongly to
0
on
\mathcal{H}
, the convergence

\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}z\to 0
is uniform on every compact subset of
\mathcal{H}
. Applied to

K_{1}
and
K_{2}
this yields

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t)u_{2}\|\to 0
and

\sup_{(t,s)\in\Delta_{T}}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\psi_{1}(s,u),\psi_{2}(s,u))\|\to 0
,
hence
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}a_{d}(t)\to 0
. Gronwall’s lemma therefore gives

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\psi_{2,d}(t,u)-\psi_{2}(t,u)\|\longrightarrow 0.

Finally, since

\Phi(t,u)=\int_{0}^{t}F(\psi_{2}(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s
and

\Phi_{d}(t,u)=\int_{0}^{t}F(\psi_{2,d}(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s
, continuity of
F
on bounded
subsets of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
yields

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\Phi_{d}(t,u)-\Phi(t,u)|\longrightarrow 0,

which proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 4.23

(Finite-rank Feynman–Kac martingale)
.

Let
(b,\mathbf{B},m,\mu)
be an admissible parameter set, fix
t>0
and

u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and let
X^{d}
be the finite-rank affine
process of Proposition
2.9
. With

(\Phi_{d}(\cdot,u),\psi_{1}(\cdot,u),\psi_{2,d}(\cdot,u))
the Galerkin joint
mild Riccati solution from Theorem
3.10
(i), define

M^{d}_{s}\coloneqq\exp\!\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\!\int_{0}^{s}\!\langle X^{d}_{r},\psi_{1}(t-r,u)^{\otimes 2}\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r\Big)\,\exp\!\big(-\Phi_{d}(t-s,u)-\langle X^{d}_{s},\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u)\rangle\big),\quad 0\leq s\leq t.

Then
(M^{d}_{s})_{0\leq s\leq t}
is a true martingale under
\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x}
, and there is
a constant
C_{t,u}>0
, independent of
d
, with
|M^{d}_{s}|\leq C_{t,u}
for all

s\in[0,t]
.

Proof.

On
\mathcal{H}_{d}
the process
X^{d}
is a finite-dimensional affine semimartingale with
bounded generator
\mathcal{G}^{d}
(Proposition
2.9
(ii) and
eq. (
2.22
)). The map

(s,y)\mapsto g_{d}(s,y)\coloneqq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\Phi_{d}(t-s,u)-\langle y,\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u)\rangle}

is
C^{1}
in
s
on
[0,t]
and exponential-affine in
y
: indeed

\Phi_{d}(\cdot,u)\in C^{1}
and
\psi_{2,d}(\cdot,u)\in C^{1}
, the latter solving the
finite-rank Riccati system
(
3.2a
)–(
3.2c
) with
L
replaced
by the bounded
\mathbf{P}_{d}L\mathbf{P}_{d}
. The potential

V(s,y)\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}\langle y,\psi_{1}(t-s,u)^{\otimes 2}\rangle
is continuous in
s
,
since
\psi_{1}(t-s,u)=S^{*}(t-s)u_{1}
is.

Write
M^{d}_{s}=E_{s}\,g_{d}(s,X^{d}_{s})
with the finite-variation factor

E_{s}\coloneqq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\int_{0}^{s}V(r,X^{d}_{r})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r}
, so
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}E_{s}=E_{s}\,V(s,X^{d}_{s})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s
. Since

g_{d}(s,\cdot)
is a scalar multiple of
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\cdot,\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u)\rangle}

with
\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u)\in\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}
, it lies in the domain of
\mathcal{G}^{d}
for every

s
. Applying the finite-dimensional Itô formula for jump semimartingales to
the
C^{1}
time-dependent exponential-affine map
(s,y)\mapsto g_{d}(s,y)
gives

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}g_{d}(s,X^{d}_{s})=(\partial_{s}g_{d}+\mathcal{G}^{d}g_{d})(s,X^{d}_{s})\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}N_{s}
, with
N
a
local martingale; here the operator
\mathcal{G}^{d}
is the finite-dimensional
integro-differential generator associated with the characteristics of
X^{d}
from
Proposition
2.9
. The product rule for the
finite-variation
E_{s}
then yields

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}M^{d}_{s}=E_{s}\Big[(\partial_{s}g_{d}+\mathcal{G}^{d}g_{d}+V(s,\cdot)g_{d})(s,X^{d}_{s})\Big]\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s+E_{s}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}N_{s}.

Using
\partial_{s}[-\Phi_{d}(t-s,u)]=F_{d}(\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u))
,

\partial_{s}[-\langle y,\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u)\rangle]=\langle y,\,R_{d}(\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u))-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{P}_{d}(\psi_{1}(t-s,u)^{\otimes 2})\rangle

(the Galerkin form of (
3.2c
) with the forcing

-\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{1}^{\otimes 2}
from (
3.1
)), and

\mathcal{G}^{d}g_{d}(s,y)=\big(-F_{d}(\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u))-\langle y,R_{d}(\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u))\rangle\big)g_{d}(s,y)
,
the drift coefficient collapses to

\partial_{s}g_{d}+\mathcal{G}^{d}g_{d}+Vg_{d}=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle y,\,\psi_{1}^{\otimes 2}-\mathbf{P}_{d}(\psi_{1}^{\otimes 2})\rangle\,g_{d}=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle y,\,\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}(\psi_{1}^{\otimes 2})\rangle\,g_{d}=0,\qquad y\in\mathcal{H}_{d},

because
\langle y,\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}(\psi_{1}^{\otimes 2})\rangle=\langle\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}y,\psi_{1}^{\otimes 2}\rangle=0
for
y\in\mathcal{H}_{d}
. Hence
M^{d}
is
a local martingale.

For the uniform bound, write
u_{1}=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}h
. Then

\psi_{1}(t-r,u)^{\otimes 2}=(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}S^{*}(t-r)h)^{\otimes 2}=-(S^{*}(t-r)h)^{\otimes 2}
, so
for
X^{d}_{r}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
,

\tfrac{1}{2}\langle X^{d}_{r},\psi_{1}(t-r,u)^{\otimes 2}\rangle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle X^{d}_{r},(S^{*}(t-r)h)^{\otimes 2}\rangle\leq 0
, whence

E_{s}\leq 1
. By Lemma
4.22
there is
M_{t,u}
,
independent of
d
, with
\sup_{\tau\leq t}\|\psi_{2,d}(\tau,u)\|\leq M_{t,u}
;
since
F
is continuous,
\sup_{\tau\leq t}|\Phi_{d}(\tau,u)|\leq C^{\prime}_{t,u}
uniformly
in
d
. As
\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u)\in\mathcal{H}^{+}_{d}
and
X^{d}_{s}\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
give

\langle X^{d}_{s},\psi_{2,d}(t-s,u)\rangle\geq 0
, we obtain

g_{d}(s,X^{d}_{s})\leq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{C^{\prime}_{t,u}}
and therefore

|M^{d}_{s}|\leq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{C^{\prime}_{t,u}}\coloneqq C_{t,u}
uniformly in
d
and
s\in[0,t]
. A bounded
local martingale is a true martingale.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Define the
d
-independent functional
\Lambda_{s}\colon D([0,t],\mathcal{H}^{+})\to\mathbb{R}
,

\Lambda_{s}(\omega)\coloneqq\exp\!\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\!\int_{0}^{s}\!\langle\omega(r),\psi_{1}(t-r,u)^{\otimes 2}\rangle\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r\Big)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\Phi(t-s,u)-\langle\omega(s),\psi_{2}(t-s,u)\rangle},

so that
M_{s}=\Lambda_{s}(X)
, while
M^{d}_{s}=\Lambda_{s}^{d}(X^{d})
with
\Lambda^{d}_{s}

the same functional carrying the Galerkin loadings
(\Phi_{d},\psi_{2,d})
. As in
Lemma
4.23
,
0\leq\Lambda_{s},\Lambda^{d}_{s}\leq C_{t,u}
.

Step 1 (loadings).
By Lemma
4.22
,

\sup_{\tau\leq t}\big(|\Phi_{d}(\tau,u)-\Phi(\tau,u)|+\|\psi_{2,d}(\tau,u)-\psi_{2}(\tau,u)\|\big)\to 0
. Hence, uniformly over paths in

\{\omega:\sup_{r\leq t}\|\omega(r)\|\leq R\}
,

\sup_{s\leq t}|\Lambda^{d}_{s}(\omega)-\Lambda_{s}(\omega)|\to 0
as
d\to\infty
.

Step 2 (continuity).
For fixed
s
, the evaluation

\omega\mapsto\omega(s)
is continuous in the Skorohod topology at every
\omega

continuous at
s
, and
\omega\mapsto\int_{0}^{s}\langle\omega(r),\cdot\rangle\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r
is
Skorohod-continuous. Thus
\Lambda_{s}
is bounded and continuous at every path
continuous at
s
. By Lemma
4.17
,

\mathbb{P}_{x}(\Delta X_{s}=0)=1
for every deterministic
s
, hence
\Lambda_{s}
is

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. continuous for every fixed
s
.

Step 3 (martingale property).
Fix deterministic times

0\leq s_{1}<\dots<s_{k}\leq s^{\prime}_{1}<s^{\prime}_{2}\leq t
and a bounded continuous

\varphi\colon(\mathcal{H}^{+})^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
. By Lemma
4.23
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x}}\left[(M^{d}_{s^{\prime}_{2}}-M^{d}_{s^{\prime}_{1}})\,\varphi(X^{d}_{s_{1}},\dots,X^{d}_{s_{k}})\right]=0

for every
d
. Writing

M^{d}_{s^{\prime}}=\Lambda_{s^{\prime}}(X^{d})+(\Lambda^{d}_{s^{\prime}}-\Lambda_{s^{\prime}})(X^{d})
, the error term
vanishes in the limit: by Step 1 and the uniform bound

\sup_{d}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x}}\left[\sup_{r\leq t}\|X^{d}_{r}\|\right]<\infty

(Lemma
4.12
), for any
\varepsilon>0
choose
R

with
\sup_{d}\mathbb{P}^{d}_{x}(\sup_{r}\|X^{d}_{r}\|>R)<\varepsilon
; on the complementary event

|\Lambda^{d}_{s^{\prime}}-\Lambda_{s^{\prime}}|\to 0
uniformly, while on the exceptional event the
integrand is bounded by
2C_{t,u}\|\varphi\|_{\infty}
. The remaining terms involve
only the
d
-independent, bounded,
\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. continuous functionals

\Lambda_{s^{\prime}_{1}},\Lambda_{s^{\prime}_{2}},\varphi
of the path; since
X^{d}\Rightarrow X

(Theorem
2.10
(2)) and Lemma
4.17

makes all fixed coordinate evaluations
\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. continuous, the
continuous-mapping theorem with the uniform bound
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[(M_{s^{\prime}_{2}}-M_{s^{\prime}_{1}})\,\varphi(X_{s_{1}},\dots,X_{s_{k}})\right]=0.

Cylinder functionals at deterministic times generate
\mathcal{F}_{s^{\prime}_{1}}
by a
monotone-class argument, and these coordinate maps are
\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. continuous
by Lemma
4.17
. Hence
M
is a martingale with respect to the

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-augmented natural filtration of
X
. Evaluating

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{x}}\left[M_{t}\right]=M_{0}
with
\psi_{2}(0,u)=u_{2}
and
X_{0}=x
yields
eq. (
4.36
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

Fix
(f_{0},x)\in H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
, let
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
,
and write
\sigma_{t}\coloneqq X_{t}^{1/2}
. By Assumption
Assumption

B

,
the stochastic convolution in (
3.7
) is well defined. Moreover,
the first Riccati equation (
3.2b
) is precisely the mild
dual shift equation, hence

(4.33)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(t,u)=S^{*}(t)u_{1},\qquad t\geq 0.

Conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(1)}=\sigma(X_{s}\colon 0\leq s\leq t)
, the random
variable
\langle f_{t},u_{1}\rangle_{H}
is Gaussian with mean

\langle f_{0},\psi_{1}(t,u)\rangle_{H}
and covariance determined by

\sigma_{s}^{*}S^{*}(t-s)u_{1}
. Therefore, using (
4.33
),

(4.34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\langle f_{t},u_{1}\rangle_{H}}\,\big|\,X\right]=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\langle f_{0},\psi_{1}(t,u)\rangle_{H}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\langle X_{s},\psi_{1}(t-s,u)^{\otimes 2}\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s}.

Hence

(4.35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\langle f_{t},u_{1}\rangle_{H}-\langle X_{t},u_{2}\rangle}\right]=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\langle f_{0},\psi_{1}(t,u)\rangle_{H}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t},u_{2}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\langle X_{s},\psi_{1}(t-s,u)^{\otimes 2}\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s}\right].

To identify the remaining expectation, we use
Proposition
3.4
. Evaluating the martingale
M_{s}
of that
proposition at
s=t
and
s=0
, and using
\psi_{2}(0,u)=u_{2}
and
X_{0}=x
,
gives

(4.36)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle X_{t},u_{2}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\langle X_{s},\psi_{1}(t-s,u)^{\otimes 2}\rangle\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s}\right]=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\Phi(t,u)-\langle x,\psi_{2}(t,u)\rangle}.

Combining (
4.35
) and
(
4.36
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\langle f_{t},u_{1}\rangle_{H}-\langle X_{t},u_{2}\rangle}\right]=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\Phi(t,u)+\langle f_{0},\psi_{1}(t,u)\rangle_{H}-\langle x,\psi_{2}(t,u)\rangle},\qquad t\geq 0,

which is exactly (
3.8
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

We begin with the proof of
3.10 i)
. Fix

d\in\mathbb{N}
and consider the finite-dimensional approximation

(f_{t}^{d},X_{t}^{d})_{t\geq 0}
, where
X^{d}
is as in
Proposition
2.9
and, in particular,

X_{0}^{d}=\mathbf{P}_{d}(x)
. By part (ii) of Proposition
2.9
,

X^{d}
is a square-integrable affine Markovian semimartingale on
\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
on
some stochastic basis
(\Omega^{d},\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{d},\bar{\mathbb{F}}^{d},\mathbb{P}_{x}^{d})
. Moreover, using
the semimartingale representation (
4.16
), we see that

(f^{d},X^{d})
solves the stochastic covariance system

(4.37)

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\begin{bmatrix}f^{d}_{t}\\
X^{d}_{t}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{A}f^{d}_{t}\\
\mathbf{\hat{B}}_{d}(X^{d}_{t})\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}g_{t}\\
\hat{b}_{d}\end{bmatrix}\right)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}t+\begin{bmatrix}(X^{d}_{t})^{1/2}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\begin{bmatrix}W_{t}\\
0\end{bmatrix}+\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\bar{J}^{d}_{t}\end{bmatrix},

with
(f^{d}_{0},X^{d}_{0})=(f_{0},\mathbf{P}_{d}(x))\in H\times\mathcal{H}^{+}
, drift

\hat{b}_{d}\coloneqq b_{d}+\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\cap\{\|\xi\|>1\}}\xi\,m_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi),

and linear drift operator

\mathbf{\hat{B}}_{d}(u)\coloneqq\mathbf{B}_{d}(\mathbf{P}_{d}u)+\int_{\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}\cap\{\|\xi\|>1\}}\xi\,\langle u,M_{d}(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)\rangle,\qquad u\in\mathcal{H}.

(Here
g_{t}
denotes the HJM drift of the forward curve component; throughout
this subsection we work with the driftless specification
g_{t}\equiv 0
.) Hence, the
finite-dimensional model is of the form in
[
23
, Definition 2.9]
, and

3.10 i)
follows from
[
23
, Theorem 3.3]
.
Since
X_{t}^{d}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
for every
t\geq 0
, the operator

(X_{t}^{d})^{1/2}
is finite-rank and therefore Hilbert–Schmidt on
H
.
Consequently the stochastic integral in the first component is well defined on
the
H
-level. In this cylindrical-noise formulation no additional background
covariance-operator commutativity condition is being imposed; the finite rank of

(X_{t}^{d})^{1/2}
is the relevant Hilbert–Schmidt condition. The finite-rank
affine transform can also be recovered from the Feynman–Kac identity in
Lemma
4.23
.
Let us verify that the hypotheses of
[
23
, Theorem 3.3]
are
met. That theorem requires: (i) the state space is a finite-dimensional convex
cone with non-empty interior (here
\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
, which is a closed convex cone in
the finite-dimensional space
\mathcal{H}_{d}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
with non-empty
relative interior consisting of strictly positive operators); (ii) the
parameters
(\hat{b}_{d},\mathbf{\hat{B}}_{d},m_{d},M_{d})
form an admissible set
in the sense of
[
23
, Definition 2.6]
, which holds because
\hat{b}_{d}\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{+}
,
the map
\mathbf{\hat{B}}_{d}
is a bounded linear operator on
\mathcal{H}_{d}
(since

\mathbf{B}_{d}=\mathbf{P}_{d}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{P}_{d}
is bounded in finite dimensions), and

m_{d}
,
M_{d}
satisfy the integrability conditions inherited from the
original admissible parameters
(b,B,m,\mu)
via the projections

\mathbf{P}_{d}
; (iii) there exists a unique square-integrable affine Markovian
semimartingale with these parameters, which is guaranteed by
Proposition
2.9
(ii). All three conditions are
therefore satisfied, and the affine transform
formula (
3.12
) follows.

We turn to
3.10 ii)
. Throughout this
proof we work in the finite-horizon canonical weighted forward-curve setting of
Example
2.5
:
\Theta_{\max}<\infty
,

V=V_{0,\beta}(0,\Theta_{\max})\oplus\mathbb{R}
,

H=L^{2}(0,\Theta_{\max},\mathrm{e}^{\beta x}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}x)\oplus\mathbb{R}
, and

B=\Delta\oplus 0
, with the corresponding finite-horizon adjoint shift
semigroup
S^{*}(s)
. Two structural facts of this canonical setting are used
below: the invariance
S^{*}(s)(V)\subseteq V
with the growth bound recalled at
Step 3, and the eigenvalue asymptotics
\lambda_{n}\sim(n\pi/\Theta_{\max})^{2}
used to control the Lyapunov semigroup
\mathcal{T}(t-s)
on

\mathcal{V}
. We do not claim the rate
in (
3.13
) for the abstract

V\hookrightarrow H\hookrightarrow V^{*}
framework outside this canonical
setting.
Assume from now on that the
limiting
H
-valued stochastic covariance model
(f,X)
from
Theorem
3.5
is well defined under
Assumptions
Assumption

C

and

Assumption

B

, as in the theorem statement. Assumption

Assumption

C

is used here to invoke the limiting affine
covariance process and Proposition
3.4
; the quantitative
estimate below uses the structural hypotheses displayed in item (ii). We now
establish the convergence rate in (
3.13
).
Fix
T>0
and
\tilde{u}\in V
, and set
u\coloneqq(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}\tilde{u},0)
.
Throughout the argument, constants may depend on
T
and
\|\tilde{u}\|_{V}
, but
are independent of
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

The argument is the inhomogeneous analogue of the convergence estimate
in Proposition
2.8
; compare also the regular-case
proof of
[
50
, Proposition 2.7]
. The only new feature is
the forcing term
-\frac{1}{2}(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{1})
in the equation for
\psi_{2}
,
which is explicit and therefore leaves the Gronwall structure unchanged.

Step 1: Uniform bounds for the Riccati solutions.

Let
M\geq 0
and consider the set

\{v\in\mathcal{H}^{+}:\|v\|\leq M\}
.
For fixed
u=(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}\tilde{u},0)
and
s\in[0,T]
, set

G_{s}(v)\coloneqq\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\psi_{1}(s,u),v)=\hat{R}(v)-\frac{1}{2}(\psi_{1}(s,u))^{\otimes 2},\qquad v\in\mathcal{H}^{+}.

The additive forcing is independent of
v
, hence the local
Lipschitz constant of
G_{s}
on this set is the local Lipschitz constant of

\hat{R}
, uniformly in
s\in[0,T]
and independently of
d
. The same
Lipschitz bound applies to the projected map

\mathbf{P}_{d}\circ G_{s}\circ\mathbf{P}_{d}
.

As a consequence, the mild solutions
\psi_{2}(\cdot,u)
and

\psi_{2,d}(\cdot,u)
of the second Riccati equation satisfy the uniform bound

\|\psi_{2}(t,u)\|\vee\|\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\|\leq\tilde{H}_{M},\qquad t\in[0,T],

for some constant
\tilde{H}_{M}>0
independent of
d
.
Note that, in contrast to Proposition
2.8
,
the equation for
\psi_{2}
contains the additional inhomogeneous term

-\frac{1}{2}(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{1})
, where
\psi_{1}(t,u)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}S^{*}(t)\tilde{u}
by Lemma
3.2
.
Consequently,
\tilde{H}_{M}
depends on
\tilde{u}
so the bound may differ from the bound used
earlier.

Step 2: Error representation via affine transform formulas.

Using the affine transform representations
(
3.8
) and
(
3.12
) for
(f,X)
and
(f^{d},X^{d})
,
respectively, we note that the right-hand sides are of the form

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{z(t)}
and
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{z_{d}(t)}
with

z(t)=-\Phi(t,u)+\langle f_{0},\psi_{1}(t,u)\rangle_{H}-\langle x,\psi_{2}(t,u)\rangle

and
z_{d}(t)
defined analogously; since
\psi_{1}(t,u)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}S^{*}(t)\tilde{u}\in\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}H

by Lemma
3.2
, the pairing

\langle f_{0},\psi_{1}(t,u)\rangle_{H}
is already purely imaginary, and no extra
factor of
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}
is needed. Since
\Phi(t,u)\geq 0
and
\langle x,\psi_{2}(t,u)\rangle\geq 0
(because
x\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
,
\psi_{2}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{+})
), and likewise
\Phi_{d}(t,u),\langle x,\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\rangle\geq 0
, the real parts of
z(t)
and
z_{d}(t)
are non-positive. The elementary bound

|\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{a+\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}b}-\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{a^{\prime}+\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}b^{\prime}}|\leq|a-a^{\prime}|+|b-b^{\prime}|
for
a,a^{\prime}\leq 0
and
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}

(which follows from
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{a}\leq 1
,
\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{a^{\prime}}\leq 1
and
|\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}b}-\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}b^{\prime}}|\leq|b-b^{\prime}|
) therefore yields, without any extra constant,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}\langle f_{t},\tilde{u}\rangle_{H}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}\langle f_{t}^{d},\tilde{u}\rangle_{H}}\right]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big(|\Phi(t,u)-\Phi_{d}(t,u)|

(4.38)

\displaystyle\quad+\|x\|\,\|\psi_{2}(t,u)-\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\|\Big).

Thus, it remains to control the difference

\psi_{2}-\psi_{2,d}
uniformly on
[0,T]
; the corresponding scalar estimate for

\Phi-\Phi_{d}
then follows from local Lipschitz continuity of
F
.

Step 3: Stability estimate for
\psi_{2}-\psi_{2,d}
.

Using the mild formulations of
\psi_{2}
and
\psi_{2,d}
, the
commutation relation
\mathbf{P}_{d}\mathcal{T}(t)=\mathcal{T}(t)\mathbf{P}_{d}
, and the fact that both equations
start from
u_{2}=0
and share the same projected explicit forcing

-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{P}_{d}((\psi_{1})^{\otimes 2})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\psi_{2}(t,u)-\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|G_{s}(\psi_{2}(s,u))-G_{s}(\psi_{2,d}(s,u))\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{L}_{M}^{(1)}\int_{0}^{t}\|\psi_{2}(s,u)-\psi_{2,d}(s,u)\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

where
\tilde{L}_{M}^{(1)}
is a Lipschitz constant of
\hat{R}
, and hence
of
G_{s}
, on
\{v\in\mathcal{H}^{+}:\|v\|\leq\tilde{H}_{M}\}
. Therefore, by the triangle
inequality,

\displaystyle\|\psi_{2}(t,u)-\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\psi_{2}(t,u)\|+\|\mathbf{P}_{d}\psi_{2}(t,u)-\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\psi_{2}(t,u)\|+\tilde{L}_{M}^{(1)}\int_{0}^{t}\|\psi_{2}(s,u)-\psi_{2,d}(s,u)\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

Step 4: Control of the projection error.

Denote the integrand appearing in (
2.13
) by

K(\xi,u)\coloneqq\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\xi,u\rangle}-1+\langle\chi(\xi),u\rangle,

so that
\hat{R}
admits the representation

\hat{R}(v)=\Gamma^{*}(v)-\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}K(\xi,v)\,\frac{\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)}{\|\xi\|^{2}}.

Jump-integrand bound (small/large jump separation).
For

v\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
with
\|v\|\leq M
, the integrand

K(\xi,v)/\|\xi\|^{2}
admits the bound
|K(\xi,v)|/\|\xi\|^{2}\leq C_{M}

uniformly in
\xi\in\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}
, where
C_{M}
depends only on
M
. On the
small-jump set
\{\|\xi\|\leq 1\}
, a second-order Taylor expansion of

\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\xi,v\rangle}
around
0
gives
|K(\xi,v)|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\xi,v\rangle^{2}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}M^{2}\|\xi\|^{2}
, so

|K(\xi,v)|/\|\xi\|^{2}\leq M^{2}/2
. On the large-jump set

\{\|\xi\|>1\}
, the truncation
\chi(\xi)=0
and the bound

|\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\langle\xi,v\rangle}-1|\leq 2
give

|K(\xi,v)|/\|\xi\|^{2}\leq 2/\|\xi\|^{2}\leq 2
. Combining the two,

|K(\xi,v)|/\|\xi\|^{2}\leq\max(M^{2}/2,2)\coloneqq C_{M}
uniformly in
\xi
.

Since
u_{2}=0
, the
\mathcal{T}(t)u_{2}
-term in the mild variation-of-constants formula
vanishes, the unbounded generator contribution is absorbed into the semigroup

\mathcal{T}(t-s)
, and we obtain

\psi_{2}(t,u)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\!\left(\Gamma^{*}(\psi_{2}(s,u))-\!\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\!\!K(\xi,\psi_{2}(s,u))\,\frac{\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)}{\|\xi\|^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}(\psi_{1}(s,u))^{\otimes 2}\right)\!\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s.

For the jump term, the strengthened hypothesis on
\mu
is used exactly here.
If

J(v)\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}K(\xi,v)\,\frac{\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)}{\|\xi\|^{2}},\qquad\|v\|\leq M,

then the Bartle total-variation estimate for
\mathcal{V}
-valued vector measures gives

\|J(v)\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|K(\xi,v)|}{\|\xi\|^{2}}\,|\mu|_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})\leq C_{M}\,|\mu|_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\}).

Thus the proof uses finite
\mathcal{V}
-total variation of
\mu
, not merely the

\mathcal{V}
-norm of its total mass.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\psi_{2}(t,u)\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\Gamma^{*}(\psi_{2}(s,u))\|\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\tilde{H}_{M}}\,|\mu|_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s

(4.39)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{t}{2}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t-s)(S^{*}(s)\tilde{u})^{\otimes 2}\|.

Here the three contributions correspond to the three forcing terms in
the mild formula: the drift-type term
\Gamma^{*}(\psi_{2})
, the compensated
jump-integral term
K(\xi,\psi_{2})\mu(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}\xi)/\|\xi\|^{2}
(controlled by
the total-variation bound just established), and the inhomogeneous forcing

-\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{1}^{\otimes 2}
. For the last, we have kept

\psi_{1}(s,u)=\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}S^{*}(s)\tilde{u}
from
Lemma
3.2
, and used the identity

(\mathop{}\!\textnormal{i}v)^{\otimes 2}=-v^{\otimes 2}
which only contributes a sign, absorbed
into the modulus on the right-hand side.
For the drift-type term, we use that
\mathcal{T}(r)
maps
\mathcal{H}
into

\mathcal{V}
for every
r>0
: using the
B
-eigenbasis expansion established below,
for
x=\sum_{i,j}x_{i,j}\mathbf{e}_{i,j}\in\mathcal{H}
we have

\|\mathcal{T}(r)x\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\leq\sum_{i,j}(\lambda_{i,j}+2\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{i,j}r}|x_{i,j}|^{2}\leq\kappa(r)^{2}\|x\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
, where

\kappa(r)\coloneqq\sup_{i,j}\sqrt{\lambda_{i,j}+2\lambda}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{i,j}r}<\infty

for every
r>0
and is integrable on
[0,T]
(by Weyl asymptotics

\lambda_{n}\simeq cn^{2}
, cf. Remark
2.6
(ii)).
Since
\Gamma^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and
\|\psi_{2}(s,u)\|\leq\tilde{H}_{M}
by
Step 1, we obtain

\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t-s)\Gamma^{*}(\psi_{2}(s,u))\|\leq\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}\,\kappa(t-s)\,\|\Gamma^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}\tilde{H}_{M}
, and the
s
-integral is finite.
Since
\tilde{u}\in V
and
S^{*}(s)(V)\subseteq V
with the growth
bound
\|S^{*}(s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(V)}\leq M_{1}\mathrm{e}^{ws}

(by Lemma
3.1
, with
M_{1}=1
and
w=0
, in the
finite-horizon canonical specialization

V=V_{0,\beta}(0,\Theta_{\max})\oplus\mathbb{R}
of
Example
2.5
), we have
(S^{*}(s)\tilde{u})^{\otimes 2}\in\mathcal{V}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{T}(t-s)
maps
\mathcal{V}
into
\mathcal{V}
: by the spectral Hilbert-scale
compatibility in Definition
2.2
(iv)(c), the
eigenbasis
(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
B
lies in
V
, is dense in
V
, and satisfies

\|v\|_{V}^{2}\simeq\sum_{n}(1+\lambda_{n})|\langle v,e_{n}\rangle_{H}|^{2}
. Consequently,
if
u=\sum_{n}u_{n}e_{n}\in V
then

\sum_{n}(1+\lambda_{n})|u_{n}|^{2}<\infty
and

T(t)u=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-\lambda_{n}t}u_{n}e_{n}

still lies in
V
, because

\sum_{n}(1+\lambda_{n})\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{-2\lambda_{n}t}|u_{n}|^{2}\leq\sum_{n}(1+\lambda_{n})|u_{n}|^{2}
;
by norm equivalence this yields

\|T(t)u\|_{V}\leq C\|u\|_{V}
, hence
T(t)(V)\subseteq V
uniformly in
t\geq 0
.

For the dual component, by self-adjointness
T^{*}(t)=T(t)
and duality,

\|T(t)\eta\|_{V^{*}}=\sup_{\|v\|_{V}\leq 1}|\langle T(t)\eta,v\rangle_{V^{*},V}|=\sup_{\|v\|_{V}\leq 1}|\langle\eta,T(t)v\rangle_{V^{*},V}|\leq C\,\|\eta\|_{V^{*}},

for
\eta\in V^{*}
. Thus

T(t)(V^{*})\subseteq V^{*}
uniformly in
t\geq 0
as well.
Consequently, for
x\in\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{L}_{2}(V^{*},H)\cap\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)
both components
are preserved:
T(t)xT^{*}(t)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(H,V)
(using
T(t)V\subseteq V
on
the range) and
T(t)xT^{*}(t)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(V^{*},H)
(using
T^{*}(t)V^{*}\subseteq V^{*}

on the input). Hence
\mathcal{T}(t-s)(\mathcal{V})\subseteq\mathcal{V}
uniformly in
t-s\geq 0
.
Set

M_{T,\mathcal{V}}\coloneqq\sup_{0\leq r\leq T}\|\mathcal{T}(r)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V})}<\infty.

The jump contribution in (
4.39
) is therefore
bounded by

T\,C_{\tilde{H}_{M}}\,M_{T,\mathcal{V}}\,|\mu|_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})\,\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{T}(t-s)(S^{*}(s)\tilde{u})^{\otimes 2}\in\mathcal{V}
.
Using
\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}\to 0
and the growth bound

\|S^{*}(s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(V)}\leq M_{1}\operatorname{e}^{ws}
, we obtain

\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\mathcal{T}(t-s)(S^{*}(s)\tilde{u})^{\otimes 2}\|\leq\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}M_{T}M_{1}^{2}\operatorname{e}^{2tw}\|\tilde{u}\|_{V}^{2}.

Step 5: Gronwall argument and rate.

Define the error function

e_{d}(t)\coloneqq\sup_{r\in[0,t]}\|\psi_{2}(r,u)-\psi_{2,d}(r,u)\|
. Combining the
bound on
\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\psi_{2}(t,u)\|
from Step 4 (each of the three terms
on the right-hand side of (
4.39
) is bounded by a

d
-uniform constant times
\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}
) with the
Lipschitz stability bound from Step 3, we obtain

e_{d}(t)\leq A_{d}+\tilde{L}_{M}^{(1)}\int_{0}^{t}e_{d}(s)\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

where the inhomogeneous term collects the three projection
contributions:

A_{d}\coloneqq\big(T\,\kappa_{\Gamma}\,\|\Gamma^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}\,\tilde{H}_{M}+T\,C_{\tilde{H}_{M}}\,M_{T,\mathcal{V}}\,|\mu|_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}^{+}\setminus\{0\})+\tfrac{T}{2}\,M_{T}M_{1}^{2}\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{2Tw}\|\tilde{u}\|_{V}^{2}\big)\,\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})},

with
\kappa_{\Gamma}=\int_{0}^{T}\kappa(r)\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}r<\infty
and
\tilde{L}_{M}^{(1)}

the local Lipschitz constant of
\hat{R}
on
\{\|v\|\leq\tilde{H}_{M}\}
.
Gronwall’s inequality therefore gives

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\psi_{2}(t,u)-\psi_{2,d}(t,u)\|\leq A_{T}\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{e}^{\tilde{L}_{M}^{(1)}T}\coloneqq\tilde{C}_{T}\,\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})},

with
\tilde{C}_{T}
independent of
d
.
The scalar Riccati component has the same rate. Let
L_{F}
be a Lipschitz
constant of
F
on the ball containing

\{\psi_{2}(s,u),\psi_{2,d}(s,u):0\leq s\leq T,\ d\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Since

\Phi(t,u)=\int_{0}^{t}F(\psi_{2}(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,\qquad\Phi_{d}(t,u)=\int_{0}^{t}F(\psi_{2,d}(s,u))\,\mathop{}\!\textnormal{d}s,

we obtain

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\Phi(t,u)-\Phi_{d}(t,u)|\leq L_{F}T\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\|\psi_{2}(s,u)-\psi_{2,d}(s,u)\|\leq C_{T}^{\Phi}\,\|\mathbf{P}_{d}^{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})}

for a constant
C_{T}^{\Phi}
independent of
d
.
Inserting the two bounds into (
4.38
) and
increasing
\tilde{C}_{T}
if necessary proves
(
3.13
), with the final

(1+\|x\|)
factor from (
4.38
) understood
in the
\mathcal{H}
norm (i.e.
\|x\|=\|x\|_{\mathcal{H}}
, consistent with the
statement of Theorem
3.10
(ii)).

∎
